# How the unimodality of the maximum-displacement counts of parking functions was proved: a history of the research

## 1. What this document is

Between 5 November and 23 November 2026 two runs of an autonomous research system, in which AI sessions do the mathematics, worked on one statement, a conjecture of Celano and co-authors about parking functions. The first run reached a proof that its own reviewers accepted but that rested on large certified computations and was never formalized. The second run, started six days after the first had stopped, reached a different proof, shorter and with far less computation, and had it checked in the Lean proof assistant. This document tells how that happened: what was tried and in what order, what worked, what failed and why, how the structure of the final proof emerged, and how the work passed from the first run to the second. What neither run answered is the question that the user put to the first on its fifth day and that shaped the second: why every row must be unimodal (sections 4.7, 7.1 and 7.4).

The account was assembled from the primary record of the two runs. That record consists of their version-control histories, in which every publication is a dated commit; the published records with their reviews; the texts submitted to the running research by the user, the person who commissioned it, and by the operator, an AI session that started and tended it on the user's behalf; the logs of the programs that drove the sessions; and, for the days between the two runs, the working files of the sessions that wrote the intermediate documents. Twenty-four assistants, called *source readers* below, each read one part of this material and reported on it. The account was written from their reports, checked against the sources by others who had not written it, read through by readers who had nothing but the text, and extended in its mathematical passages from the records themselves. Section 9 says what these checks covered and what they could not cover.

A few conventions hold throughout. A statement taken from a source carries a coordinate, a reference to that source. The coordinates are many. In the running text they stand in notes at the end of the document, and a numbered mark after a statement leads to its note; a reader who wants only the course of events need not follow the marks. The head of the notes explains the forms of the coordinates and says where the sources are published. A statement that is an inference of this account, and not something a source says, is marked as an inference. Where the sources do not say something that the account needs, the account says that it was not found and does not supply a guess. Times are Korean Standard Time (UTC+9) on the 24-hour clock, and all dates are in 2026.

The mathematical passages tell the mathematics directly: they define the objects, state what is proved and give the argument. Each such stretch says once, where the reader meets it, what standing its statements have, for example that a review inside the run accepted the proof in words, or that such a review reports having compiled a Lean proof. "Holds" in such a passage means what that sentence says and no more, since no one in the making of this account verified a proof (section 9). The words for the standings are defined in section 2.2. Which record states a step, a record's own name or letter for something, and how this account came to a statement are said in the notes.

The account is arranged as follows. Section 2 states the conjecture, says where it comes from and what was known about it, explains the research system, whose words are used throughout, and sets the two runs side by side. Section 3 gives the whole course of the research in brief. Sections 4 to 6 then tell it in order and in detail: the first run, the days between the two runs, and the second run. Section 7 looks across the two runs, at the user's interventions, at the roles of computation and of Lean, at what was carried from the first run to the second, and at how the two were run. Section 8 lists the dated milestones in a table, and section 9 is the report on the checks.

## 2. The statement, and the vocabulary of the system

### 2.1 The statement

Fix an integer $n \ge 1$. There are $n$ parking spots in a row, numbered 1 to $n$, and $n$ cars that arrive one after another. Car $i$ has a preferred spot $a_i$ in $\lbrace 1, \ldots, n \rbrace$. It drives to $a_i$; if that spot is free it parks there, and otherwise it takes the first free spot after $a_i$. If every spot from $a_i$ onward is taken, the car fails to park. The sequence $(a_1, \ldots, a_n)$ is a *parking function* if every car parks. There are $(n+1)^{n-1}$ parking functions of length $n$; this number is written $p_n$ below.

When car $i$ parks in spot $s_i$, its *displacement* is $s_i - a_i$, the number of spots it had to drive past its preference. The *maximum displacement* $M$ of a parking function is the largest displacement of any of its cars; it lies between 0 and $n-1$. The maximum displacement depends on the order in which the cars arrive, so it is not determined by the multiset of preferences. For $0 \le \ell \le n-1$ let

  $b_{n,\ell} =$ the number of parking functions of length $n$ with $M = \ell$,

  $A_{n,\ell} =$ the number with $M \le \ell$, so that $b_{n,\ell} = A_{n,\ell} - A_{n,\ell-1}$.

The sequence $b_{n,0}, b_{n,1}, \ldots, b_{n,n-1}$ will be called the *row* of $n$. For $n = 7$ the row is 5040, 42253, 62145, 57398, 46142, 32359, 16807. Its two ends are known in general: $b_{n,0} = n!$, and $b_{n,n-1} = n^{n-2}$ for $n \ge 2$.

A finite sequence is *unimodal* if it rises (weakly) to some position and falls (weakly) from there on. The statement under research is:

> **For every $n \ge 1$ the row $b_{n,0}, \ldots, b_{n,n-1}$ is unimodal.**

It is Conjecture 6.5 of Celano et al., "Statistics on ℓ-interval parking functions", arXiv:2507.07243. Both runs call this statement the *root*.

Almost everything in this account is phrased through the *step*

$$d_{n,k} = b_{n,k} - b_{n,k-1}, \qquad 1 \le k \le n-1,$$

the difference of two neighbouring entries of a row. A row is unimodal exactly when there is no pair of positions $i \lt j$ with $d_{n,i} \lt 0 \lt d_{n,j}$, that is, no decrease followed later by an increase.

It is useful to picture the steps $d_{n,k}$ arranged in the plane, with the *row* $n$ (the length) running one way and the *column* $k$ (the level of displacement) running the other. The counts are arranged in the same plane: the *entries* of column $k$ are the numbers $b_{n,k}$, its *steps* are the numbers $d_{n,k}$, and where a column is called positive or negative, or is said to turn positive, its steps are meant. Unimodality of every row says that in each row the signs of the steps are first non-negative and then non-positive. Much of the later history consists of statements about how the sign of $d_{n,k}$ behaves when one moves along a column ($k$ fixed, $n$ growing) or along a diagonal ($n$ and $k$ growing together).

Two numbers measure where a position $(n,k)$ lies in its row: the level $k$ itself, counted from the left end, and the *deficit* $r = n - k$, counted from the right end. The last position of a row, $k = n - 1$, thus has deficit 1. A column of steps is counted from the row $n = k$: there $b_{k,k}$ is read as 0, so that the step of row $k$ is $-b_{k,k-1}$. A column therefore begins at deficit 0, as section 4.3 says where the convention is first used. "The level" below always means the $k$ of the step under discussion, and "row" means the sequence $b_{n, \cdot}$ or, where steps are concerned, its index $n$. The peak of a row lies near the right end, at a deficit of order $n^{2/3}$; this is shown by local limit theorems of the research itself, proved on its first day, 5 November (section 4.2).

Both runs divide a row near its middle into a *left half* and a *right half*, and they do not draw the line at quite the same place. For the first run a position $(n,k)$ lies in the right half when $k \ge (n+1)/2$, that is, in the rows $n \le 2k - 1$ of column $k$ (section 4.6). For the second run it lies in the right half when $n \le 2k$, and in the left half when $n \ge 2k + 1$ (section 6). The two lines differ in one row of each column, the row $2k$, whose step $d_{2k,k}$ stands in the middle of an even row: the second run counts it to the right half, and the first had a statement of its own for the step at the centre of a row (section 4.5).

The statement was posed as a conjecture by Celano, Elder, Hadaway, Harris, Martin, Priestley and Udell, in the paper named above. In its Definition 2.7 that paper calls a parking function an $\ell$-*interval parking function* when its maximum displacement is at most $\ell$. In its terms, then, $A_{n,\ell}$ is the number of $\ell$-interval parking functions of length $n$, and $b_{n,\ell}$ is the number of those that are $\ell$-interval and not $(\ell-1)$-interval. Its Table 1 gives the numbers $b_{n,\ell}$ for $n \le 9$, and its Example 2.8 and Section 6 give the two ends of each row, the values $n!$ and $n^{n-2}$ stated above. In Section 6, in the sentence before the conjecture, the paper states for all $n \ge 1$, as consequences of its enumerative results, the inequalities $b_{n,0} \le b_{n,1} \le b_{n,2}$ and $b_{n,n-2} \ge b_{n,n-1}$, and it then adds that experimentally the pattern seems to continue. Two of these inequalities fail for the smallest $n$, as the research remarked from its own computed rows: $b_{n,0} \le b_{n,1}$ fails for $n = 2$, and $b_{n,1} \le b_{n,2}$ fails for $n = 3$, 4 and 5.[^1]

The works that the research read contain, among other things, two exact descriptions of the cumulative counts $A_{n,\ell}$ and a limit theorem for the maximum displacement. One description is a sum over the outcomes of the parking process, an outcome being the record of which car stands in which spot: each outcome contributes a product of $n$ factors, one for each spot. It is in Section 4.1 of the paper of Celano and co-authors, in its equations 6 to 8. The other description is a recursion in $n$, obtained by fixing the spot of the car that arrives last, and it is Theorem 3.7 of Aguilar-Fraga et al., arXiv:2311.14055. Section 4.2 gives both formulas and tells how the first sessions of the research came to them. The limit theorem is a theorem of Petersson on hashing with linear probing. It concerns a table with $m$ cells and $m - 1$ items, conditioned on its last cell remaining empty, and says that the difference between $m$ and the maximum displacement of the items, divided by $m^{2/3}$, converges in distribution to a limit whose distribution function the theorem gives (N. Petersson, The maximum displacement for linear probing hashing, Combin. Probab. Comput. 22, Theorem 5.1). For $m = n + 1$ such a table is a uniformly random parking function of length $n$; this identification is the research's reading of the model and is not a quotation from the article.[^2] Under it the theorem says that all but an arbitrarily small fraction of the sum of the row of $n$ lies at a distance of order $n^{2/3}$ from the right end of the row, which is the scale of the peak named above.[^3] In the research's assessment the theorem is a statement of convergence in distribution, however, and not a local limit theorem: it compares no two neighbouring entries of a row.[^4]

The paper that was written between the two runs (section 5.3) reports what was looked for in the literature and not found. Apart from the paper that states the conjecture, it found no article or preprint with a proof of the conjecture, or with a result on the monotonicity of the rows, on the position of their largest entry, or on a local form of the limit law. For this the other sources that it names at its Theorem 5.3 were read in full and contain no such result, and on 19 November a search was made: six queries on arXiv, the lists of the works that cite the three works named above, and keyword queries in zbMATH, OpenAlex and Crossref. About 210 records were examined, by their titles and, for the newest, by their abstracts. Two keyword queries could not be run, and MathSciNet, Web of Science, Scopus and Google Scholar were not searched. The paper adds the caution that a search of this kind shows where the statements were looked for and does not show that they are new.[^5] One of the notes on the literature in the records of the first run, written on the first day, limits itself in the same way: it says that its limited search found neither a complete proof nor a refutation of the conjecture, and that this is not a finding, from a survey of all public literature, that the conjecture is unsolved.[^6]

### 2.2 The research system, and how the sources are cited

The system is called do-math. One run of it on one statement is an *instance*: a directory with its own Git repository. The two runs of this account are two instances, and from here on they are called the earlier instance and the later instance.

Inside an instance, many AI *sessions* work at the same time and one after another, each in one of several roles. An *attempt* session takes a statement and tries to prove it. An *explore* session, or exploration, investigates freely, without a statement to prove. A *formalizer* session writes a Lean proof of something that has been proved informally. These are the *producer* sessions.

Whatever a producer session leaves behind is given to a *peer* session, which checks it, repairs and extends it, and writes it up as a candidate record. By the attributions that the records themselves make, the peer often contributes as much mathematics as the producer, and in places the decisive step; sections 4.6, 4.8 and 6.9 have examples. A *review* session then judges the candidate. If the review decides to publish, the candidate becomes a *record*, and its publication is one commit of the instance's repository. The sequence of producer, peer and review for one piece of work is a *pipeline*.

*Organizer* sessions maintain an index of the published records, and a program called the *runner* launches the sessions, up to a set number at once.

A record consists of a mathematical exposition, a list of *claims* (its separate assertions), and the review's verdict on each claim: *supported* or not. A record can also propose changes to the instance's map of the problem, which is called the *frontier*.

The frontier consists of *nodes* and *relations*. A node is a precisely stated proposition; the root is one. A relation is an implication between nodes that a review has accepted, for example "these two nodes together imply the root". The frontier also carries *questions*, which are open-ended and are either open or resolved.

A record that claims to have proved a node proposes a *solution*. The review gives the solution a level, *informal* (a proof in words that the review accepts) or *formal* (a Lean proof that the review has compiled itself and compared with the node's statement), or refuses it. A node is accordingly in one of the states *unresolved*, *informal_proved* or *formal_proved*. The state propagates along relations: when all the premises of an accepted relation are proved, its conclusion counts as informally proved. A node that is proved in this way is said below to be proved *by relation*, or to stand by relation: the words say that its state comes from other nodes through accepted relations. They do not say that the node has no solution of its own: a node that stands by relation can also receive one, and a node that has both is told with both. A node is formally proved only through a Lean proof of its own statement.

These verdicts give the words in which this account says what standing a statement has. Each word says what a review did with a claim, with a solution or with a relation, except *proposed*, which says that no review judged the statement. Where it matters which of the three a review judged, the text names it. A statement is:

- *formally proved* when there is a Lean proof of it that a review of the instance reports having compiled. For a node this is a solution at the level formal: a review can accept a solution at that level too, and the text then says *formally proved*.
- *accepted* when a review of the instance accepted its proof in words, as a claim that the review supported, as a solution at the level informal, or as a relation. The word claims nothing more, and it says nothing of a formal proof.
- *accepted, by relation* when it is a node that is proved by relation, in the sense of the preceding paragraph. The two forms say the same thing.
- *certified by computation* when it rests on a computation, which is then named with its range and the kind of its arithmetic. Section 4.2 says what this account asks of a computation before it calls it certified, which includes that its review accepted it.
- *accepted and afterwards set back* when it was accepted and a later ruling returned it to open.
- *refused* when a review did not accept it.
- *proposed* when a session stated or sketched it, with a proof or without one, and no review judged it. This includes a statement that was stated and then not pursued. The session need not belong to an instance: the word also covers what is proved only in the text of a document that was written outside the instances.

Three further words serve for statements that have none of these standings. A statement is:

- *observed in computed rows* when it was seen in a computation that no record gives as part of a proof.
- *refuted* when a proof or an exact computation shows it to be false.
- *heuristic* when the record that states it or takes it up, or the review of that record, marks it so.

Three cases need a word more than the list gives. Where a statement that was accepted was later formally proved, by the same proof put into Lean or by another, the text tells both, each at its time. A review can support the claims of a proof and still refuse the proof as a solution; then both are said. And the system has a verb of its own, by which a record proposes a solution or a relation that a review then accepts or refuses: such a solution or relation is told with the review's verdict, and *proposed* is said only of what no review judged.

One more term of the system is needed for the earlier instance. An attempt session does not have to take a whole node. It can register a *section* of a node, a part of the node's statement that its approach addresses, and other attempts can then register other parts. Sections are bookkeeping for the division of labour; they are not part of the frontier.

Two kinds of outside party act on an instance. The *user* is the person who commissioned the research. The *operator* is an AI session, outside the instance, that starts and tends it on the user's behalf; different sessions held this role at different times. Either can submit a *standing input*: a text that every session started afterwards receives along with its task. Standing inputs are the channel through which the user's judgment reached the sessions, and section 7.1 lists them all.

The standing inputs of the later instance are referred to by their order of submission, as that instance's records refer to them. The first is the handoff, the document with which the instance started (section 5.2); what each of the others carries, and which texts are not counted among them, is told beside the table of section 7.1. The records call these texts standing conditions; this account says input, and "the fifth condition" in a quotation is the fifth input.

The coordinates by which this account cites its sources, in the notes and in the cells of the tables, are explained at the head of the notes, at the end of the document. In them "seq" with a number counts the publications of an instance in their order, and "M-" with a number names a note in the working memory of the repository in which the research system itself is developed. One such note is kept with each commit of that repository, and this account calls them memory notes. The head of the notes also says where the sources are published.

The Lean names and the counts of computed facts that this account gives are those of the records of the two instances, which their archives hold, and not all of them are found in that library.

## 3. The course of the research in brief

The research falls into three parts: the earlier instance, the days between the two instances, and the later instance. This section tells each in outline, and the table at its end gives the same course by periods, with the sections that tell each period in detail.

**The earlier instance (5 to 13 November)** started from the statement alone and reached, in six days, a proof that its reviews accepted. Its shape is that of a problem taken apart by regions. Each row is cut at its centre. The right half is closed by an induction on $n$ along the chain of largest trees, a sequence of nested subtrees in a picture of parking functions as forests (section 4.2), and the induction is started from a certified computation. The left half is closed column by column, and within a column by ranges of rows, down to the comparison of two neighbouring entries, $b_{n,k-1} \le b_{n,k}$, in a range of rows called the second layer (section 4.8). Every row with $n \le 20000$ is covered by computation, and many of the inequalities above that range are themselves verified by machine.

The proof was never formalized, because the user paused formalization on the fifth day; and by the judgment of its own reviews it does not say why a row is unimodal. On the fifth day the user told the instance that this "why" was the thing most wanted, and the instance spent its remaining days partly on that question without answering it.

**Between the instances (12 to 20 November)** the earlier proof was written up as a manuscript. A night's analysis of that manuscript, on 19 November, made by a session that the user opened with the manuscript and by the sessions that this session sent out, produced a structure document and five reports which reduced the conjecture to statements about how the sign of the step $d_{n,k}$ moves along columns and diagonals, and named the places where the analysis was stuck. (The earlier instance had proved a reduction of the same kind, and had not pursued it.) A handoff document summed these up. Separately, a paper was written that proves three theorems on the conjecture and reduces it to one open inequality: agents that the operator dispatched built a first document from documents that the user had, and the user had it rewritten as a research article. The later instance was started from the handoff and received the paper on its second day, so that the two lines of documents, that of the handoff and that of the paper, reach into its first two days (section 5.4). It did not receive the earlier instance's records.

**The later instance (19 to 23 November)** worked under rules that the earlier one had not had: computation only for small cases, nothing handed over to be taken as proved, and success defined as a short explanatory proof with a Lean formalization. Within two days it had informal proofs of the right half. These were then set back, repeatedly: their acceptance was withdrawn under the user's requirement that a proof which can still be made shorter is not yet accepted. Under that requirement the proofs shrank and their computations shrank, and the structure of the final proof settled.

In that structure the unimodality of every row follows from three statements about columns. A column is said here to turn positive at the first row in which its step is positive. The first statement, (L), says that every column $k \ge 6$ is non-negative from row $2k + 1$ on. The second, (C), says that a column $k$ which has turned positive in one of its rows up to $2k - 1$ stays positive in the rows after that one, as far as row $2k - 1$. The third, (Mono), says that a column cannot turn positive before the column to its left has. The three statements are *formally proved*, and so is the root, by the argument that the next two paragraphs give in outline and section 6.9 in full.[^419]

A row could fail to be unimodal only by a negative step that is followed, later in the same row, by a positive one, and the proof asks where the negative step would lie. In a column $k \ge 6$ it cannot lie in a row from $2k + 1$ on, by (L). If it lies in a row up to $2k - 1$, then by (C) its column has not turned positive in any row up to that one; by (Mono) the next column has then not turned positive either, nor, repeating the argument, has any column further to the right, so that no later step of the row is positive. The one row between, $2k$, is the row of the *middle step* $d_{2k,k}$, the step of column $k$ in the middle of the even row $2k$. From column 8 on the middle step is non-negative, so that no negative step lies there: the proof of (L) gives this as well from column 9 on, and a few comparisons of neighbouring entries that the Lean kernel computes cover what is left. They settle column 8, and in the columns 2 to 7, where the middle step is negative, they show that the next step of the row is not positive, and the argument through (C) and (Mono) does the rest (section 6.9). That the middle step is negative in the columns 1 to 7 is known by exact computation, and the proof of the root does not use it.[^420]

The columns 1 to 5, which (L) leaves out, are closed separately in the rows from $2k + 1$ on. Column 1 is closed by an induction. In each of the columns 2 to 5 the step is negative at one exceptional position; that it is negative there is shown by exact values and is no Lean theorem. Outside that position Lean proves that the step of the column is non-negative from row $2k + 1$ on, from finitely many computed facts about the lower rows of the column, which a theorem carries to every later row (the critical-tilt propagation of section 5.2); and at the position itself the Lean kernel checks directly that no step to its right in the same row is positive (section 6.9).[^421]

The first Lean proof came on the third day, the informal proof of the root on the evening of the fourth, and the formal proof on the morning of the fifth, 23 November at 08:10. The formal proof rests on the definition of the parking process, one recurrence, and 6,118 small facts that the Lean kernel computes, all in rows $n \le 300$.

| period | what happened | section |
|---|---|---|
| 5 Nov 15:08 – 7 Nov | exact tools; first reduction; asymptotic results in every region; the root cut into nodes | 4.1 – 4.5 |
| 8 – 9 Nov | the right half closed; the user's decisions on formalization, computation and the question "why" | 4.6 – 4.7 |
| 9 – 11 Nov | the left half reduced to one comparison; root informally proved 11 Nov 16:14 | 4.8 – 4.10 |
| 12 – 13 Nov | explorations of "why"; a gap in the proof repaired; the instance stops 13 Nov 06:35 | 4.11 |
| 12 – 20 Nov | the manuscript; the early hours of 19 Nov; the paper | 5.1 – 5.4 |
| 19 Nov 11:50 – 20 Nov | the later instance: first wave, the paper inserted, first proofs accepted and set back | 6.1 – 6.4 |
| 21 Nov | the standard of acceptance settled; first Lean proof | 6.5 – 6.6 |
| 22 Nov | the right half stands; the left half; root informally proved 21:19 | 6.7 – 6.8 |
| 22 – 23 Nov | formalization; root formally proved 23 Nov 08:10; the table of nodes | 6.9, 6.10 |

## 4. The earlier instance, 5 to 13 November

### 4.1 The terms of the start

The earlier instance was created on 5 November at 15:08.[^8] Its settings at that commit allowed eight sessions at a time, on one model with a second as fallback, and named Lean 4.32.0 with Mathlib as the proof assistant, with the three standard axioms `propext`, `Classical.choice` and `Quot.sound` as the only ones a formal proof might use.[^9]

A standing input was submitted with the creation,[^10] and it set the terms. The text is unsigned and speaks of the user in the third person. The sources do not say who drafted it.

By these terms the instance was to start from the statement alone: no earlier instance, session, proof, computation or summary of the literature was to be opened, and only knowledge produced and reviewed inside the instance was to pass between sessions. The input defined two levels of success. "Mathematical resolution" meant a proof for all $n$, or an exact refutation; a wider computed range or a result for particular $n$ was a partial result. "Academic completion" asked in addition for an account of what the result adds to the literature and of the structure or obstacle that produces the conclusion, and it sets three questions for a reviewer. The first is whether the result has been compared, hypothesis by hypothesis, with existing theorems that might contain it. The second is which decomposition, correspondence or inequality the proof rests on and which phenomenon of the problem it explains. The third is whether the range and the limits of the result are stated exactly, beyond computations and examples. Separately, the whole statement was required as a Lean theorem about the original parking process, compiled by an independent review, before full achievement could be declared. The main product was to be a mathematical exposition in Korean that a person could follow.

The records that were read show the mark of this input:[^422] each begins by restating the original process with cars in arrival order, separates what is known in the literature from what is its own, and closes by saying what of the two levels of success remains unachieved.

The input gives no reason for the clause on starting afresh. It is to be read against a fact that the sources attest: the same conjecture had been attempted before, more than once.[^423] A directory beside the instance holds an older run of 22 October, of an older generation of the system, which published 165 records in one day and ended with the root unresolved.[^11] Its own goal file already forbids the use of still earlier research. A further instance was started on 23 October and has operator files up to 2 November;[^12] its idle runner was stopped on the evening of 5 November.[^13]

The sources do not say why the research was begun again from nothing on 5 November. The memory notes, which are kept one with each commit of the repository in which the research system itself is developed (section 2.2), record that the system had been rebuilt: its new baseline was committed that morning,[^14] and its final verification was committed at 15:04, four minutes before the instance was created.[^15] The nearness of the two times is all that connects the rebuilding with the fresh start.

### 4.2 The first day: the tools, and a first reduction

The first two sessions, an attempt and an exploration, each finished in about twenty minutes, within a minute of each other, and their records were published two seconds apart at 15:55.[^16] Independently of each other they had built the same set of exact tools, and almost everything that follows rests on these. Reviews of the earlier instance *accepted* what this section tells as proved, on proofs in words: as claims of the records of the first day and of the night after it and, where the section tells how a parking function determines its forest, as claims of the records of the following days or inside the proofs of such claims. Where a statement rests on a computation, its sentence names the arithmetic, and where it has another standing, its sentence says so.[^424] The last-car recurrence was also proved in Lean on the next day, as section 4.3 tells.

The first tool is a recurrence for the cumulative counts $A_{n,\ell}$, found by looking at the last car to arrive. If that car parks in spot $j+1$, the spot was empty until then, so no earlier car drove past it. The cars to its left and the cars to its right then form two smaller parking functions whose displacements are unchanged, and the last car has $\min(j+1, \ell+1)$ preferences that keep its displacement at most $\ell$. Hence

$$A_{n,\ell} = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} \binom{n-1}{j} \cdot \min(j+1, \ell+1) \cdot A_{j,\ell} \cdot A_{n-1-j,\ell}, \qquad A_{0,\ell} = 1.$$

This is the *last-car recurrence*, and it is a known theorem (Aguilar-Fraga et al., arXiv:2311.14055, Theorem 3.7).[^425] It allows exact rows to be computed by integer arithmetic, and it is the combinatorial fact about parking on which the Lean development of the later instance would be built, sixteen days later (section 6.6).

The second tool looks at where the cars end up. The *outcome* of a parking function is the permutation $\sigma$ that records which car stands in which spot. For the car in spot $i$ let $h_i$, its *capacity*, be the distance back to the nearest spot on its left that holds a later-arriving car (or to the start of the row). Given $\sigma$, the displacements of the $n$ cars can be chosen independently, the $i$-th in $\lbrace 0, \ldots, h_i - 1 \rbrace$. So the parking functions with outcome $\sigma$ and $M \le \ell$ number $\prod_i \min(h_i, \ell+1)$. This is the *product formula*, and it is in Celano et al.

The product formula suggests proving unimodality one outcome at a time, and both records close that road at once. The outcome (1,2,3,5,4,6,7) for $n = 7$ has capacities 1, 2, 3, 4, 1, 6, 7, hence 1008 parking functions, and the numbers of them with $M = 0, 1, \ldots, 6$ are 1, 31, 130, 222, 216, 264, 144, which fall from 222 to 216 and rise again. The factors $\min(h_i, \ell+1)$ stop growing at different levels $\ell$, and a single outcome need not be unimodal.

The same two records closed a second road. From exact rows (computed up to $n = 120$ in one and up to $n = 150$ in the other) they found that the usual stronger property fails. A positive sequence is *log-concave* if $c_i^2 \ge c_{i-1}c_{i+1}$, and log-concave sequences are unimodal. But $b_{80,40}^2 \lt b_{80,39} \cdot b_{80,41}$, and the first record proves that log-concavity fails for every sufficiently large $n$. Whatever proves unimodality has to be weaker than log-concavity.

The second record proposed the instance's first reduction, forty-seven minutes after the start. Call Q the statement that, for every $n$, once a row has strictly decreased it is log-concave from there on:

$$b_{n,\ell} \gt b_{n,\ell+1} \quad \text{implies} \quad b_{n,\ell+1}^2 \ge b_{n,\ell} \cdot b_{n,\ell+2}.$$

Then $b_{n,\ell+2} \le b_{n,\ell+1}^2/b_{n,\ell} \lt b_{n,\ell+1}$, so a row that has begun to fall keeps falling, and Q implies the root. The review accepted Q as a node and "Q implies the root" as a relation at 15:55.[^17] Six days later the frontier would count the root as proved through this relation, among others (section 4.9).

During the rest of the day the sessions added the picture in which most of the earlier instance's analysis is set. A record of 16:52[^18] replaced parking functions by labelled rooted forests on $n$ vertices. Write $t_v$ for the number of vertices of the subtree of a vertex $v$, the vertex itself included. The record counted, at each vertex, the descendants whose label is smaller than the label of the vertex. It showed that the forests in which this number is at most $\ell$ at every vertex are as many as the parking functions with $M \le \ell$, because both counts satisfy the last-car recurrence; and that, when the shape of the forest is fixed and the labels are uniformly random, these numbers are independent, the one at $v$ being uniform on $\lbrace 0, \ldots, t_v - 1 \rbrace$.[^426] So if each vertex $v$ of a uniform random forest is given an independent mark uniform on $\lbrace 0, \ldots, t_v - 1 \rbrace$, then the largest mark has the same distribution as the maximum displacement of a uniform parking function. In particular the probability that $M \le \ell$ is the expectation of $\prod_v \min(t_v, \ell+1)/t_v$ over uniform random forests. This is the *forest picture*.

A parking function determines its forest through its outcome. The vertices are the spots. The parent of a spot is the nearest spot to its right whose car arrived later, and a spot with no later-arriving car to its right is a root. The subtree of a spot $i$, that is, the spot with all its descendants, is then a stretch of consecutive spots: it ends at $i$ and begins just after the nearest spot on the left of $i$ that holds a later-arriving car, or at the start of the row. Its number of vertices is the capacity $h_i$ of the product formula, so that $t_v = h_i$ when $v$ is the vertex of spot $i$.[^126] The mark of a vertex is the displacement of the car in its spot: given the outcome, it can be any of the values $0, \ldots, h_i - 1$, independently for the different spots, which is the product formula.[^427]

In cars and spots, then, a tree of the forest is the subtree of a root: a stretch of consecutive spots whose last spot holds a car that arrived later than every car to its right, and so last among the cars of the stretch. The trees cover the row from left to right, one stretch after another. The cars of a tree park among themselves: restricted to the stretch, they form a parking function of the length of the stretch in which the car that arrives last takes the last spot, and their displacements are unchanged. Call such a parking function, of length $t$ and with its last car in its last spot, an *arrangement* of a tree with $t$ vertices. Before the last car arrives, the other cars of the tree have filled all its other spots, so that they form a parking function one shorter, and the forest of that shorter parking function is what hangs from the root of the tree. A tree with $t$ vertices is thus the spot of its root together with a parking function of length $t - 1$ on its other spots. The mark of the root, which is the displacement of the last car, can be any of the $t$ values $0, \ldots, t - 1$ whatever that parking function is, so that in a uniformly random arrangement it is uniform on these values and independent of the rest. Hence the arrangements of a tree with $t$ vertices that have $M \le \ell$ number

$$\min(t, \ell+1) \cdot A_{t-1,\ell},$$

the displacements of the last car that do not exceed $\ell$, times the parking functions of length $t - 1$ with $M \le \ell$ that the cars before it can form.[^428] Conversely, a parking function is given exactly by the division of its cars among the trees and by an arrangement of each tree; the trees then stand in the row in the order of their last cars, the tree whose last car arrives latest at the left.[^429]

Without a restriction on the displacements, that is, for $\ell = t - 1$, the count is $t \cdot p_{t-1} = t^{t-1}$: a tree with $t$ vertices has as many arrangements as there are rooted labelled trees on $t$ vertices. A parking function of length $n$ and a rooted labelled forest on $n$ vertices are thus made in the same way, by dividing $n$ labels into sets and choosing for each set of $t$ labels one of $t^{t-1}$ structures, and so the trees of the forest of a uniformly random parking function are distributed as the trees of a uniformly random rooted forest.[^430]

On the first day itself the forest picture was a model with the same counts as the parking functions. The record that brought it proved the equality of the counts through the recurrence. For the equality itself it pointed to a paper of 2011, by Guedes de Oliveira and Las Vergnas, in which the displacements of a parking function are a rearrangement of the numbers counted at the vertices of a labelled tree that corresponds to it; its exposition did not describe that correspondence and did not use the outcome. What the outcome carried on that day, in the first record of the day, was a binary tree.[^431]

The construction of the forest from the outcome, as it is told above, was written down on the two days that followed. On 6 November, at 03:56, a record turned that binary tree into a forest on the spots, in which the subtree of spot $i$ has $h_i$ vertices. At 05:46 a record proved that the pairs of capacity and displacement of a uniformly random parking function, taken all together, are distributed as the pairs of subtree size and number of descendants with a smaller label in a uniformly random forest of the model. At 23:43 a record cut a parking function into its trees, as stretches of spots, and counted them. On 7 November, at 04:25, a record gave the rule for the parent directly in spots.[^432] Later records state the correspondence again where they use it; this account tells it once, where the picture is introduced. Nothing of what these records state was formalized in the earlier instance.[^433][^127]

The forest picture brings with it the *chain of largest trees*. In the product $\prod_v \min(t_v, \ell+1)/t_v$ of the forest picture, a vertex contributes a factor different from 1 only if its subtree has more than $\ell + 1$ vertices, and two disjoint subtrees cannot both have more than $n/2$ vertices. So at a level in the right half of the row the subtrees that matter are nested: the largest tree of the forest, then the largest of the subtrees hanging from its root, and so on down for as long as the size stays above $\ell + 1$. This sequence of sizes is the chain of largest trees, or simply the chain, and it is what is meant wherever this account speaks of largest trees or of nested large subtrees; outside the right half, disjoint subtrees of more than $\ell + 1$ vertices can occur, and the chain no longer counts every factor.[^434] By this account's reading, the nesting is the reason why positions in the right half of a row proved so much more tractable than positions in the left half, and why the decompositions told in this account cut the row near its centre.

The first sign result for a range that grows with $n$ came in the record that brought the forest picture: the last $R$ entries of a row decrease strictly as soon as $n \ge 20 \cdot R^{3/2}$. The place of the peak followed the same evening, when records of 17:57 and 18:59[^19] proved local limit theorems showing that the peak of the row sits at a deficit of order $n^{2/3}$.

A record of 19:11[^20] derived a recurrence along the chain, with the weights of the Borel distribution $\mathrm{Bor}(m) = e^{-m} m^{m-1}/m!$, and from it the profile of the cumulative counts $A_{n,\lambda n}$ for $1/2 \lt \lambda \lt 1$. That the row itself increases in that range was still a conjecture in this record. Its numerics for $n \le 1200$, which are not certified, put the smallest margin of increase near $\ell \approx n/2$ for the larger $n$. The review refused the statement that this is so always (at $n = 100$ the minimum lies elsewhere).

The same results were found several times over that day, because sessions running side by side cannot see each other's unpublished work. Three sessions independently cut a parking function at its *breakpoints*, the positions $j \lt n$ such that exactly $j$ cars prefer one of the first $j$ spots, into *prime* parking functions, those with no breakpoint. They found that the statement corresponding to the root is false for primes: for every $n \ge 21$ the numbers $q(n,\ell)$ of prime parking functions of length $n$ with $M = \ell$ satisfy $q(n,n-3) \gt q(n,n-2) \lt q(n,n-1)$, so that the row of the primes dips just before its right end.[^399] Since the primes are asymptotically a fixed fraction $e^{-2}$ of all parking functions and have the same limit law, the first of the three records concludes that no argument using only the limit law can prove unimodality.

In the evening the program driving the sessions was changed. Between 20:32 and 23:21 nothing ran, and the runner was restarted with a version in which an attempt registers a section of a node, so that several attempts can share one node.[^21] The change shows in the session files, where the commands offered to a session change from one for taking a whole node, `claim` (unrelated to the claims of a record), to `lease`, `section`, `release`. Until then a node could be claimed only whole, and at most one attempt had worked on the root and one on Q at any time, so that of eight possible sessions about three were at work.

By the next morning two computer-assisted statements were in hand, the first of them for every $n$. Q holds on the last 102 entries of every row,[^22] and every row with $n \le 800$ is strictly unimodal, by exact integers.[^23]

The first of these is a statement for all $n$ obtained from finitely many certificates. An entry at a fixed distance from the right end of row $n$ is, for all large $n$, a fixed finite combination of the numbers $p_{n-1}, p_{n-2}, \ldots$ with coefficients that are polynomials in $n$. For each of the one hundred positions concerned (an inequality involves three consecutive entries, whence 102) the required inequality therefore becomes the negativity of one explicit polynomial expression. This was certified once in exact rational arithmetic for all $n$ beyond an explicit point, and the rows below that point were checked directly. This account calls a computation *certified* when its record gives it as part of a proof and says what guarantees its arithmetic, and its review accepted it, in some cases only after correcting it and running it again; the guarantees are of different kinds, exact integers or rationals, interval arithmetic, or double precision with error bounds fixed beforehand, and the account as a rule names the kind with the computation.

The second of these records also proved that $b_{n,k} \gt b_{n,k-1}$ whenever $n \ge 2k^2 + k$, so that each column of steps is eventually positive.

### 4.3 The second day: every region asymptotically, and a Lean proof that is not on the path

On 6 November, between 05:48 and 05:54, three attempt sessions each registered a section of the root. The three sections cut each row at its *centre*, the position $m = \lceil n/2 \rceil$: they are the part of each row to the right of the centre, the part to the left, and the pairs of positions that straddle the centre.[^400] A fourth session then found nothing left to register, and its report on why became the published record of the logic of the split:[^24] a sequence is unimodal exactly when there is no strict decrease followed later by a strict increase, and cutting the positions of such a pair at a chosen point of the row gives three conditions, left, right and straddling. The division of labour made here is, with the straddling part later split in two, the decomposition of the root that the frontier would carry from the next day. It existed for thirty-one hours only as sections.

Working under these sections, the sessions obtained on that day the sign of the step in almost every region of the row, but only for all sufficiently large $n$. Their reviews *accepted* these results, and the others that this section tells, as claims proved in words; where a statement rests on a computation or on a Lean proof, its sentence says so.[^435] The right half is unimodal for large $n$.[^25] The single step at the centre is eventually positive.[^26] The row increases in a band of width of order $n^{2/3}$ below the centre,[^27] on an initial segment of about $n \cdot \log\log n/(\log n)^2$ entries,[^28] and, by the evening, at all positions between any fixed fraction of $n$ and the centre. The last of these is in a record of 22:44:[^29] for each fixed $\lambda_0$ in (0, 1/2) and all sufficiently large $n$, $b_{n,q} \gt b_{n,q-1}$ whenever $\lambda_0 \cdot n \le q \le \lceil n/2 \rceil$. This theorem is a result of the peer, which proved it after it had ended its work: since that afternoon the system made a session that had ended its work go on in the same conversation and decide whether it might stop (section 7.5), and the peer proved the theorem in such a continuation. None of these results has an explicit starting point, and every record says so.

The explicit statements of the day were all at the two ends of the row. For example, the last $R$ entries decrease for $n \ge (16/5) \cdot R^{3/2}$, by a proof in words whose constant rests on 254 comparisons of rational numbers in exact arithmetic,[^30] and the first 121 entries increase for $n \ge 143$, by an exact finite certificate joined to a propagation that is proved in words.[^31] The second of these records certified more than its headline: for each column $2 \le k \le 120$ the step is negative from row $k$ up to a certain row and positive from that row on, and that row does not decrease with $k$.[^32] (Row $k$ is counted as the first row of column $k$: $b_{k,k}$ is read as 0, so that $d_{k,k} = -b_{k,k-1}$ is negative.)

The row at which a column turns from negative to positive will be called its *crossing row*.[^436] The records differ in whether they mean by it the row of the first positive or of the first non-negative step, which is the same row wherever no step vanishes. That every column crosses once, at a row that does not decrease with $k$, is the pattern of signs to which section 4.8 returns.

One exact identity of the morning returns throughout the rest of this account. A record of 08:09[^33] obtained it by a sign-reversing involution on the parking functions with a given outcome, in the sense of section 4.2:

$$d_{n,k} = 2 \cdot T(n,k) - B(n,k-1),$$

where $T(n,k)$ counts the parking functions with $M = k$ in which the maximum is attained by a positive even number of cars, and $B(n,k-1)$ counts those with $M = k-1$ in which every car attaining the maximum stands in a spot of capacity exactly $k$, so that its displacement could not have been larger. Parity enters through the involution. It exchanges the displacements $k - 1$ and $k$ of one car and leaves the outcome as it is. Within $M = k$ this pairs the parking functions in which an odd number of cars attain the maximum with those in which an even number do, and the odd ones that are left over it pairs with the parking functions with $M = k - 1$ that $B$ does not count.[^437] The sign of a step is thus a contest between two positive counts: the *ties* at level $k$, which $T$ counts, and the *blocked maxima* at level $k - 1$, which $B$ counts.

At the centre of the row these two counts were found to agree to leading order, with a relative difference of order $k^{-1/3}$,[^34] and the same was reported for the range between the centre and the peak.[^401] By this account's reading, that near-cancellation is the reason soft arguments fail on the problem. It is met again in every later phase.

The day also produced the instance's only formal proof. At 04:48 a record had isolated an asymptotic statement about a generating function.[^35] With $F_{\ell}(z) = \sum_m A_{m,\ell} z^m/m!$, it says that the increments $F_y(1/e) - F_{y-1}(1/e)$ are asymptotic to $e/\sqrt{2\pi} \cdot y^{-3/2}$. The statement is the node D of the earlier instance. It was accepted as a node because the root implies it, so that refuting D would refute the root; it is a necessary condition, not a stage on the way to a proof.

D was proved informally thirty-five minutes later.[^36] At that time the system assigned a formalizer to every informally proved node, and so seven formalizer sessions in turn worked on D, each given the records of those before. The first six published partial Lean developments, whose claims the reviews supported after compiling the Lean sources themselves; none of the six proposed a solution of the node.[^37] They followed the informal proof, and the sixth had a Lean proof of an implication: D holds if a certain family of finite measures built from the counts converges weakly to its expected limit. The convergence itself remained unproved in Lean. The seventh went round it by a different analytic route, and at 17:13 the review accepted the theorem `Parking.critical_increment_unconditional`, with 98 Lean files, all of which went through the review's own build, and no axioms beyond the three allowed.[^38] With this the node D was *formally proved*.

The review states explicitly that this is not a solution of the root or of Q and adds no implication from D to the root. The operator's note of that evening records that D, being a necessary condition, had not contributed to the root.[^39] What the effort left behind was a Lean model of the parking process itself, with proofs that the number of parking functions is $(n+1)^{n-1}$ and that the counts $A_{n,\ell}$ of the actual process satisfy the last-car recurrence.

### 4.4 An evaluation from outside, and the turn to explicit statements

On the evening of 6 November, at 20:43, a standing input of a new kind was submitted.[^40] It carried the conclusions of an AI session outside the instance that had read the published records up to then and written an evaluation, and it presented itself as reference knowledge and not as an instruction. The input is no longer among the standing inputs of the instance. It was withdrawn between 13:48 and 13:56 the next day, and it can be read in the files of the sessions started in between and in a copy kept by the operator.[^41] No source records who withdrew it or why.

Its diagnosis was that the records were correct as far as checked, but that dividing a row into regions and proving limits in each "structurally yields only sufficiently large n", with thresholds that are not explicit or are far beyond computation.[^438] Its proposal was to control one identity with explicit constants and to cover the range below the explicit threshold by finite computation.

The identity comes from the product formula, read in the forest picture of section 4.2, where every subtree of the forest contributes one factor. As long as the level is near $k$, a subtree with more than $k$ vertices contributes to $A_{n, \cdot}$ a factor proportional to the level. A subtree with exactly $k$ vertices, the *threshold size*, contributes a factor that stops growing exactly at level $k - 1$. The step $d_{n,k}$ is a second difference of the cumulative counts in the level (section 2.1), and taking the second difference in the level therefore gives

$$d_{n,k}/A_{n,k-1} = \mathbb{E}[(1+1/k)^Z + (1-1/k)^Z - 2] - \mathbb{E}[(1-1/k)^Z \cdot (1 - (1-1/k)^H)],$$

where $\mathbb{E}$ is the expectation over a random forest weighted by the product formula at level $k - 1$, $Z$ is the number of subtrees with more than $k$ vertices and $H$ the number with exactly $k$. The first term is non-negative. It is the *curvature term* of this account, and the second term is the loss from threshold subtrees. In the evaluation the two terms were a heuristic expansion; the exact form given here is that of a record of the next morning, all of whose claims its review *accepted*.[^439]

The records of the following night and day take the evaluation up by name, and what is told of them here are claims that their reviews accepted, on proofs in words where the sentence names no computation. The evaluation had proposed to prove the left half through a comparison of moments, $\mathbb{E}[Z^2] \ge C \cdot k \cdot \mathbb{E}[H]$ with a constant $C \gt 1$. One record showed that this fails eventually for every fixed $C \gt 1$, and that with $C = 1$ it holds only by a relative margin that tends to zero, of order $k^{-1/3}$ at the centre; an observation of the same record in double precision, which is not a certified computation, has it fail with $C = 1.5$ already at $n = 1327$.[^42] Another turned the evaluation's heuristic expansion into an exact lower bound for the step $d_{n,k}$ and computed it at the centre of the row. By a computation in exact integers it found the bound negative for $16 \le n \le 21$,[^43] rows in which the step at the centre is positive, so that the bound cannot show the increase at the centre in those rows.[^44] A third[^45] showed that the second-moment inequality to which such a route leads is $2 \cdot T_2(n,k) \ge B(n,k-1)$, with $T_2$ the number of parking functions in which exactly two cars attain the maximum $k$. That inequality is the identity $d = 2T - B$ of section 4.3 with the ties of four or more cars discarded, hence slightly stronger than $d \ge 0$ and no simpler. And the record of 08:12 on 7 November wrote the evaluation's two terms without remainder.[^46]

More broadly, from this day the instance's work was aimed at statements with explicit ranges, closed below by certified computation. Statements with explicit ranges had been proved before the input arrived, as sections 4.2 and 4.3 tell. Fifteen records name the evaluation, all of them published between the night of 6 November and the afternoon of 7 November: the four just told and eleven others, which take up one or another of its proposals, set their own results beside its diagnosis or check its numbers.[^440] None of the passages in which these records name the evaluation says that a record turned to explicit ranges or to certificates because of it, so that, to judge by these passages, how much of the turn is the input's doing is not established.

### 4.5 The root is cut into nodes

On 7 November the division of labour became the frontier. At 08:12 a record split Q at the centre into a right half Q_R and a left half Q_L.[^47] At 12:50 another record[^48] created four nodes. They are stated in terms of a decrease and an increase: a *decrease at position* $i$ means $b_{n,i} \gt b_{n,i+1}$, and an *increase at position* $j$ means $b_{n,j} \lt b_{n,j+1}$. There is one node for each place where a decrease at position $i$ followed by an increase at position $j$ could lie relative to the centre $m = \lceil n/2 \rceil$:

- P_C, the *central* part: $i = m-1$ and $j \ge m$;
- P_R, the *right* part: $m \le i \lt j$;
- P_X, the *straddling* part: $i \lt m-1$ and $j \ge m$;
- P_L, the *left* part: $i \lt j \lt m$.

Each node says that no such pair exists, for every $n$. The four together imply the root. They are the division of labour of section 4.3, with its straddling part split in two. The record gives its reason for making nodes of what had been sections: as sections the results existed only inside records, and the same section had been registered anew each time a pipeline ended.

P_C was proved in the same record as the four nodes, and the review *accepted* the proof as a solution of the node. It is the one solution that this section tells. The rest of what the section gives as proved are relations and claims that reviews of the earlier instance accepted, all on proofs in words. No formal proof of P_C or of the rest was published; where a statement rests on a computation, its sentence says so and names the arithmetic.[^441] The content of P_C is that the step at the centre, $d_{n,m}$, is positive for every $n \ge 16$. In the rows $2 \le n \le 15$ the step at the centre is negative, which is a decrease at position $m - 1$, and the node holds in these rows because no increase follows at a position $j \ge m$; the record takes this from an exact computation published before it.[^49] The proof has the two-part shape that all the earlier instance's proofs would have. One part is an analytic estimate with explicit constants for $n \ge 19999$. It is obtained from contour-integral representations of two positive quantities that bound the two counts in the identity $d = 2T - B$ of section 4.3, the ties of exactly two cars from below and the blocked maxima from above. The other part is a certificate in integer interval arithmetic with directed rounding for $16 \le n \le 20000$.

By the evening, relations accepted in a dozen records had collapsed the frontier to two statements. On the right, P_R was equivalent to Q_R and to a plain increase statement W: for $n \ge 20001$, the row does not decrease from the centre up to the position at distance $\mathfrak{m}(n) + 2$ from the right end, where $\mathfrak{m}(n)$ is about $n^{2/3}$.[^50] On the remaining stretch at the right end, which contains the peak, the property Q had been proved with explicit constants, in the first of these records by a theorem that is itself computer-assisted and before it by certificates. So W was all that Q_R still needed.

On the left, P_L, P_X and Q_L were all equivalent to another plain increase statement: for columns $k \ge 501$ and rows $n$ from $\max(2k+1, 10001)$ up to about $0.27 \cdot k^2$, $b_{n,k-1} \le b_{n,k}$.[^51] This account calls it the *left increase statement of 7 November*.

The upper end of this range had moved, and would move again. By this account's reading of the statements, the end moved each time because a ratio of neighbouring cumulative counts was bounded better: the bound of the first night made column $k$ positive from $n = 2k^2 + k$ on;[^52] a sharper bound of the same ratio $A_{n,k}/A_{n,k-1}$ brought this to about $0.48 \cdot k^2$;[^53] a bound on the number of subtrees of size exactly $k$ brought it to about $0.27 \cdot k^2$;[^54] and when that bound was replaced, on 8 and 9 November, by an estimate of the true order of that number, the end came down to about $2 \cdot k^{3/2}$, in a claim that its review accepted as a computer-assisted proof, in interval arithmetic in floating point.[^55]

Everything outside these two ranges had been closed, the lower ranges by certificates. The certificates cover:

- all rows $n \le 1000$, exactly;[^56]
- the left half of every row $n \le 10000$, and the columns $k \le 500$ for all rows $n \ge 2k + 1$ apart from four small exceptions;
- the right-half statement for $n \le 20000$, at 99,970,002 positions, in integer interval arithmetic.[^57]

The two certificates of the second item are of one record, which uses no floating-point operation in them. They therefore rest on no assumption about how such arithmetic rounds, unlike the certificates published before it for these ranges, which had rested on such an assumption. For $k \ge 501$ the record certifies in integer interval arithmetic the 20,245,500 comparisons of neighbouring entries with $2k + 1 \le n \le 10000$.[^402]

The certificate of the columns closes every row $n \ge 2k + 1$, for $121 \le k \le 500$, by joining a computation over finitely many rows to a propagation. The interval computation certifies two things: that the ratio $A_{n,k}/A_{n,k-1}$ is at least 17/10 in every row of a band, from some row to its double, and that the step is positive in the rows up to the end of the band. A lemma published earlier carries the first of the two to all later rows: a lower bound, not below 1, for this ratio that holds in every row of such a band holds in every later row. And a ratio of at least 17/10 makes the step positive: the step is $A_{n,k-1}$ times the sum of this ratio and of the ratio $A_{n,k-2}/A_{n,k-1}$, less 2, and an inequality published earlier bounds the second ratio from below by a function of the first, with which the sum exceeds 2.[^58] For the columns $k \le 120$ its record relies on the exact certificate of 6 November, of which section 4.3 tells, for the rows $n \ge 16$, and on exact rows below them; the four exceptions are the positions $(n,k) = (5,2)$, (7,3), (9,4) and (11,5), at which the step is negative.[^59]

A by-product of that morning's record of 08:12, the record that split Q at the centre, deserves mention because it returns later under another name. For a position in the right half, the record replaced the four cumulative counts $A_{n,k-2}, \ldots, A_{n,k+1}$, which two consecutive steps involve between them, by the values at $\gamma = k - 1, \ldots, k + 2$ of one polynomial $\mathcal{A}(\gamma)$ with non-negative coefficients, corrected at the two ends by positive boundary terms $B_-$ and $B_+$.

The polynomial is the product formula with the set of subtrees larger than $k$ held fixed and the level made a variable. In the forest picture of section 4.2 the count $A_{n,\ell}$ is a sum over forests in which each vertex whose subtree has more than $\ell + 1$ vertices contributes the factor $(\ell+1)/t_v$. Let instead each vertex with $t_v \gt k$ contribute the factor $\gamma/t_v$, where the variable $\gamma$ takes the place of $\ell + 1$, the number with which a level $\ell$ enters the product formula. The sum is then the polynomial $\mathcal{A}(\gamma)$, the *frozen polynomial*, and the counts at the levels $k - 2$ to $k + 1$ are set beside the values at $\gamma = k - 1$ to $k + 2$.[^60]

The *boundary terms* measure by how much the values at the two ends differ from the counts. At the two middle values the frozen polynomial is the count itself, $\mathcal{A}(k) = A_{n,k-1}$ and $\mathcal{A}(k+1) = A_{n,k}$; at the two ends it exceeds the count, $\mathcal{A}(k-1) = A_{n,k-2} + B_-$ and $\mathcal{A}(k+2) = A_{n,k+1} + B_+$. Each excess comes from one size of subtree that the frozen set treats otherwise than the count does. A subtree of exactly $k$ vertices is left out of the frozen set, while at level $k - 2$ it has the factor $(k-1)/k$; this accounts for $B_-$. A subtree of exactly $k + 1$ vertices has the factor $(k+2)/(k+1)$ in $\mathcal{A}(k+2)$, while at level $k + 1$ its factor is 1; this accounts for $B_+$. At a position of the right half a forest has at most one subtree of each of these two sizes.[^442] The term $B_-$ is the blocked count $B(n,k-1)$ of the identity of section 4.3. The term $B_+$ is not the blocked count of the next step: the two are values of one polynomial in $\gamma$, whose value at $\gamma = k$ is the blocked count $B(n,k)$ and whose value at $\gamma = k + 2$, the place at which the frozen polynomial is set beside $A_{n,k+1}$, is $B_+$.[^62]

Write $\Delta$ and $\Delta'$ for the second differences of $\mathcal{A}$ at $k$ and at $k + 1$. They are positive because the coefficients are, and $\Delta' \ge \Delta$. A step is a second difference of the cumulative counts in the level, and so two consecutive steps become

$$d_{n,k} = \Delta - B_- \quad \text{and} \quad d_{n,k+1} = \Delta' - B_+,$$

where $\Delta$ is the term $2T$ of the identity of section 4.3 and $B_-$ its blocked count. Divided by $A_{n,k-1}$, the same two are the curvature term and the loss from threshold subtrees in the identity displayed in section 4.4.[^61]

The record showed that the single inequality

$$S = B_+ \cdot d_{n,k} - B_- \cdot d_{n,k+1} \ge 0$$

forces a row that has started to fall to keep falling: if $S \ge 0$ and $d_{n,k} \lt 0$, then $d_{n,k+1} \le d_{n,k}$. By the two displayed steps $S = B_+ \cdot \Delta - B_- \cdot \Delta'$, so that $S \ge 0$ gives $B_+ \ge B_- \cdot \Delta'/\Delta$; then $d_{n,k+1} - d_{n,k} = (\Delta' - \Delta) + B_- - B_+$ is at most $(\Delta' - \Delta) \cdot (1 - B_-/\Delta)$, which is not positive, because $\Delta' \ge \Delta$ and because $d_{n,k} \lt 0$ says that $B_- \gt \Delta$.[^63]

The producer conjectured a stronger, term-by-term form. Apart from a constant term, the frozen polynomial is a sum, with positive coefficients, of polynomials of one explicit family; this writes $\Delta$ and $\Delta'$, and with them $S = B_+ \cdot \Delta - B_- \cdot \Delta'$, as sums over that family with the same coefficients, and the conjecture was that every term of the sum for $S$ is non-negative by itself. The conjecture was refuted: the peer found by an exact interval computation at $n = 7485$ that one of the terms is negative there.[^443] The inequality $S \ge 0$ itself stayed an unresolved node of the earlier instance to the end, the right half being closed another way. The quantity $S$ returns in sections 5.3 and 6.3.

The equivalences had a side effect on the running of the system. Attempts started in the afternoon began to conclude that every unresolved node was "occupied", because each was equivalent to one that another session held. Five attempts between 15:35 and 18:10 ended without doing research, and the three of them that left a note give that reason. (Fourteen more had ended in the same way earlier that day, before 08:02, for the older reason that every part of the root and of Q was covered by a registered section.)

A standing input of 18:42 answered this:[^66] an unoccupied node is not to be passed over merely because it is equivalent to an occupied one, since starting from a different statement may show different objects and methods. The input is unsigned, and it arrived in the same minute as an operator command that made the waiting attempts reconsider. The sources do not say whose words it carries. The attempts started afterwards took equivalent nodes side by side.

Between 18:06 and 18:35 that evening the settings were changed so that attempts, explorations and formalizations ran on a different model as their first choice, `claude-fable-5-1`. The change is visible in the files of the sessions started at 18:06 and at 18:35.[^444] No source says who made the change or why. At 22:02 an operator input asked attempts to register their section before computing, because unregistered work was invisible to the others.[^67]

### 4.6 The right half is closed

The right half was closed in the night of 8 to 9 November, at 01:16.[^68] The proof is an induction on $n$, and it rests on three ideas, which came one after another over the preceding day. The first two appeared in records of 02:03 and of 07:03 on 8 November, which proposed no solution and said that W was unresolved; what these two records proved are claims that their reviews accepted. The third came with the record that closed the right half. The review of that record *accepted* the proof on 9 November at 01:16 as a solution of the node W, the plain increase statement of section 4.5: a proof in words, not formalized, in which the inequalities with explicit numbers are *certified by computation*, in interval arithmetic, each on the range that is given where the inequality stands below.[^445] Where this section gives an estimate, an observation or a computed value that is no part of these proofs, its sentence says so.

The setting is the forest picture of section 4.2. Consider a position in the right half, $k \ge (n+1)/2$. A tree with more than $k$ vertices is then the largest tree of the forest and the only one that can produce a mark above the level, so the step of row $n$ is a mixture over the size $t$ of the largest tree:

$$d_{n,k}/p_n = \sum_{t=k}^{n} \pi_n(t) \cdot \chi_t(k).$$

Here $\pi_n(t)$ is the probability that the largest tree of a uniform random forest on $n$ vertices has $t$ vertices, and $\chi_t(k)$ is the same step for a single tree: the probability that the largest mark in a uniform random rooted tree with $t$ vertices equals $k$, minus the probability that it equals $k - 1$. The law $\pi_n$ has two properties on the right half, and the proof uses both: it increases with $t$, and it is convex, so that its increments increase as well.

The smaller rows enter through the $\chi_t(k)$. A tree with $t$ vertices is the spot of its root together with a parking function of length $t - 1$ on its other spots (section 4.2), and the mark of its root is uniform on $t$ values and independent of the rest. A tree with fewer than $k$ vertices has $\chi_t(k) = 0$, a tree of exactly $k$ vertices has $\chi_k(k) = -1/k$, and for $t \gt k$

$$\chi_t(k) = (k/t) \cdot d_{t-1,k}/p_{t-1} + (b_{t-1,k} + b_{t-1,k-1})/(t \cdot p_{t-1}),$$

where for $t = k + 1$ the entry $b_{k,k}$ is read as 0, as in section 2.1.[^446] The first term is the step of row $t - 1$ at the same level, with the positive factor $k/t$. The second is positive, and it is called below the *source term* of row $t - 1$: of the two terms it is the one that contains no step. So the induction hypothesis is the statement W itself in the smaller rows: wherever row $t - 1$ is known not to decrease at level $k$, $\chi_t(k)$ is positive and bounded below by the source term of row $t - 1$, which is explicit and positive. Besides $t = k$, $\chi_t(k)$ is negative for the sizes $t$ in a *window* just above $k$, which correspond to the rows in which level $k$ lies beyond the peak. The window is about $0.6 \cdot k^{2/3}$ wide; this figure is *observed in computed rows* and not proved, and the proof does not use it.[^447]

What is to be proved is W: for $n \ge 20001$ the row does not decrease from the centre up to the position at distance $\mathfrak{m}(n) + 2$ from the right end. The proof is by induction on $n$. At a position $(n,k)$ in the range of W the inductive argument uses that the steps $d_{m,k}$ of the same column are non-negative in every row $m$ from a first row, written $t_0 - 1$ below, up to $n - 1$. That first row is the first $m \ge k + 1$ whose deficit $m - k$ at level $k$ is at least $\lfloor (3/4) \cdot m^{2/3} \rfloor$, so that the rows left out include those in which level $k$ lies beyond the peak. In terms of trees, $\chi_t(k)$ is at least the source term of row $t - 1$ for every $t \ge t_0$, and the window lies among the sizes $k \lt t \lt t_0$. In the rows from $t_0 - 1$ to $n - 1$ the non-negativity follows from W for the rows 20001 to $n - 1$, from published statements near the peak, and from the certified rows up to 20000. Each of these uses rows below $n$ only, so that the whole is one induction on $n$; this frame was set up on 8 November, with the first of the three ideas.[^448]

The closing record did not have to run the induction from row 20001. The rows up to 50000 and the positions nearer to the peak it took from what was already published, as the close of this section tells. It ran the induction from $n = 50001$, and what the induction closes are the positions with $(n-k+1)/k^{2/3} \gt 10$.[^449]

The induction thus turns on one fact: at a position in the right half the step of row $n$ is an average, over the size of the largest tree of the forest, of the steps of single trees, and a tree with $t$ vertices carries the step of row $t - 1$, so that the rows below $n$ give the sign of almost every term of the average. Three things stood in the way of using this, and each of the three ideas removed one. First, the negative terms, those of the window, nearly cancelled the positive ones, each side being far larger than their difference; summing by parts in $t$ sets the negative terms against the positive ones before any weight is applied. What then remained was one sum of nearly cancelling terms, the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$, for which no bound was known; comparing each row with the next bounds it by the loss from a single tree of exactly $k$ vertices, a little enlarged. What remained after that was to bound the terms that were left by estimates that hold for all $n$ at once. The producer of the record that closed the right half put the lower bound for the positive terms into closed form, and supplied such an estimate from above for what the window still cost. The other upper bounds that were needed it had not been able to make explicit row by row; the third idea made all but one of them unnecessary, and the one that is left, a sum over the rows of the neighbouring column $k - 1$, becomes explicit when these rows are taken together and not one by one.

Used directly, this mixture gives nothing. It expresses the step of row $n$ through the steps of the rows below it, which the $\chi_t(k)$ contain, and earlier records had applied it to those steps in their turn, again and again. This expands the step along the chain, the nested sequence of largest trees of section 4.2, of which the largest tree of the forest is the first.[^450] In that expansion the chains passing through the window contribute to the positive and to the negative part alike, each larger than their difference by a factor that is estimated, and not proved, to be of order $n^{1/3}$: the estimate rests on a formal calculation of the orders and on values computed in double precision, and the review of the earlier record that states it supported it as an observation.[^451]

The remedy was the first of the three ideas, in a record of 8 November at 02:03:[^69] to sum by parts in $t$. The step then equals $\pi_n(k)$ times the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$, plus the increments $\pi_n(s+1) - \pi_n(s)$ times the tail sums $\sum_{t \gt s} \chi_t(k)$; and since the law $\pi_n$ is increasing on the right half, every increment is positive.[^70] Inside a tail sum the negative $\chi_t$ of the window are added to the positive $\chi_t$ of the larger sizes before any weight is applied, and what multiplies the result is an increment of the law and not its value. By a heuristic reckoning of the orders, which numerical values for the rows from 1000 to 20001 support and which is not proved, the window then costs an amount of the same order as the main term and no longer $n^{1/3}$ times it.[^452]

With the hypothesis on the rows from $t_0 - 1$ to $n - 1$ each tail sum has a lower bound, and together they give a lower bound for the step in three terms:

  $d_{n,k}/p_n \ge \pi_n(k)$ times the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$, minus a window loss, plus a sum of source terms.

The window loss is the sum of the negative parts of the $\chi_t(k)$ over the sizes $k \lt t \lt t_0$, each weighted by $\pi_n(t) - \pi_n(k)$. The third term is the sum of the source terms of the rows $m$ from $t_0 - 1$ to $n - 1$, each weighted by $\pi_n(m+1) - \pi_n(t_0-1)$; it is positive, and it is called below the *sum of the source terms*. For these two terms the record had explicit bounds.[^453] What remained was the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$, a difference of two nearly equal quantities.

The rearrangement has a free parameter: before summing by parts one may subtract from the law any linear function $\alpha \cdot t$ with $0 \le \alpha \le \pi_n(k+1) - \pi_n(k)$, at the price of a term $\alpha \cdot \sum_t t \cdot \chi_t(k)$. This uses the other property of the law: it is convex on the right half, so that what is left of it after the subtraction is still non-decreasing from $t = k$ on, and the rearrangement is carried out with that in the place of the law.[^71] The factor that multiplies the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$ is then $\pi_n(k) - \alpha \cdot k$, and with $\alpha = \pi_n(k)/k$ it is zero: the one term without a bound disappears. The record of 02:03 took $\alpha$ as large as is admissible, up to $\pi_n(k)/k$. The value $\pi_n(k)/k$ is admissible only where the first increment of the law is at least as large, which for large $n$ is where $k/n \ge 5/8$. Towards the centre the admissible $\alpha$ is close to zero, and the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$ stays in the bound. The peer of that record removed it there by subtracting a second function from the law, and was then left with two unproved statements. One was an explicit inequality that still had to be evaluated. The other was not explicit: a lower bound of order $1/k$ for a difference between the sums of two neighbouring columns of probabilities, each summed along its rows. The single terms of those sums are of order $k^{-2/3}$, so that the cancellation between the two columns would have had to be proved to a relative precision of order $k^{-1/3}$.[^454]

The second idea, in a record of 07:03,[^72] bounded the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$ without cancellation, by comparing each row with the next. The comparison of the rows is a statement about the deficit of the maximum displacement: for $m \ge 2$ and $1 \le r \le m/2$, the probability of deficit $r$ in row $m + 1$ is at least $1 - \beta$ times that in row $m$, with an explicit $\beta$ of order $1/m$.[^455] Its proof moves chains. The probability that $M = m - r$ in row $m$ is a sum over the possible chains $s_1 \gt s_2 \gt \ldots$ of products of Borel weights $\mathrm{Bor}(j)$ of the gaps between consecutive sizes, $\mathrm{Bor}$ being the Borel distribution of section 4.2. Adding 1 to every size maps the chains for row $m$ and deficit $r$ one-to-one onto those for row $m + 1$ and the same deficit, and leaves every gap, hence every Borel weight, unchanged. Comparing the remaining factors term by term gives the statement.

The comparison of the rows bears on the sum of the $\chi_t(k)$ because of how that sum is made up.
