The sum is $-1/k$, which is the $\chi_k(k)$ of the tree with exactly $k$ vertices, plus a sum over the rows $m$ from $k$ to $n - 1$ in which the probability $b_{m,k}/p_m$ of level $k$ in row $m$ enters with a positive coefficient and the probability $b_{m,k-1}/p_m$ of level $k - 1$ with a negative one.[^73] Pair the positive term of row $m + 1$ with the negative term of row $m$. The two have the same deficit, so the comparison of the two rows bounds each pair from below by a multiple of $b_{m,k-1}/p_m$ alone. The negative term of the top row $n - 1$ has no partner and stays as it is.[^74] Hence

$$\sum_{t=k}^{n} \chi_t(k) \ge -(1/k) \cdot (1 + \mathcal{E} + \text{the term of the top row}).$$

The 1 is the loss $\chi_k(k)$ from one tree of exactly $k$ vertices. $\mathcal{E}$ is what the pairs leave, a weighted sum of the entries $b_{m,k-1}$ of column $k - 1$, each divided by $p_m$, over the rows of its right half; and the term of the top row, the unpaired one, is a multiple of the entry $b_{n-1,k-1}$.[^75]

With this the step of row $n$ is bounded below by an expression in which every term has one sign. When the parameter $\alpha$ is zero the expression is the sum of the source terms, minus the window loss, minus the loss $\pi_n(k)/k$ from one tree of exactly $k$ vertices, the last multiplied by the sum of 1, of $\mathcal{E}$ and of the term of the top row. What the bound still lacked were estimates of its terms that hold for all $n$ at once: a lower bound for the sum of the source terms, and upper bounds for the window loss, for $\mathcal{E}$ and for the term of the top row. For the window loss it sufficed to estimate the upper bound that the first record had given, an explicit sum over the sizes $k \lt t \lt t_0$; this estimate, too, was still to be made.[^456]

The third idea came from the peer of the closing record.[^76] The producer of that record had put the lower bounds into closed form and had supplied the estimate of the window loss that was still to be made, a closed form that bounds the upper bound of the first record for all $n$ at once; it had not been able to bound $\mathcal{E}$ and the term of the top row uniformly in $n$.[^457] The peer met this obstacle from another side: of the rows of column $k - 1$ the bound for the step needs only a weighted sum, and not the value in each row.[^458] The peer made the missing upper bounds unnecessary, all but one, in three stages. By this account's reading, that is why the third idea counts as an idea, although it brings none of the estimates row by row that had been missing: it changes what has to be estimated.

The first stage ends the story of the free parameter. Since the second idea the sum of all the $\chi_t(k)$ has a lower bound of its own, and the bound for the step holds for every admissible $\alpha$, zero included. A positive $\alpha$ lowers the coefficient $\pi_n(k) - \alpha \cdot k$ of the loss from one tree of exactly $k$ vertices, and it lowers the sum of the source terms and the window loss as well; and it multiplies three further quantities, each of which then needs an upper bound. The producer had still taken $\alpha$ positive where $k/n \lt 5/8$, and had needed those bounds. Taking $\alpha = 0$ for the free parameter removes all the other terms that would need an upper bound: of the quantities that still need one, only $\mathcal{E}$ and the term of the top row are left.[^77]

The second stage disposes of the term of the top row without bounding it. The entry of the top row $n - 1$ is contained with a larger coefficient in the sum of the source terms and is simply subtracted from it. In the bound the term is $\pi_n(k) \cdot (k-1)/n$ times the probability $b_{n-1,k-1}/p_{n-1}$. The sum of the source terms contains the same probability, in its summand for the row $n - 1$, with the coefficient $(\pi_n(n) - \pi_n(t_0-1))/n$. Here $\pi_n(n) \ge e^{-1}$, while $(k-1) \cdot \pi_n(k)$ is at most 0.0232 at every position concerned. The coefficient is therefore still positive after the subtraction, so that the lower bound already in hand for the sum of the source terms applies with this one coefficient reduced, and no upper bound for the entry is needed.[^78]

In the third stage $\mathcal{E}$, which had resisted estimates row by row, becomes explicit when its rows, the rows of column $k - 1$ in its right half, are summed. For a fixed row the chain is a random walk with Borel jumps conditioned on where it starts. Summing over the rows in which it starts, each with its Borel weight, removes the conditioning and leaves an unconditioned walk with independent Borel jumps, started just above the level and read upwards, for which all expectations are products of values of the Borel generating function. This gives $\mathcal{E} \le 7.72 \cdot (k-1)^{-1/3}$ for every $k \ge 25001$, which are all the levels that occur in the induction. The constant is the one that the review certified, after it had corrected the certificate of the record.[^459]

The proof is not self-contained. Its induction starts at $n = 50001$, and below that it rests on computation. For the right half of every row $20001 \le n \le 50000$ it rests on a record that covers 524,955,000 positions in double-precision arithmetic with a bound on the rounding error proved beforehand, which is not interval arithmetic.[^79] For the rows up to 20000 it rests on the integer-interval certificate of 7 November (section 4.5).

Near the peak, at positions with $1.4 \le (n-k+1)/k^{2/3} \le 10$, the argument uses a computer proof by interval arithmetic of the increase,[^80] in which peer and review between them found and bounded seven defects of rigour. In the remaining range the inequality to be shown is that the certified lower bound for the sum of the source terms minus the window loss is at least the certified upper bound for the loss from one tree of exactly $k$ vertices. The review of the closing record found one defect in each of two large interval certificates behind these bounds, re-ran them corrected, and accepted the solution with the corrected constants. At the positions up to two thirds of the row, $k \le 2n/3$, the ratio of the two sides, which has to be at least 1, is certified as at least 1.0727. The certified ratio is smallest at the centre of the row $n = 50001$. There the ratio of the true values, a figure that the review computed and gives beside the certified one, is about 2.09; the proof does not use it.[^81] At the positions beyond $2n/3$ the same inequality is proved by hand.

The review describes what it accepted as an analytic proof with interval-arithmetic certificates, two of which it had corrected by one line each and run again, resting on published computer proofs, with no independent re-implementation of the certificates and no Lean. Of the proof near the peak on which it depends,[^82] that record's own review says that it is an interval-arithmetic computer proof with corrections made after the fact, not a clean re-run and not Lean.

A second, independent proof of the same range followed at 04:40.[^83] It proves at these positions the inequality $2 \cdot T_2(n,k) \gt B(n,k-1)$ between the count of ties of exactly two cars and the blocked count of section 4.3.

The system started a formalizer on each of the two nodes that had received a solution of their own, within a minute of it: the increase statement W at 01:17 and this two-count inequality at 04:41. The other right-half nodes were proved by relation and received none.

### 4.7 What the user asked for, 8 and 9 November

Between the evening of 8 November and the night of 9 November the user intervened four times: with two texts on the evening of 8 November, and on 9 November with a pause of formalization, a decision on the weight of computation, and the input that names the question. Between the first and the second of these the operator placed five questions on the frontier, and the section tells them and their answers at that place. By this account's reading, the four interventions describe the dissatisfaction from which the later instance would grow (section 7.1).

On 8 November at 18:59 two long texts were submitted.[^84] Both were written for the user by the operator: the first through five versions under the user's corrections, the second, at the user's request, as a record of what those corrections showed about the kind of writing the user prefers.[^403] The files were replaced in place by their final versions by 19:23, before any session received them. The inputs that followed on 9 November were likewise written by the operator, in the third person about the user.

The first text, "What mathematical research is done for", was written after reading the records in order. It observes that the range in which the statement is confirmed had grown while the understanding of why the object behaves as it does had not grown with it. It observes also that when a method stopped, the stopping point was measured precisely and used for the next estimate, while the question why different methods stop at similar places was almost never asked. It declines, in so many words, to add instructions, on the ground that an instruction works only for someone who already understands what it points to, and instead recounts what mathematicians have sought.

The first text is cited by its title in one record[^85] and in three reviews,[^86] each time where a session had rebuilt something already published. Otherwise its effect shows indirectly, in sections of later records and reviews that ask why a result holds and what a failure shows.

On the morning of 9 November, at 08:30, the operator deployed a new facility into the running instance: questions on the frontier, which explorations take up. Five questions drafted from the records were placed.[^87] Four were answered within seventeen hours and the fifth the next afternoon,[^88] each by an exploration whose answer a review accepted with stated limits.

Two of the questions concerned the two counts whose difference is the step, the ties and the blocked maxima of section 4.3. One asked whether blocked mass can be matched to tie mass by a pairing that is local along the chain, that is, one that matches a blocked maximum to a tie whose chain, the chain of section 4.2, ends at nearly the same size.[^89] The answer was that the pairing cannot be local. On the right half and for large $k$ the blocked maxima are concentrated on chains that end at a distance of order 1 above $k$, while the ties are spread over distances of order $k^{2/3}$; a pairing that moves the end of the chain by a distance of order $k^{1/3}$ can therefore carry only a share of order $k^{-1/6}$ of the blocked mass.[^90] The review accepted this as a theorem for large $k$ without explicit constants, and noted that for $k \le 2100$ a local pairing does exist and that the theorem does not cover the rows in which $n - k$ is small against $k$.[^91]

The other asked what the two counts are in the limit process that governs the region of the peak. That process is a standard Brownian motion run in a variable $x$, which measures the deficit $n - k$ in units of $n^{2/3}$. With the motion go its running maximum $R$, the largest value that the motion has taken up to $x$, and the integral $\Lambda$ of $R$ from 0 to $x$.[^92] The answer was that the two counts are two explicit functionals of the limit process: the limit density of the ties of exactly two cars is half the expectation of $R^2 \cdot e^{-\Lambda}$, and that of the blocked maxima is the expectation of $e^{-\Lambda} \cdot dR$ per unit of the variable $x$.[^93] Their balance is the product rule: the expectation of the equation $d(R \cdot e^{-\Lambda}) = e^{-\Lambda} \cdot dR - R^2 \cdot e^{-\Lambda} \cdot dx$ is an identity that had been observed numerically, by which the density of the blocked maxima minus twice that of the ties is the slope of the limit density of the deficit, taken in the same variable $x$.[^94] The review accepted the identification of the two functionals as proved; with it, the convergence of the two counts, on compact ranges of positive $x$, is that of two local limit theorems which the instance had published before, each as a claim that its review accepted.[^460]

Of the other three questions, one asked why several independently measured landmarks fall close together in the rows just above $2k$ of a column, $n = 2k + t \cdot k^{2/3}$, where level $k$ stands just below the centre of the row. The landmarks are values of $t$ between about 2 and 4, measured in double precision: where the step has its peak, where a quantity built on the chain changes sign, where a published lower bound ceases to hold, and where a relative second difference and a ratio of moments have their maxima.[^95] The answer was that two of the landmarks coincide because they are one zero of one function, the change of sign and the failure of the bound, which both lie at $t = 2.034699$ in the limit; that the peak of the step, at $t = 2.051526$, lies close to them by a near coincidence with no identity behind it; and that the others are derived from the same profiles.[^96] The record adds that this means that no binding identity is known, not that none exists,[^97] and the review marks the identification of the common zero as heuristic.[^98]

One asked which property of the left half makes it harder than the right, and whether the device that had closed the right half, the summing over the rows of section 4.6, has a counterpart there. The answer was that the left half differs through large subtrees that are not nested: from $n = 2k$ on, two subtrees with at least $k$ vertices can stand side by side.[^99] Their own contribution to the step is non-negative, outcome by outcome. Their cost is a damping factor on the part that the chain contributes, for which the device of the right half does have a counterpart, and an exact one: each jump of the walk that is longer than $k$ carries the probability that the forest which falls off the path at that jump has $M \le k - 1$.[^100] The review adds that the positivity of the chain's part is an observation only, and that no induction over the rows has been built on this representation.[^101]

And one asked whether the object that decides the step at its thin margin of order $k^{-1/3}$ is the same in every regime where that margin appears: at the centre of the row, in the region of the peak, and at the positions with $k/n$ fixed between 1/2 and 1 or between 1/3 and 1/2.[^102] The answer was that the object at the threshold is the same throughout. The weighted number of subtrees of the threshold size, the $H$ of section 4.4, is an exact sum over the paths that climb from such a subtree through the larger subtrees containing it. On such a path the sizes rise by jumps that follow the Borel distribution, and a subtree passed at height $h$ above $k$ contributes the factor $k/(k+h)$; the record calls the path with these factors a killed Borel renewal bridge.[^103] In the left half a jump longer than $k$ carries the damping factor of the preceding question as well.[^104] The stretch of the bridge just above the threshold does not depend on $n$ or on the regime, and the regimes change only how the bridge is entered from above.[^105] The review takes the sameness of the object as exact, and the account of the entry at the centre and at the positions with $k/n$ fixed as heuristic.[^106]

At 11:46 the user paused all formalization until the research should be finished.[^107] Five formalization pipelines were ended unpublished, those for the central part P_C (section 4.5), the right-half statement W and three others. The formalizer of the central part had been running since 7 November and had compiled the statement for $n \ge 19999$ and for $n \le 1808$. The input says that the criterion for the root is unchanged, a reviewed Lean proof still being required, and that the absence of a formal proof of a node is from then on a consequence of the pause and not a judgment on its informal proof. No formalization was done in this instance again.

At 16:05 the user decided on the weight of computation:[^108] many sessions were spending much of their time on large computations and computer-assisted certification, and the research was to give more weight to thinking about the problem itself. Computation that is really needed and that deepens understanding remained right to do. What was to be avoided was large computation used mainly to fill the remaining range or large gaps one by one.

The operator relayed this to the eleven sessions that had been started before the decision and were still running, and their reports show what they did. The number of sessions allowed at once, eight at the start (section 4.1), had by then been raised to twelve, as the table of section 7.5 records, and twelve sessions were running: the twelfth, a review, had been started after the decision and had received it with its task.[^109] One stopped a batch that was certifying columns one after another, at column 3131, and wrote that adding more columns "produces no new understanding".[^110] After this no record extends the column-by-column certificates beyond column 3131; where later records check a finite range of $k$, they evaluate an inequality in closed form.[^111] One record states that the columns from 3131 to 6666 could be closed by a computer check and that it did not do so because of the decision.[^112]

At 23:34 came the input that names the question.[^113] The user, being briefed, had asked: supposing the statement true, do we know only that it is true, or also why? The operator had answered that the records show where unimodality arises in several places but give no principle showing why the row must be unimodal for all $n$, and had suggested that the thinness of the margins might mean there is no such principle. The user corrected this: that one side wins by a very small margin is no proof that there is no evident reason why it must win.

What the user wants is stated as one thing: to understand why the whole row must rise once and fall once, the whole row and not a half or a region, the split into parts being a device of proof. The research was to focus on this understanding from then on. The standard of review was left unchanged.

From that night almost every record carries a section measuring its result against this question, and almost every review ends by saying that the record gives no principle for the unimodality of the whole row.

### 4.8 The left half: columns, layers and one comparison

The left half took three more days. Since there the subtrees above the level need not be nested, the sessions worked column by column: fix the level $k$ and follow the step $d_{n,k}$ as the row $n$ grows from $2k+1$. The path to the proof can be told as a sequence of reductions of the set of positions $(n,k)$ still open, down to one comparison of two neighbouring entries of a row, $b_{n,k-1} \le b_{n,k}$, in a range of rows that the records call the second layer. None of this was formalized: what the section gives as proved are claims, solutions and relations that reviews of the earlier instance *accepted* on proofs in words, and where a statement rests on a computation or has another standing, the text says so at the statement.

By the morning of 9 November computer-assisted records had closed all rows $n \le 20000$ by two independent interval computations,[^114] all columns $k \le 2000$, a strip of rows just above $2k$, and all rows beyond about $2 \cdot k^{3/2}$.[^115] The columns were closed by the certificates that section 4.10 sets out, the strip by exact rational arithmetic and by certificates in integer arithmetic, and the rows beyond about $2 \cdot k^{3/2}$ by interval arithmetic in double precision whose bounds are widened outward, on an assumed accuracy of the library functions.[^461] The strip is 102 rows wide: the rows $2k + 1$ to $2k + 40$ of a column had been closed by one record and the rows $2k + 41$ to $2k + 102$ by another.[^116] The second of these records says of the width 102 that it is how far its method reached and not a natural dividing line of the problem.[^117] What remained was a region of positions: columns $k \ge 2001$ and rows from $\max(2k+103, 20001)$ to about $2 \cdot k^{3/2}$.

The records that worked on this region cut a row in two ways. The first cut is that of the forest of section 4.2. A tree of the forest is the subtree of a root, a stretch of consecutive spots whose last spot holds a car that arrived later than every car to its right, and the subtree of a spot is the stretch that ends at that spot and begins just after the nearest later-arriving car on its left. The second cut is that of the last-car recurrence of the same section. Call a stretch of consecutive spots, taken with the cars that stand in it, a *block* when these cars park among themselves, that is, when they form a parking function of the length of the stretch with their displacements unchanged. The whole row is a block. The last car of a block divides it at the spot where it stands: the spots on its left are the *left part* of the block and those on its right its *right part*, and each part is a block again, which its own last car divides in turn.[^131]

The two cuts fit together. A tree is a block, and it is the kind of block whose last car stands in its last spot. In any block, the left part together with the spot of the last car is such a tree: it is the subtree of that spot. So a block whose last car stands at its $j$-th spot begins with a tree of $j$ vertices; dividing the right part again and again cuts the block into its trees from left to right, and dividing a left part gives the subtrees inside a tree.[^130] A tree and a block are thus different objects: every tree is a block, and a block is in general a row of several trees. The breakpoints of section 4.2 cut the row into blocks as well: the cars that stand in one of the stretches into which the breakpoints cut the row prefer spots of that stretch and park among themselves, so that every prime piece of the row is a block.

Sizes are counted in two ways, and every size in this section and the next says which. A tree is counted by its vertices, which are its spots, the spot of its root included. A block or a part is counted by its spots, which is its length as a parking function. A tree with $t$ vertices is thus the spot of its root together with a left part of $t - 1$ spots. Its first $t - 1$ cars form a parking function of length $t - 1$, and its last car, which takes the last spot, has $\min(t, \ell+1)$ displacements that keep the maximum at most $\ell$, so that the tree can be arranged in $\min(t, \ell+1) \cdot A_{t-1,\ell}$ ways with $M \le \ell$, as section 4.2 has it.[^128] This is why a tree with $m + 1$ vertices carries the counts of row $m$.

A tree enters the arguments of this section through its own step at the level $k$, which is fixed throughout. Write $\delta_t$ for the number of arrangements of a tree with $t$ vertices whose maximum displacement is exactly $k$, minus the number of those whose maximum displacement is exactly $k - 1$. Then $\delta_t = 0$ for $t \lt k$. A tree of exactly $k$ vertices, the threshold size of section 4.4, has maximum displacement $k - 1$ only when its last car has displacement $k - 1$, and it never has maximum displacement $k$, so that $\delta_k = -k^{k-2}$. For a tree with $m + 1$ vertices, where $m \ge k$,

$$\delta_{m+1} = (k+1) \cdot b_{m,k} - (k-1) \cdot b_{m,k-1} = k \cdot d_{m,k} + b_{m,k} + b_{m,k-1},$$

and the first term of the right side is $k$ times the step of row $m$, the row of the tree's left part.[^462] A tree with $t$ vertices has $t^{t-1}$ arrangements in all, as many as there are rooted labelled trees on $t$ vertices,[^129] and $\delta_t$ divided by that number is the $\chi_t(k)$ of section 4.6: $\chi_t(k) = \delta_t/t^{t-1}$.[^463]

Two ranges of rows of a column have names. The rows $k \le m \le 2k$ are what the records call the *first layer* of column $k$. They are the rows in which level $k$ lies in the right half or, in row $2k$, at the centre of an even row (section 2.1), and in all of them the signs were known by then. The rows $2k \lt t \le 3k$ are the *second layer*.[^464] In the forest, the trees with $k + 1$ to $2k + 1$ vertices are the trees whose left part has a number of spots in the first layer.

A record of 08:07[^118] put the column into a form in which rows can be treated one at a time. It applies the division of a block at its last car to a parking function of length $t$, that is, to row $t$. Write $\rho_t = d_{t,k}/A_{t,k-1}$ for the normalized step of row $t$, and $P_t(j)$ for the probability that the last car stands at the $j$-th spot in a uniformly random parking function of length $t$ with $M \le k - 1$; by the last-car recurrence of section 4.2 it is $P_t(j) = \min(j,k) \cdot \binom{t-1}{j-1} \cdot A_{j-1,k-1} \cdot A_{t-j,k-1}/A_{t,k-1}$. With the last car at the $j$-th spot the left part has $j - 1$ spots and the right part $t - j$, and the last-car recurrence becomes

$$\rho_t = \mathrm{sr}\mathrm c_k(t) + \sum_{j=1}^{t} P_t(j) \cdot (\rho_{j-1} + \rho_{t-j}).$$

The sum is the average, over the position of the last car, of $\rho_a + \rho_b$ for the two parts, of $a$ and $b$ spots.[^465] The term $\mathrm{sr}\mathrm c_k(t)$ is the *source term* of this recurrence: as in section 4.6, the word names the term in which no step occurs. It is explicit, and the rows below $t$ enter it through their entries, which are not negative, and not through their steps.[^466] Its only negative contribution comes from a last car standing exactly at the $k$-th spot of its block, the stretch of $t$ spots that this car divides, so that the part to its left has $k - 1$ spots.[^119] That part and the spot of the last car are a tree of exactly $k$ vertices.

Now keep, of the steps in the sum, only those of the parts whose number of spots lies between $k$ and $2k$, left parts and right parts alike and each with the weight that it has in the sum, and collect them with the source term. For a row $t \gt 2k$ the sum so made is what the record writes $(\star)(t)$:

$$(\star)(t) = \mathrm{sr}\mathrm c_k(t) + \sum_{j=k+1}^{2k+1} P_t(j) \cdot \rho_{j-1} + \sum_{j=t-2k}^{t-k} P_t(j) \cdot \rho_{t-j}.$$

The first sum runs over the left parts with $k$ to $2k$ spots and the second over the right parts with $k$ to $2k$ spots.[^467] The remaining parts have more than $2k$ spots and fewer than $t$, or fewer than $k$, and the latter have step 0. Hence, by induction up the column: if $(\star)(t) \ge 0$ for all rows $t$ with $2k \lt t \le n$ other than those in which the step $d_{t,k}$ is already known to be non-negative for another reason, then $d_{n,k} \ge 0$.[^120] The record calls this an absorption induction, because rows closed by other means are absorbed into it and need not satisfy $(\star)$. The statement $(\star)(t) \ge 0$ concerns one row $t$ at a time, and the only steps that enter it are those of the first layer of the same column.

On 9 November the statement $(\star)$ was *certified by computation* for the columns 2001 to 3130, in double precision with error bounds fixed beforehand, which is not interval arithmetic.[^121] (The test on which the certificate rests passes at column 3130 by a margin of $2.4 \cdot 10^{-4}$ and fails at column 3131.) A second certificate, in the same arithmetic, of a moment inequality that implies the comparison $b_{n,k-1} \le b_{n,k}$, reached column 3131 that afternoon, with five sample columns up to 20000, and was stopped there by the user's decision on computation (section 4.7).[^404]

For the columns from 3131 on, a record of the evening treated the rows above $3k$ and the second layer separately.[^122] In a row $t \ge 3k + 1$, whenever one of the two parts has a number of spots in the first layer, the last car of the block stands beyond the block's $k$-th spot. The factor $\min(j, k)$ with which a last car at the $j$-th spot enters the last-car recurrence of section 4.2 is then the constant $k$. With it the contribution to $(\star)(t)$ of a part whose number $m$ of spots lies in the first layer, its step together with the piece of the source term that belongs to it, becomes $\delta_{m+1}$, divided by $e^m \cdot m!$, times a positive weight that belongs to the other part and does not decrease with $m$.[^123] The divisor $e^m \cdot m!$ is the normalization in which the record writes the counts of a row $m$: with every $A_{m,k-1}$ divided by it, the probability $P_t(j)$ is a factor that depends on $t$ alone, times $\min(j,k)$, times the normalized count of each of the two parts.[^468] For a left part this is the contribution of a tree, since the part and the spot of the last car are a tree with $m + 1$ vertices. A right part of $m$ spots enters with the same expression. By this account's reading, that is because the weight that the recurrence gives to a division, once its factor is the constant $k$, treats the two parts alike.

So $(\star)(t) \gt 0$ follows from two statements. One is that the source term is larger than the sum of the pieces of it that belong to the parts in the first layer, in every such row, which the record proved for all $k \ge 3131$; that proof rests in part on a certificate in double precision of an earlier record.[^469] The other is that these contributions, summed over the first layer with such weights, are non-negative. By a summation by parts it is enough for this that every tail sum of the unweighted contributions is non-negative, which is an inequality on the first layer alone, in which $t$ no longer occurs, and which the record left open:

$$\sum_{m=j}^{2k} \delta_{m+1}/(e^m \cdot m!) \ge 0 \quad \text{for all } k \le j \le 2k.$$

It is called the *first-layer inequality* from here on. The review made it a node, for all $k \ge 3131$. It was still open when the root was proved, and section 4.9 tells how it was reached on the morning after.

In the second layer the argument does not work, because there the last car of a block can stand at or before its $k$-th spot with a part to its right that has a number of spots in the first layer. The record located the real cancellation there, in a strip of rows just beside $2k$; its review counts this diagnosis of the second layer as measured and heuristic.[^470]

The columns 3131 to 6666 need only the rows above $3k$: for them $3k \lt 20001$, so their rows up to $3k$ lie inside the range $n \le 20000$ already certified. Apart from column 3131 and the sample column 5000 of the second certificate, no record was found that computes them column by column, and the proof does not use those two computations either. All these columns were closed on 11 November, when a weaker version of the first-layer inequality, which suffices for the rows above $3k$, was accepted for all $k \ge 3131$, as is told later in this section.

In the night of 10 November the pieces already published were put together into a first statement about the root itself, though not the one wanted: every row is unimodal for all $n$ beyond some $N_0$ that is not explicit.[^124] The statement was published at 00:25, and a second record, published at 00:57, had found it independently. Its review remarks that the ingredients had been in print since the evening before and that what the record itself contributed was their assembly. Under a heading on the user's question it remarks that this eventual validity is a patching together of results that differ from regime to regime and does not show by one principle why a row rises once and falls once. The second of the two records states what had become of the left half: it "now remains in one form only: explicit constants".

On 10 November at 07:41 a record gave the second layer its working form.[^125] It works with the trees of the forest. For $n \le 3k - 1$ at most two trees can have $k$ or more vertices, and the step is then exactly a sum of five pieces,

$$d_{n,k} = -\Theta_n + \mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n + \mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n + \mathrm{K}\mathrm D_n + \mathrm{PAI}\mathrm R_n,$$

where each term counts parking functions of length $n$ according to the one or two trees that reach the level. The names of the five pieces are the record's own notation, and it does not expand the abbreviations.[^471]

The first four pieces belong to one tree. Together they are the sum, over the number $t$ of vertices of a tree, of its step $\delta_t$ times the number $\binom{n}{t} \cdot A_{n-t,k-1}$ of ways to choose its cars and a parking function with $M \le k - 1$ for the other spots.[^472] The four are this sum taken apart by the size of the tree, and the fifth belongs to two trees.

- $\Theta_n$, the *threshold loss*, is the term $t = k$ with its sign reversed: $\Theta_n = \binom{n}{k} \cdot k^{k-2} \cdot A_{n-k,k-1}$ is the loss from a tree with exactly $k$ vertices, the threshold size of section 4.4, whose last car is at displacement $k - 1$ and cannot move further.
- $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n$ is the sum of the terms $k + 1 \le t \le 2k + 1$: the net contribution of one tree with $k + 1$ to $2k + 1$ vertices.
- $\mathrm{K}\mathrm D_n$ and $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n$ share the terms $t \ge 2k + 2$ between them. A tree with $m + 1 \ge 2k + 2$ vertices contributes $\delta_{m+1}$, times the number of ways to choose and complete it, and of the second right side of the formula for $\delta_{m+1}$ above, $k \cdot d_{m,k} + b_{m,k} + b_{m,k-1}$, the first term goes to the one piece and the sum of the other two to the other. $\mathrm{K}\mathrm D_n$ is the sum of the terms $k \cdot d_{m,k}$, which carry the step of row $m$, the row of the tree's left part. $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n$ is the sum of the terms $b_{m,k} + b_{m,k-1}$, and the record calls it the *stem gain* of the large tree.
- $\mathrm{PAI}\mathrm R_n \ge 0$, the *pair term*, is the contribution of two trees that both reach the level, both with maximum displacement exactly $k$ or both with exactly $k - 1$.

The record does not say what the stem is. A record of the next day splits $\delta_{m+1}$ in a neighbouring way, into two summands, and says what they are: one, which it calls the stem term, is the share of the root of the tree, whose car can take two more values of the displacement when the maximum rises by one level, and the other is what the tree inherits from the step of the forest below its root, that is, of its left part.[^473]

Of the five, $\Theta$ is the only pure loss. The piece $\mathrm{K}\mathrm D_n$ is a combination with positive coefficients of the steps of the same column in the rows $2k + 1, \ldots, n - 1$, so it is non-negative by induction up the column; and $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge 0$ follows by a summation by parts from the first-layer inequality. Under that hypothesis the second layer is therefore reduced to a contest without cancellation, $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n \ge \Theta_n$: does the stem gain of large trees pay for one threshold tree?

For $k \to \infty$ the record finds the limit of the ratio $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n/\Theta_n$ on three ranges of rows: where the distance of the row from the start of the layer is fixed, where it is a fixed multiple of $k^{2/3}$, and where it is a fixed fraction of $k$. On each of the three the limit is part of a claim that the review accepted, as an informal conclusion from published results that are themselves informal; between the ranges, and in the last rows of the layer, the record did not prove that the limits hold uniformly, and what is said of those rows is heuristic, as the record marks it.[^474] At a fixed distance $\nu = n - 2k$ from the start of the layer the limit is $2 \cdot \Pr(X \lt \nu)$ with $X$ a Borel variable, about 1.84 in the first open row $n = 2k + 103$ of a column; further up the layer it rises to about 4, and it falls back towards 2 at the end of the layer. In that first row the ratio *observed in computed rows*, in double precision, is 3.03, 2.40 and 2.14 for $k = 1600$, 6400 and 25600.

The same morning, in two records that were not on the path of the proof, the instance proved a reduction of the kind that the later instance would come to anew and put on its frontier, and set it aside.

The first of the two was an exploration that held the question of the root as a whole, published at 08:23.[^132] It noted that, if no step is zero, all rows are unimodal with a peak that never moves back as $n$ grows exactly when every column of steps changes sign once, from negative to positive, at a crossing row that never decreases with $k$. It also *observed*, in exact rows computed up to $n = 170$, that the signs lie so, the peak moving by zero or one from each row to the next.

The second, published at 12:00,[^133] had begun from the wish to prove the single change of sign in each column by a soft argument, and its peer stated and proved the reduction, which this account calls *the reduction of 10 November*. The reviews of the two records accepted it as a claim of each, and it was never a node. Let $N_k$ be the first row $n \ge k$ with $d_{n,k} \ge 0$. If every column, once non-negative, stays non-negative in all later rows, and $N_k$ is non-decreasing in $k$, then every row is unimodal; this implication makes no assumption on zeros.[^405] Conversely, if no two neighbouring entries of a row are equal, if all rows are unimodal, and if the peak never moves back, then both column properties hold. In that case the peak moves by at most one from each row to the next exactly when $N_k$ is strictly increasing, which is the same as the rule that a negative step stays negative along its diagonal. In the later instance a reduction of this kind would be one of the three relations through which the root followed in the informal proof; the formal proof is the Lean form of the proof of another of the three and uses, of this one, the statement on neighbouring columns. Section 6.2 tells how the two reductions correspond, and section 6.9 how the proofs of the root stand to them.

The soft argument that the record had hoped for failed, and the record shows why. Its starting point is an exact equation for one column: the step of row $n$ equals a source term $\phi_n$ plus a combination $\sum_{m \lt n} K(n,m) \cdot d_{m,k}$ of the steps of the lower rows with positive coefficients $K(n,m) = k \cdot \binom{n}{m+1} \cdot A_{n-m-1,k-1}$. The source term of this equation is the share of the step that is left when the whole recursion over the rows has been put into the combination. The equation is made of the steps of single trees. Of the step $\delta_{m+1} = k \cdot d_{m,k} + b_{m,k} + b_{m,k-1}$ of a tree with $m + 1$ vertices, the term $k \cdot d_{m,k}$ goes into the combination, with the number of ways to choose the tree and a parking function with $M \le k - 1$ for the other spots as its coefficient. Everything else is the source term: the terms $b_{m,k} + b_{m,k-1}$ of the trees with more than $k$ vertices, the loss from a tree of exactly $k$ vertices, and a remainder that is not negative.[^475] In every column that was computed, the source term $\phi_n$ is negative in the first rows of the column, about $\sqrt{k}/2$ of them, and positive afterwards. Let $\Gamma$ be the matrix that solves the equation, $d = \Gamma \cdot \phi$; $\Gamma(n,m)$ is the total weight of all ways of climbing from row $m$ to row $n$ in several jumps.

If $K$ or $\Gamma$ were totally positive of order 2, that is, if all their $2\times 2$ minors were non-negative, a single change of sign of $\phi$ would force a single change of sign of $d$. The peer proved that neither is. For $\Gamma$ the reason is structural: a climb from $m$ to $n'$ either passes through an intermediate row $n$ or jumps over it, so $\Gamma(n',m) \gt \Gamma(n',n) \cdot \Gamma(n,m)$, which is the reverse of what total positivity requires, and a source term with one change of sign can be written down whose solution has two. For $K$ the reason is specific to the problem: for jumps of length up to $k + 1$, and up to factors that do not affect the argument, $K$ as a function of the length $n - m$ of a jump is the Borel law, which is log-convex because of its heavy tail, so that a late row gives relatively more weight to the earliest rows of the column, exactly where the source term is negative.

The record concludes that the column statement cannot be had from the sign pattern of the source term and the positivity properties of the kernel $K$ of the equation alone: the size of the positive source term has to be used. It observed that the column statement asks more than the left half that was then being proved, since it also requires that in the right half the peak does not move back as $n$ grows, which was "not in the published results"; and it did not propose it as a node.[^415] The reduction remained a supported claim in two records, and the instance went on with its layers.

The first-layer inequality was the hypothesis from which the rows above $3k$ and, in the second layer, $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge 0$ were to follow. Over 10 and 11 November it was weakened by changing the weight of row $m$ in the tail sum, and from here on four versions have to be kept apart. Each was posed as a node and says, for every $k \ge 3131$ and every $j$ with $k \le j \le 2k$, that the sum of the $\delta_{m+1}$ over the rows $j \le m \le 2k$, each with its weight, is non-negative.[^476] They differ in the weight of row $m$ only.

- The first-layer inequality itself has the weight $1/(e^m \cdot m!)$.
- The version *with probability weights* has the weight $1/p_m$. Its summand is $(k+1) \cdot \Pr_m(M = k) - (k-1) \cdot \Pr_m(M = k-1)$, where $\Pr_m$ is the probability for a uniformly random parking function of length $m$: a statement in probabilities for row $m$.
- The version *with the critical tilt* has the weight $x_k^m/m!$, where $x_k = (k/(k+1))^{k-1}$ is the *critical tilt*.
- The *flat* version, as the records name it, has the weight $1/(m+1)^m$, the reciprocal of the number of arrangements of a tree with $m + 1$ vertices, so that its summands are the $\chi_{m+1}(k)$ of section 4.6.

The first-layer inequality implies the version with probability weights, and that version implies the one with the critical tilt. Each of these two implications is a summation by parts, which applies because the quotient of the weights of the weaker version and of the stronger does not decrease with $m$.[^406] The first-layer inequality also implies the flat version, again by a summation by parts.[^477] Each of the first three versions suffices for the rows above $3k$.[^478] The reduction of the second layer needs $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge 0$, which the first-layer inequality gives and the flat version gives as well.

The version that was accepted on 11 November at 03:44, by relation, is the one with probability weights, for all $k \ge 3131$.[^139] Its case $j = k$ says that $\sum_{m=k}^{2k} [(k+1) \cdot \Pr_m(M = k) - (k-1) \cdot \Pr_m(M = k-1)]$ is positive; the record of 03:44 shows this sum to exceed 0.082, and a relation accepted three and a half hours before leads from that case, together with nodes that were proved by then, to every $j$. The proof of the case $j = k$ is computer-assisted: among its inputs are the integer certificate of the rows $n \le 20000$ and an upper sum, computed in double precision, for a theorem of the record that had brought the relation. The version with probability weights has a larger margin than the first-layer inequality. The first-layer inequality and the flat version were not proved at that time.

The tool behind several monotonicity lemmas of these days is an inequality proved on 10 November at 02:44 for every $k \ge 2$, by an elementary argument, one part of which is proved by analysis from $k = 43$ on and by an exact computation in rationals for the columns 2 to 42:[^140]

$$A_{N+1,k-1} \le ((k+1)/k)^{k-1} \cdot (N+1) \cdot A_{N,k-1}.$$

The inequality says that the cumulative counts of a column, tilted by $x_k$, do not increase with the row.[^141] It is an equality at $N = k - 1$, so that $x_k$ is the largest tilt for which this holds in every row.[^479] Later sections call it the *growth inequality*. It returns: it is one of the three conditions of the critical-tilt propagation, a theorem on the left half of a column that was handed to the later instance (section 5.2), and the later instance's node (CB) is its counterpart for the exact counts $b$ (section 6.4).

The record of 03:44 on 11 November also made the root equivalent, on the frontier, to a single node: the plain comparison $b_{n,k-1} \le b_{n,k}$ in the second layer, whose open content was $k \ge 6667$ and $\max(2k+103, 20001) \le n \le 3k-1$ (for $k = 6667$ the range is empty, so in effect $k \ge 6668$). This node is the *second-layer comparison*. Above $3k$ the increase now propagated up the column, by the version of the first-layer inequality with probability weights, just accepted.

On a part of that range, the *band* $n - 2k \le 0.48 \cdot k$, the inequality "stem gain at least threshold loss", $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n \ge \Theta_n$, had been proved at 01:26 by a computer-assisted record,[^142] which closes the second-layer comparison there once a first-layer hypothesis is available. The hypothesis that the record names is the first-layer inequality, the version with the weights $1/(e^m \cdot m!)$, for the column concerned.[^143] The certificates of that record run in double precision, and one point of its proof had been accepted by its review on numerical probes; that point was proved by hand on 12 November (section 4.11). What had to be shown in the rows of the second layer beyond the band was the last gap.

### 4.9 The root is proved informally

The last gap was closed on 11 November between 13:42 and 16:14 by three records. The first weakened what had to be shown beyond the band to "stem gain plus pair gain is at least the threshold loss", $\mathrm{BV} + \mathrm{PAIR} \ge \Theta$, and reduced that to an inequality on the pair term alone.[^144] The second, at 15:04, proved the band without any first-layer hypothesis and had the relation from the weakened statement to the second-layer comparison accepted.[^145] The third proved the pair inequality.[^146] Their reviews accepted what the three records proved, as claims, as relations and, for the pair inequality, as the solution of a node, and with the third the root was accepted, by relation; none of this was formalized, and the computer-assisted statements on which the proofs rest are named where they enter.

The first and the third belong together, since the third proved the inequality to which the first had reduced its statement,[^480] and they are told first. The pair inequality concerns what is left of the threshold loss once the stem gain has paid for a share of it. The threshold loss $\Theta_n$ counts the parking functions of length $n$ with a marked tree of exactly $k$ vertices whose last car is at displacement $k - 1$, the other $n - k$ spots having $M \le k - 1$. The first of the three records had shown that the share of this loss in which the other spots contain a tree with more than $k + 103$ vertices is paid for by the stem gain. What is left is the share in which the rest of the forest is cut into trees of at most $k + 103$ vertices, which happens with probability of order $k^{-1/2}$. Write $A^{\diamond}(N)$ for the number of parking functions of length $N$ with $M \le k - 1$ and no tree with more than $k + 103$ vertices. The pair inequality says that for $k \ge 6668$ and $0.48 \cdot k \lt n - 2k \le k - 1$ the pair term alone exceeds this remaining share:

$$\mathrm{PAI}\mathrm R_n \ge 1.149 \cdot \binom{n}{k} \cdot k^{k-2} \cdot A^{\diamond}(n-k).$$

A tree is said to stand exactly at the level when the maximum displacement of its cars is exactly $k$, or exactly $k - 1$; the pair term counts the pairs in which both trees are of the one kind and those in which both are of the other.[^152] The proof keeps, of all pairs, only those in which both trees have between $k + 1$ and about $k + 4 \cdot k^{2/3}$ vertices. It bounds from below the probability that such a tree stands exactly at the level, by a computer-assisted theorem of an earlier record, certified in double precision,[^151] and it bounds from above the probability of a finely cut forest, by sums of Borel probabilities. That theorem is one of the certificates of the record of the band (section 4.8), and the point of that record's proof which its review had accepted on numerical probes lies under it as well: until that point was proved by hand on 12 November (section 4.11), the pair inequality rested on it too.[^481] The ratio of the two bounds is monotone in $k$ in the favourable direction, so that one function of one variable, evaluated at $k = 6668$, settles all $k$; its minimum is 1.149. The variable that remains is $(n - 2k)/k$, the place of the row in the second layer.[^153]

The second record, published between the two, removed a hypothesis on the first layer. The reduction of the second layer to $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n \ge \Theta_n$ (section 4.8) takes $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge 0$ from the first-layer inequality or from the flat version. Neither was proved, the version accepted at 03:44 being the one with probability weights, and the relation by which the first record led on to the second-layer comparison still had the flat version among its premises.[^147] The second record did without the premise. It summed $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n$ by parts, showed from published results that every tail sum but the first is non-negative, and so obtained a lower bound for $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n$ whose positive term is a multiple of the sum that the record of 03:44 had bounded from below, the case $j = k$ of the version with probability weights.[^148] Beyond the band this bound gives $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge 0$. In the band it is non-negative except in a front stretch of rows, and there the margin with which $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n \ge \Theta_n$ holds in the front of the band makes up for it.[^149] For the columns below 38887 this leaves the first rows of the stretch open; for them the record first sharpened the bound, by an upper bound on the tail sums that it drew from a record published during the session, and the margin then suffices; in these columns the inequality that closes the argument is checked in double precision, which is not interval arithmetic.[^150]

At 16:14:19 the third record was published,[^154] and the frontier, applying relations that earlier reviews had accepted, changed the root and ten further nodes to informal_proved. The derivation it followed runs as follows.

  pair inequality and the band $\Rightarrow$ the statement for the rows beyond the band $\Rightarrow$ second-layer comparison;

  with the version of the first-layer inequality with probability weights, of 03:44, the positivity of the source term $\mathrm{sr}\mathrm c_k$ of section 4.8, proved on 9 November,[^416] and the certified columns 2001 to 3130 $\Rightarrow$ the left increase statement of 7 November (section 4.5) $\Rightarrow$ P_L $\Rightarrow$ P_X;

  and P_C, P_R, P_X, P_L $\Rightarrow$ the root.

A second listed derivation goes through Q, the node of section 4.2.

The review of the closing record says that the change of the root's state is the mechanical consequence of relations accepted before, that it did not review those relations again, and that the state "does not reach the user's final criterion of mathematical resolution", a Lean theorem that has passed independent review.[^155]

Nothing in the instance marked the moment. No input followed, the runner was not restarted, and the sessions went on.

Three more derivations followed within fourteen hours, all sharing the right half and the certificates. One, of 18:54, reaches the one-row statement $(\star)$ itself in the second layer, in the columns $k \ge 6667$ and the rows of the second layer from $\max(2k+103, 20001)$ on, the band and the rows beyond it together; what the record proves is a statement on tail sums over the first layer, and an accepted relation leads from it and the inequality of the band to $(\star)$ in those rows.[^156] One, of 22:31, closes the rows beyond the band differently, by showing that there the trees with $k + 1$ to $2k + 1$ vertices alone pay for the threshold tree, $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge \Theta_n$.[^157] The last, completed at 05:42 the next morning after a refused part of its proof was repaired, proves the one premise still open in a sequence of accepted relations that ends in the first-layer inequality, the version with the weights $1/(e^m \cdot m!)$, which the route of 16:14 had avoided.[^158] With that premise the first-layer inequality was accepted, by relation, and at the last commit the frontier has it and the flat version as informally proved.[^482] What each of the three records proved was accepted by its review as the solution of a node, on a proof in words that was not formalized. The computations of their own on which these proofs rest are checks in double precision, which are not interval arithmetic, in the first; tables certified in interval arithmetic and sums in double precision in the second; and a covering in interval arithmetic at the one column $k = 3131$ in the third.[^483]

What the last of the three proves is a lower bound, in every column $k \ge 3131$ and for the deficits $r$ with $k/10 \lt r \le k$, for the source term of a recurrence that runs over the deficits, the term in which none of the quantities at the smaller deficits occurs; the note says what the recurrence is.[^159] The first-layer inequality, at which the sequence ends, is the version that gives $\mathrm{F}\mathrm L_n \ge 0$ in the reduction of the second layer to $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n \ge \Theta_n$ (section 4.8). The record itself says that the sequence is not a premise of the root, which had already been informally proved through other sequences, but a route independent of it.[^160]

How many routes led to the root depends on what is counted: one route closed the root at 16:14, further derivations followed as just told, and the frontier's own entry for the root lists two derivations, the four-part split and Q, and did not change after 16:14. The sources count them differently: a memory note (section 4.1) says that the root was proved "by two paths",[^161] and a working document from the writing of the manuscript (section 5.1) speaks of three.[^162][^163]

### 4.10 What kind of proof this was

The sources describe the proof precisely, and the description matters for what followed.

It is an informal proof spread over several dozen records, each link accepted by a review session, with no formal proof of any link on the path. Every row with $n \le 20000$ is covered by certified finite computation. The general argument is used only for $n \ge 20001$, and it is itself computer-assisted at many points. The right half rests on a certified base up to $n = 50000$ and on interval-arithmetic proofs of explicit inequalities. The left half rests on certificates for the columns up to 3130: an exact certificate with exact rows up to column 120, integer interval arithmetic for the columns 121 to 500, and double precision with error bounds fixed beforehand above them. Above the columns of these certificates it rests on statements proved for all larger columns at once. These statements themselves rest in part on the certified rows $n \le 20000$ and on evaluations in double-precision arithmetic that the reviews recomputed with independent code but that are not interval-certified. Margins are sometimes thin where the truth is not: in the right half, a certified ratio of 1.07 against a true ratio of 2.09 at the centre of the row $n = 50001$. Several certificates were found defective as first run and were repaired by a peer or a review; in one case none of the 443 units into which a certificate was divided had verified its hypothesis, because a bound was coded $k$ times too small.[^164] In every such case the theorem survived the repair.

And by the judgment of its own reviews, repeated in each closing record, it gives no reason why a row is unimodal. It shows, region by region of the $(n,k)$ plane, that one positive quantity exceeds one negative quantity, by a different argument in each region; the manuscript of section 5.1 lists them in its own map of the proof.[^165]

### 4.11 The last two days

After the root was closed the instance divided its sessions between alternative routes, side nodes, and explorations of the user's question. The explorations produced negative results that sharpen the question.

The row is not log-concave, and no fixed exponent $\beta$ makes $b^{-\beta}$ convex on every row either, the exponent needed growing without bound.[^166] The record of the second result reads it as ruling out one form that an answer to the user's question could take: a uniform regularity of second order, which the convexity of a power of the row would be.[^484] The table of probabilities $\Pr(M = k)$ for length $n$ was observed to be unimodal along rows, columns and diagonals of constant deficit, exactly for $n \le 140$ and in the limit; but in the limit it is not unimodal along every line, slices in two narrow bands of slopes having a valley that no computed row is large enough to show.[^167] The record of the table concludes that the unimodality of a row cannot be explained as a consequence of a table with a single peak.[^485]

A record of 13 November at 04:33[^168] compared the true row with a simplified model, in which the numbers of subtrees of each size are independent with their true means, so as to separate what these means alone explain of the unimodality from what depends on the random forest.[^486] This *simplified model of independent subtree sizes* is unimodal for every $n \ge 2$ and log-concave for every $n \ge 3$; both are claims that the review of the record accepted. One inequality proves them for $n \ge 299$, and the rows up to $n = 400$ were decided in exact rational arithmetic with interval arithmetic in directed rounding, which the review repeated with its own code. The true row keeps unimodality and loses the stronger shape properties: log-concavity of the row from $n = 80$, of the cumulative counts from $n = 780$, and two monotonicity properties, whose large failures begin at $n = 494$ and 499; both also fail in some of the rows $n \le 10$. What the record observes, as its review corrected it, is where these properties fail: in the exact rows up to $n = 500$ every failure in a row $n \ge 11$ lies between about $0.49 \cdot n$ and $0.70 \cdot n$, on the rising part of the row to the left of its peak, while on the falling part the true row behaves like the model; of the failures in the rows $n \le 10$ some lie after the peak. The record reads this as showing that the unimodality of the true row is not explained by what makes the model unimodal, a single quantity that decreases along the whole row and passes zero once.

Some statements of single-crossing type were posed, for instance that a certain difference of tree masses, taken between two neighbouring positions of a diagonal of constant deficit, changes sign once as its index $m$ runs from $k$ to $2k$;[^169] they were stated for the first layer of a column only, and none was proved beyond a partial range. A tree mass is there the probability that the largest mark of a uniform random rooted tree with a given number of vertices has a given value, and the difference is that between the tree with $m + 1$ vertices at the value $k$ and the tree with $m$ vertices at the value $k - 1$, which have the same deficit.[^170] The reduction of 10 November to single-crossing columns (section 4.8) was not taken up again. The later instance would come to a reduction of this kind anew, and the root would follow through it, among other relations, in that instance's informal proof (sections 6.2 and 6.9).

Two inputs were passed on by the operator in these days, both at the user's request.

One, on 12 November at 04:54, was offered as material for the sessions' judgment and not as an instruction. An AI session outside the instance, working without the records, had re-derived how the row changes when one car is added, in terms of the proportion $\theta_n(k)$ of the mass at level $k$ that moves up one level when the length grows from $n$ to $n + 1$. The operator checked the report in exact arithmetic for $n \le 7$ and against the records. The operator found the frame of the report published already, and also the identity that the outside session had taken to be new, a bijection that removes a leaf from a forest, together with the outline of a split built on it. The split is this: $\theta_n(k)$ is exactly the sum of two terms, the value it would have if a forest grew by attaching a new leaf at a uniformly chosen place, and a correction. What the input added to the record was a reading of the correction, as a covariance between the number of leaves of a forest and its weight in the count $A_{n+1,k}$. The correction was positive in every case computed, and its positivity in general was unproved.

At least six records[^171] and three questions[^172] followed the lead of this input in the next ten hours. What this work established about the correction is partial. Its positivity was proved for $k \ge n/2 - 1$, from the fact, which an earlier record has as an informal theorem, that the probability of $M \le k$ does not increase with the length; in the rest of the left half it stayed open, and the record that took up the question placed on it did not resolve it.[^173] One record compared the true rows with rows grown by the first term alone, which in its computations are unimodal as well, with the peak in another place, and read the correction as moving the peak and not as making it; its review calls this a reading that rests on finite computation and on a comparison of models.[^174]

The other, at 14:15, came from the writing of the manuscript (section 5.1): a session writing up the proof had found that one point on which the routes to the root depend had been accepted by its review on numerical probes alone. It concerned a table that the three certificates of the record of the band read (section 4.8), among them the certificate of the inequality of the band itself, $\mathrm{B}\mathrm V_n \ge \Theta_n$ in the rows with $n - 2k \le 0.48 \cdot k$, and the theorem on which the proof of the pair inequality rests (section 4.9); what had no proof were two monotonicities behind the table.[^487] The first is the monotonicity of a bound in the variable along which it is tabulated, and the second is that of six elementary pieces in the parameter $k$. Without the first, the table is not a lower bound; without the second, the evaluation at the endpoints of a partition is not one. The input, headed as a request of the user, asked the instance to prove both by hand before anything else, allowing a rigorous computation over a narrow range only where a hand proof failed.

A complete proof by hand was published 1 hour 57 minutes later, at 16:12,[^175] and five more derivations followed from other sessions, each called independent in its record or its review. The state of the root did not change. The memory note that records the episode draws the lesson that "supported", in a review's verdict, means that the review accepted the conclusion and not that a proof exists.[^176]

On the evening of 12 November, at about 19:20, the user ordered a stop, and the session that was writing the manuscript sent the runner its stop command.[^177] The instance did stop: the runner stayed alive but launched nothing after 19:17, the ten sessions then running were left to finish their turn, and nothing was published between 18:57 and 02:59. Ten was the number of sessions that the settings then allowed at once, as the table of section 7.5 records.[^178]

At 00:58 on 13 November the turns still pending were cancelled, and a minute later the user restarted the machine.[^179] The runner was started again at 02:36:54 with six sessions at a time.[^180] No source that was read says who started it or on whose word. The working log of the session that had stopped it records no restart.

The last record was published at 06:12.[^181] At 06:35 the output of five of the six running sessions stops within twelve seconds, with no record of a termination and no stop file; the sixth had written nothing since 06:00. Six pipelines were in progress. The system log shows no shutdown or restart of the machine at that time or in the two days after, so the machine did not go down. Beyond that, no source says why the instance stopped.

At its last commit the root was informal_proved, fifty-five nodes were unresolved, none of them needed for the root, and seven questions were open.

## 5. Between the instances, 12 to 20 November

The later instance was not started from the earlier instance's records. Nothing of the earlier instance was copied into it, no standing input of the later instance names the earlier one, and its published records do not mention it. What its sessions were given, as files they might read, was a manuscript of the earlier proof and documents written from that manuscript, and, from its second day, a paper whose making had drawn on the earlier instance's records as an inventory of tools (section 5.3).

What connected the two instances was a line of documents written outside either of them, each made from the one before, and the content that reached the later instance was what survived that line. There were two such lines, and they met inside the later instance on its second day. This section follows them, because what was and was not carried over explains a good deal of what the later instance did.[^182]

### 5.1 The manuscript

By 11 November, while the earlier instance was still running, the user had had an expository text written from its records.[^183] When the root was informally proved, the user asked, early on 12 November, for a new text that would convey the proof as a proof, leaving out what was incidental.[^407] This new text is what this account calls the *manuscript*. After reading its first edition the user set a rule for the second: everything the text uses is to be proved in the text, or cited as proved by an actual mathematician elsewhere, and what is not proved is not to be mentioned.[^184] It was while tracing proofs under this rule that the writing session found the unproved monotonicity which the earlier instance was then asked to supply (section 4.11).

The second edition was first assembled in the night of 13 November, between 01:09 and 03:46, about two and a half hours before the earlier instance's last publication.[^408] It was then revised through twenty-five rounds of independent review and repair by subagents until 18 November, when the user ended the rounds.[^185] Its final state is a text of 1.6 megabytes in ten chapters with appendices.[^186]

Each statement in the manuscript carries the identifiers of the earlier instance's records on which it rests.[^488] Of the routes by which the earlier instance had closed the root, the manuscript keeps one.[^187] The manuscript also redid large parts of the computations itself after the instance had stopped: the certificates of the left columns $2 \le k \le 500$, those of the columns up to 120 by running a copy of the instance's own code and those of the columns 121 to 500 by a new implementation; and the whole right half for $n \le 50000$, by a newly written program in interval arithmetic with directed rounding, whose row data agree with the instance's.[^188]

The manuscript therefore holds the earlier instance's proof and nothing else of it. The exploratory records, the refuted strengthenings and the unresolved nodes, which are the larger part of the 350 records, were left out by the user's request for the proof as a proof and by the rule set for the second edition. The sources do not show that any mathematician or journal has examined the manuscript. It contains no formal proof; it calls its main theorem proved in a sense it defines itself, each statement being either proved in the text or computed.[^189]

### 5.2 The night of 19 November: a structure document, five reports, a handoff

On 19 November at 02:10 the user opened a new session with the manuscript and a complaint. The manuscript shows that the problem can be solved this way, the user wrote, but not why. What was wanted was an understanding of how unimodality arises and a proof for all $n$ of why it does, which might consult the manuscript but should neither depend on it nor be confined to it. It should not be made of complicated technical inequalities with arbitrary constants supplied by computed proofs, nor avoid the core of the problem by treating small pieces around it.[^489] Below, "the night of 19 November" always means the hours from the opening of that session to the morning of that day.

In that night the session wrote a *structure document*,[^190] from the manuscript, from summaries of its chapters, from a note on the literature, and from exact computations of its own up to $n = 220$. At 04:10 the user had the session send subagents, that is, sessions sent out by the session itself, at the places that the structure document had left open (five were sent), with the instruction to face the core of the problem and to reason about the mathematical objects in preference to computing. Their five reports were finished between 05:33 and 06:38,[^196] and the structure document was last written at 06:44.[^191] A handoff followed that morning. This subsection describes the document, the reports and the handoff in turn.

To judge by these times and by what the document and the reports say of each other, the document was still being revised while the reports were written, and the two draw on one another. One of the reports says that what it first read was a version of the document of 04:40, and that the document was revised while the report was being written.[^193] The document in its last state, the file of 06:44, says that the five reports were commissioned on the basis of it, and it gathers what they found in a section of its own, the document's §6.5.[^192] The session also passed corrections among the five while they worked, so that the reports are not fully independent of one another.

Neither the structure document nor the reports nor the handoff mentions the earlier instance or cites a record of it. Two of the session's working files do: the chapter summaries keep the record identifiers that the manuscript attaches to its statements, and the note on the literature describes the public deposit of the instance's records, of which it says it read the landing page only. The sources that were read do not say whether the session opened the instance itself.

None of this passed through an instance, so that no review accepted or refused it: what the structure document and the reports give as proved, in their own texts or, in part, by taking a statement as proved from the manuscript, is in the words of section 2.2 *proposed*. Each of these documents distinguishes what it proves from what it only computes or conjectures, and the reports also from what they only sketch.[^490] The later instance was to take none of it as proved (section 5.4), and section 6.2 tells what of it that instance proved again.

The view of the problem that the structure document sets out is this. Let $M$ be the maximum displacement of a uniformly random parking function of length $n$. In the forest picture of section 4.2, with $t_v$ the number of vertices of the subtree of a vertex $v$, every vertex carries an independent mark uniform on $\lbrace 0, \ldots, t_v - 1 \rbrace$ and $M$ is the largest mark. The document counts each mark down from the top of its range instead, and obtains a minimum: $n + 1 - M$ has the distribution of the minimum over all vertices $v$ of $G_v + U_v$, where $G_v = n + 1 - t_v$ measures how far the subtree of $v$ falls short of the whole, and $U_v$ is uniform on $\lbrace 1, \ldots, t_v \rbrace$ (the document's Theorem 3.1). In the notation of section 2.1 the event $n + 1 - M = r + 1$ is the event that $M$ stands at the position of deficit $r$, so this is a statement about the row read from its right end.

The document reads this representation as a point process. Each vertex puts a point at the place $G_v$, near the origin when its subtree is large, and throws from there one uniform mark over a range of length $t_v$; the first place that is hit is $n + 1 - M$, which is $r + 1$ when $M$ stands at the position of deficit $r$.[^194]

If the numbers of points at the places $g = 1, \ldots, n$ were independent Poisson variables with means $\mu(g)$, the row would have a closed form. The probability that no place up to $r$ is hit is then $\exp(-\Sigma(r))$ with $\Sigma(r) = \sum_{g \lt r} \mu(g) \cdot (r-g)/(n+1-g)$, and the probability $m_r$ of deficit $r$ is $\exp(-\Sigma(r)) \cdot (1 - \exp(-\nu(r)))$ with $\nu(r) = \sum_{g \le r} \mu(g)/(n+1-g)$. The quotient $\mu(g)/(n+1-g)$ is the mean number of points at $g$ divided by the length of the range over which each spreads its mark, that is, the rate at which the points at $g$ hit any one given place.

For this idealized row the document proves, for any means $\mu$, that if this rate does not increase for $g$ from 1 up to some $R$, then $m_1, \ldots, m_R$ are log-concave, and that they decrease, whatever $\mu$ is, once $\nu(r)$ is at least $\log 2$ (the document's Theorem 4.1). The two together give a unimodal row under the condition, which the theorem states, that $\nu(R) \ge \log 2$: the row is then log-concave up to $R$ and decreases from $R$ on.[^195] The peak is where two rates balance: the number of points that can hit the next place grows with $r$, and the probability that nothing has been hit yet falls. For the true mean number of subtrees of each size the balance falls at a deficit of about $0.73 \cdot n^{2/3}$, and this is the document's account of why the peak lies at a deficit of order $n^{2/3}$ (the true constant in the limit is 0.60).

The true points are not independent, and the document says where the idealization fails. For the positions in the right half of a row only the subtrees with more than $n/2$ vertices matter. These are nested; they form the chain of section 4.2. Their places $G_1 \lt G_2 \lt \ldots$ have the law of the partial sums of independent gaps with the Borel distribution of that section, multiplied by a factor that depends on the last point: a renewal sequence with Borel gaps, slightly tilted. For the true mean number of subtrees of each size, the condition of the document's theorem on the idealized row holds up to about $g = n/6$. What is lost is the point of the proof at which independence is used: in a renewal sequence the probability that nothing has been hit up to $r$ and the number of points that can hit the next place are functions of the same points, and their joint expectation has to be handled directly. On the left half the large subtrees are not nested, and the points form a branching structure.

The document proves the minimum representation and the theorem on the idealization, and says plainly that it contains no complete proof for all $n$. It leaves two conjectures. The first (the document's Conjecture 6.4) is the missing statement on the right half: that the minimum of place plus uniform mark has a unimodal distribution on the deficits up to $n/2$, for every renewal sequence whose renewal density does not increase and, as a separate statement, for the tilted Borel sequence of the forest. Its experiments show that some condition on the gaps is needed: with Borel, geometric or power-law gaps the computed distribution was unimodal; with gaps all equal to 5, or with two widely separated gap lengths, it was not. The second (the document's Conjecture 6.3) belongs to the left half, and it is stated later in this subsection with the result that uses it.

The five reports took up what the document had left open. The report on renewal smoothing attacked the document's Conjecture 6.4 in the form of a general theorem, and the report on the Borel chain was, in the words of its dispatch, to close the right half through the exact combinatorial structure of the Borel chain; the report says that every vertex that enters the steps there lies on the chain of section 4.2.[^409] The report on the left half took the document's Conjecture 6.3 and the question of what carries its single change of sign to all rows $n \gt 2k$; the report on the continuous limit asked why the density of the limit distribution of the deficit is unimodal and which part of the reason carries over to finite $n$; and the report on free exploration was to look for a structure that treats a row whole, without dividing it at its centre.[^197] Three of their results matter for what followed.

The first is the *staircase reduction* of the report on free exploration (the report's Theorem A). Consider the two statements

 (Col) for all $n$ and $k$: if $d_{n,k} \ge 0$ then $d_{n+1,k} \ge 0$,

 (Diag) for all $n$ and $k$: if $d_{n,k} \le 0$ then $d_{n+1,k+1} \le 0$.

The first says that a step which is non-negative stays so in the next row of its column; the second that a step which is non-positive stays so one place down its diagonal. Suppose row $n$ is unimodal: its steps are non-negative up to some position $\kappa$ and non-positive after it. Then (Col) makes the steps of row $n + 1$ non-negative up to $\kappa$, and (Diag) makes them non-positive from $\kappa + 2$ on. Only the step at $\kappa + 1$ is left open, and whichever sign it has, row $n + 1$ is unimodal, with its turning position at $\kappa$ or at $\kappa + 1$. So if (Col) and (Diag) hold from some row on and that row is unimodal, every later row is unimodal and the turning position moves by zero or one from each row to the next: the signs of $d$ form a staircase.

Since both statements are weak inequalities, the argument allows steps that are zero. The report also proved a converse for the case that no step is zero (if all rows are unimodal and the peak moves by zero or one, then (Col) and (Diag) hold), and found no violation of either statement, and no zero step, in exact rows up to $n = 140$.

The second is the corresponding reduction for the right half alone, in the report on the Borel chain. It concerns the positions with $n \le 2k - 1$, where the subtrees above the level form one chain, and the statements

 (C) if $d_{n,k} \gt 0$ and $n + 1 \le 2k - 1$, then $d_{n+1,k} \gt 0$,

 (D) if $d_{n,k} \le 0$, then $d_{n+1,k+1} \le 0$.

These are (Col), with strict signs, and (Diag), each restricted to that range. Together they imply that no row rises again after falling in its right half: if $d_{n,k} \le 0$ there, then $d_{n-1,k} \le 0$ by (C) read backwards, and then $d_{n,k+1} \le 0$ by the report's statement (D).

Both became nodes of the later instance: the report's (C) under the same letter and the report's statement (D) under the name (Diag₁); the node (D) of the later instance is another statement (section 6.4).

The report also derived, for a fixed column $k$ and $0 \le r \le k - 1$, the equation

$$d_{k+r,k}/(k+r)! = \sum_{j=0}^{r} h_{r-j} \cdot c_j, \quad \text{with } h_i = (i+1)^i/i!,$$

in which $c_0 = -k^{k-2}/k!$ is one negative "shock", coming from the trees with exactly $k$ vertices, and each further $c_j$ is an explicit combination of two entries of the smaller row $k + j - 1$. Statement (C) says that the solution of this equation, once positive, stays positive.
