The report names the standard tool for carrying a single change of sign over, a single-crossing lemma: if the terms change sign once and the ratio of the weights of one row to those of the row before does not decrease along the sum, a positive step is followed by a positive step. The handoff's phrase for it is *argument by order*. The report's verdict was that what decides the sign is size and not order.[^198] It explained why arguments by order fail on (C): the weights $h_i$ and the Borel masses behind them are log-convex, so that the shock is forgotten only at the rate of a power of $r$, and the comparison of weights that a single-crossing argument needs goes the wrong way. Section 4.8 tells how the soft argument of the earlier instance had met the log-convexity of the same Borel law.

The third is the *critical-tilt propagation* of the report on the left half, a theorem that under three hypotheses makes the steps of a column non-negative in every row from a certain row on. Fix a column $k \ge 2$, and recall from section 4.8 the contribution $\delta_t$ of a single tree with $t$ vertices to the step at level $k$, the quantity that the earlier instance had met in its five pieces: $\delta_t = 0$ for $t \lt k$, $\delta_k = -k^{k-2}$, and $\delta_t = (k+1) \cdot b_{t-1,k} - (k-1) \cdot b_{t-1,k-1}$ for $t \ge k + 1$.[^491] Choosing one tree of a forest and leaving the rest gives the lower bound

$$d_{n,k} \ge \sum_{t=k}^{n} \binom{n}{t} \cdot \delta_t \cdot A_{n-t,k-1},$$

in which what is dropped is non-negative.

Let $x_k = (k/(k+1))^{k-1}$, the critical tilt of section 4.8, and let $n_0 \ge 2k + 1$. The theorem has three hypotheses. The first, (A1), is that the sequence $\delta_t$ changes sign once for $k \le t \le n_0$, from non-positive to non-negative. The second, (A2), is that the numbers $A_{N,k-1} \cdot x_k^N/N!$ do not increase with $N$. The third, (A3), is that the sum $\sum_{t=k}^{n_0} \delta_t \cdot x_k^t/t!$ is non-negative. The conclusion of the theorem is that $d_{n,k} \ge 0$ for all $n \ge n_0$, and that $\delta_t \gt 0$ for all $t \gt n_0$.

The proof multiplies the lower bound by $x_k^n/n!$. It becomes a sum of the terms $\delta_t \cdot x_k^t/t!$, which change sign once, against the weights $A_{n-t,k-1} \cdot x_k^{n-t}/(n-t)!$, which by (A2) do not decrease in $t$. Such a sum is at least the weight at the change of sign times the unweighted sum, and that is non-negative by (A3). A non-negative step $d_{n,k}$ in turn makes $\delta_{n+1}$ positive, so both the sign pattern and the non-negative sum pass to the next row.

Hypothesis (A2) is the growth inequality of the earlier instance (section 4.8). The report takes it from a lemma of the manuscript, stated there for $k \ge 43$, and from exact computation for the smaller columns. (The earlier instance's own record has it for every $k \ge 2$, as a claim that its review accepted, and its proof divides at the same column: one part of it is proved by analysis from $k = 43$ on and by an exact computation in rationals for the columns 2 to 42.)[^492] What is left open in each column is therefore (A1) and (A3), and with $n_0 = 2k + 1$ both speak of rows up to $2k$ only: the whole left half of a column would follow from two statements about its first layer, the rows $k$ to $2k$ of the column (section 4.8). Conjecture 6.3 of the structure document is (A1) for all $t$.

In the report's computations, exact up to $k = 220$ and in floating point up to $k = 2000$ and at three larger columns, (A3) holds at $n_0 = 2k + 1$ precisely for the columns $k \ge 186$; that it holds for every $k \ge 186$ the report leaves as a conjecture. The columns $2 \le k \le 220$ were closed for $n \ge 2k + 1$ with exact computation, except at the four positions (5,2), (7,3), (9,4) and (11,5), where the step is negative. The computation is finite in each column, and the propagation theorem supplies the rest: the report computed, in exact integers and for trees of at most $3k + 3$ vertices, the signs of the $\delta_t$ and the first row $n_0 \ge 2k + 1$ at which the sum of (A3) is non-negative, so that (A1) and (A3), which are statements about the $\delta_t$ with $t \le n_0$ only, hold at that $n_0$ and the theorem gives every row from $n_0$ on. The rows $n$ with $2k + 1 \le n \lt n_0$, at most 13 in a column and none for $186 \le k \le 220$, were computed directly, and the four negative steps lie among them.[^199] The reports also added exact counterexamples to a list of stronger properties one might hope for.

Statements about the sign of $d$ along columns thus came, on this night, to stand at the centre as reductions of the root. They were not new. A proposition of the same kind had been proved in the earlier instance on 10 November and accepted as a claim, and had not been made a node (section 4.8).[^493]

By this account's reading, the two are not the same statement. Both use the column statement. For their second hypothesis the report takes the diagonal statement (Diag), and the earlier record takes a comparison of neighbouring columns: the first row at which column $k$ is non-negative does not decrease with $k$. The earlier record's implication therefore needs no first unimodal row and concludes unimodality only. The diagonal rule, and the fact that the peak moves by at most one, appear in it only in the converse, where they are shown to be equivalent to each other under the assumption that no two neighbouring entries of a row are equal. Both texts allow zero steps in the implication and exclude them in the converse.[^494] Section 6.2 tells how the two stand to the relations that the later instance would accept.

The earlier instance had also certified two of the counterexamples that the reports found again: the failure of log-concavity of the cumulative counts at $n = 780$[^200] and the failure of a term-by-term inequality at $n = 7485$.[^201] It also had the critical tilt with its growth inequality, of which the manuscript kept a lemma that the report on the left half cites. The reduction and the counterexamples had not been on the path of the earlier proof, so they were not in the manuscript, and the session of 19 November, working from the manuscript, found them again in a few hours. By this account's reading, their absence from the manuscript is why they were found again.[^495]

The idealization has a counterpart in the earlier instance too. By this account's reading, the Poisson model of the structure document with the true means is, formula for formula except for the convention at the end of the row, the simplified model of independent subtree sizes of the earlier instance's record of 13 November (section 4.11).[^410] That record proves the model's row unimodal for every $n \ge 2$ and log-concave for every $n \ge 3$, by one inequality for $n \ge 299$ and, in the rows up to $n = 400$, in exact rational arithmetic with interval arithmetic in directed rounding, and its review accepted both as claims.[^496] By contrast the structure document, whose theorem covers the true means only up to about $g = n/6$, has the log-concavity of the idealized row over its whole range only as a numerical observation at three values of $n$. The structure document does not cite the record.

At 11:09 the user asked for a new instance that would go on with the subject from what the structure document held, and at 11:37 specified that it should start from the place where this attempt was stuck. In a dialogue with the session, between 11:16 and 11:40, the user fixed its terms. The criterion of success was to be a new, concise and explanatory complete proof that is not filled in by computational certification and is formalized. Computation was to serve for base cases only, and the handed-over material for reference only. The terms also covered the language models on which the sessions of each role were to run and a change to the review procedure, by which a review sends out three independent sub-reviewers and then judges for itself.

The session wrote these terms into a *handoff* document, together with an account of the night's findings in five parts. The first part is the view of the structure document, as told earlier in this subsection.

The second part is a reading of the right half of a row, drawn from computations by three of the reports. Near the right end, up to a deficit of about $2 \cdot n^{2/3}$, lies a *critical regime*, which contains the peak and in which the computed rows are log-concave and the comparisons of ratios that had been tried all hold. From a deficit of about $2.4 \cdot n^{2/3}$ to the centre lies what the handoff calls a *bulk*.[^202] In the bulk the row follows a power law and is log-convex, and every monotonicity of sizes that had been tried fails, a positive step being there the difference of two counts of nearly equal size, the ties and the blocked maxima of section 4.3. From this the handoff draws a judgment, and marks it as a judgment about where to look and not as a necessity: a proof without numerical constants would have to pass, in the bulk, through statements that speak only of signs.

The third part names four *blocked places*, where the night's work had stopped: the report's two sign statements (C) and (D) on the right half; the two open hypotheses of the critical-tilt propagation on the left; a gap between the limit and finite $n$; and a gap inside the limit. As to the limit, one of the reports had given a new proof that the limit distribution of the deficit divided by $(n+1)^{2/3}$ is unimodal, a theorem the manuscript already had (the limit law itself is Petersson's, of 2013). The report wrote the limit as an exponential clock run at a single random rate, the integral of the running maximum of a Brownian motion, and showed that the density of this rate is log-concave. At finite $n$ no object is known that plays the part of this rate; and the log-concavity of its density is itself known only from a closed formula and not from the structure of the process.

The fourth part lists the roads closed by exact counterexamples, and the fifth the reductions proved in the reports, with the request that the new instance prove them again.

### 5.3 The other line: from five documents to a paper

A second line had begun the evening before, in another session. The user had five documents on what these documents call the Cayley–Borel coherence of the problem, and three notes of the same day.[^497] The sources do not say who or what wrote them, beyond "the user's separate session".[^203] They build on the manuscript, and one of the notes claimed a full proof of the conjecture by citing from the manuscript the certificate up to $n = 20000$ and, above it, the increase before the peak. The name Cayley–Borel coherence is proposed by the main one of the five documents. That document starts from one random tree, the critical Poisson Galton–Watson tree written as a labelled tree: taken at random size, its size has the Borel distribution of section 4.2, and with the size fixed it is a uniformly random rooted labelled tree, which the document calls a Cayley tree. The coherence is the system in which the document arranges this fact together with forests, the marking of a vertex, chains of ancestors, the cutting of edges and its reverse, the merging of trees, and the document says that most of the results so arranged are classical theorems.[^204]

The user's plan was to have these unified into one structure in standard mathematical language and to plant that structure in a new instance which would not depend on the earlier instance or the manuscript once started.[^498]

Agents dispatched by the operator built such a document, in two rounds of work, between the late evening of 18 November (the operator's plan is of 22:19, the first dispatch of 23:05) and 19 November at 07:24, reading the earlier instance's records and the manuscript as an inventory of where each tool appears.[^205] Two critical readings on the morning of 19 November judged that its mathematics was genuine but that its organizing superstructure was "naming". The document had arranged its matter as one random forest with three descriptions, two mechanisms that act on it, a dictionary to the classical names, and statements that were to account for why the same classical tools recur in the research on the conjecture.[^206] The two readings had been commissioned to test the suspicion that this arrangement was a forced assembly of known results under new headings. Both found the forest a genuine object, known in each of its parts, and the theorems built on it correct where they checked them; what they classed as naming were the organizing statements of the document, and one of them called the two mechanisms, the dictionary and the document's test of its own account a classification that no outcome could have contradicted.[^207] The user then had it rewritten as a research article in standard frameworks, and the result, last built at 22:14, is the *paper*: "The maximum displacement of parking functions and the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform random forest, with three theorems on a unimodality conjecture".[^208] The paper holds the mathematics of the document, that is, the statements to which the document had given a proof status,[^499] placed in four settings of the literature: parking as linear probing, fringe subtrees of conditioned Galton–Watson trees, Boltzmann models, and the second difference in the language of log-concavity and total positivity. The organizing sections of the document are not in it, and it uses none of the words structure, coherence, system, mechanism and dictionary as the name of anything.[^209]

The paper starts from one formula, the forest representation in the form $\Pr(M \lt k) = E \prod_v \min(1, k/t_v)$, with $t_v$ the number of vertices of the subtree of $v$. It proves three theorems for all $n$. The first is that $d_{n,k} \gt 0$ when $n \ge 21k^2$. The second is that $d_{n,k} \lt 0$ when $k \ge 4(n-k+1)^{3/2}$. The third concerns the positions with deficit $r = n - k \ge 2$ and $k \ge r^{3/2}$. At these positions what the paper calls the *cross product*, $S = B_+ \cdot d_{n,k} - B_- \cdot d_{n,k+1}$, is positive, from which it follows that a step which is not positive at such a position is followed in its row only by negative steps, the next of them smaller than the step itself (the paper's Theorem 5.9). The cross product is the quantity $S$ of section 4.5, with the boundary terms $B_-$ and $B_+$ of that section. Like the documents of the night of 19 November, the paper was written outside the instances: its theorems are proved in its own text, and in the words of section 2.2 they were *proposed* until the reviews of the later instance took them up (section 6.3).[^500]

With exact rows for $n \le 300$ the paper reduces the conjecture to one inequality, which it calls Target T and leaves open: for $n \ge 301$ and $k^2 \lt (n-k)^3$, $d_{n,k} \ge 0$. The curve $k = r^{3/2}$, on which the deficit equals $k^{2/3}$, is the paper's way of writing the scale of the peak (section 6.3 says more).

Three computations of the paper recur in what follows, and the later instance's records cite them by the numbers of the paper's appendix. The first is the table of all rows for $n \le 300$ in exact integers (the paper's item C5): every row is unimodal, no step is zero, and the reduction to Target T stands on it.

The second sits inside the proof of the third theorem (the paper's item C8). That proof ends in an explicit rational function of $r$ and $k$ that has to be positive. Estimates show it positive for $r \ge 256$. For $2 \le r \le 255$ it increases with $k$, and its values were computed in exact rational arithmetic from $k = \lceil r^{3/2} \rceil$ up to the first positive one, the cross product being evaluated directly at the 122 positions where the function is not positive, all in rows $n \le 102$.

The third is a second table of rows, up to $n = 600$, computed by an independent program for the comparison with a neighbouring model (the paper's item C14). The paper reports from it that Target T holds in every row from 95 to 600, gives this the status of evidence, and does not build on it.

In this account "the paper's rows" or "the paper's table" are the first, "the paper's rational computation" the second, and "the paper's larger table" the third.

The paper does not contain the earlier instance's proof. The note that had imported the manuscript's certificate was rewritten without it, and by the user's decision the paper mentions neither the earlier argument nor its deposit.[^210]

The paper's content was cut into six packages, each a part of its text copied by line range with a framing note, meant for a new instance that would see only the paper. The system has no command for planting records. The route chosen was to place each package where a finished exploration session would have left its work, so that a peer and a review would make a record of it in the ordinary way.[^211] This was rehearsed on a scratch instance in the late evening of 19 November.[^212] The planned instance had not been created when, on 20 November at 00:44, the packages went into the running one (section 5.4).[^501]

### 5.4 What reached the later instance

The later instance was initialized on 19 November at 11:50:29, from the first of the two lines, that of the structure document and the handoff. The handoff was submitted as its first standing input at 12:04:04, and five operator questions built from the handoff were published two seconds later as its first publication.[^213]

By this account's reading, three of the five questions restate blocked places.[^502] One asks from what regularity of the terms of the column equation it follows that the solution, once positive, stays positive, which is the statement (C), and whether that regularity comes from the complete monotonicity of the Borel distribution. One asks why the tilted sum of hypothesis (A3) is positive from $k = 186$ on, and whether that can be said without constants. One asks what, at finite $n$, corresponds to the random rate of the limit.

The fourth asks what quantity a single argument would have to consider in order to cover both regimes of the right half. The fifth concerns the open statements of the two halves, which look at the law of the same chain, on the left with the level fixed and the size of the tree varying, on the right with the length fixed and the level varying: it asks whether they can be written as one statement, and offers the staircase of (Col) and (Diag) as a candidate. Beside these five the frontier carried, from the initialization, a sixth question that the system itself places: a standing question about the root as a whole.

The handoff gives the paths of the manuscript, the structure document, the five reports and their computations, and three rules. Computation may be a proof step only for a base range, an exact table for $n \le 30$, which this account calls the *base table*. The handed-over material is information and may not be cited as proved: only what is proved again inside the instance and passes its review counts. Records are written in Korean. The handoff does not name the earlier instance; its "earlier research" is the manuscript and the work of that night.

On 20 November at 00:44 the second line, that of the paper, met the first inside the later instance. By a decision of the user the six packages were inserted, not into a new instance, but into the running one. No session was launched after 00:19:59. A script placed the six packages as ended exploration sessions at 00:44:02 and recorded them in a file in the runner's folder,[^503] a fourth standing input explaining them was submitted at 00:44:08 (the second and the third, of the first day, are told in section 6.1), and the runner was started again at 00:44:27. To judge by these times and by the script's refusal to run beside a live runner, the runner had been stopped in between;[^504] no file records the stop.

The fourth input also settled a conflict with the first of the handoff's three rules: the paper's own computations, the rows $n \le 300$ above all, may be used as proof steps. The user qualified this in words the input quotes: that it permits using what is already computed and is not a permission to widen it crudely, the structural understanding remaining the thing sought.

No source of the research that was read describes the later instance as based on the earlier one.[^505] The nearest thing that a source says is the reason that the operator gave with the five questions of the first publication, that the instance continues from the place where the preceding research was blocked.[^506] What did pass from the earlier instance to the later is the following. The earlier instance's proof reached the later one as the manuscript, whose path the handoff gave to every session, and as a summary of its route in the handoff, both marked as information that may not be cited. Its exact identities and objects reached it twice: through the manuscript and the night's reports as information, and through the paper as text that the later instance's own reviews examined and published. Its refuted strengthenings reached it partly, as rediscoveries.

Its records and the files of its certificates were not given to it. Their identifiers were within reach, since the manuscript attaches them to its statements and lists them in an appendix, and the note on the literature gives the address of the public deposit of the earlier records. So were copies of the certificate files of eighteen of the records, which lie in the manuscript's data bundle,[^214] to which the manuscript's Appendix D points. The published records of the later instance mention none of these.[^507] The later instance's rules would in any case not have let it use the earlier route or its certificates.

## 6. The later instance, 19 to 23 November

The work of the later instance is told below through the nodes that it put on its frontier, and the story turns on eighteen of them. The list that follows states each of them once, in the order in which they were posed: what the node says, which part of a row it concerns, and what it has to do with the root; a node that proved to be a side road is marked as one. This account follows the instance up to the commit considered here, that of 23 November at 08:10, at which the root is formally proved (section 2.3): everything said in this section of what was or was not proved is said of that time, and what the instance proved after it is not told.

Two words name the two parts of a row throughout this section. A position $(n,k)$ lies in the *left half* of its row when $n \ge 2k + 1$ and in the *right half* when $n \le 2k$, so that along a column $k$ the right half comes first, in the rows up to $2k$, and the left half begins at row $2k + 1$. A row fails to be unimodal exactly when some strict descent $d_{n,i} \lt 0$ is followed by a strict ascent $d_{n,j} \gt 0$ with $i \lt j$, and the first two nodes say where such a pair cannot lie.

On the first evening, 19 November, at 19:37, the first record cut the root in two, into (S_L) and (S_R), and posed with them (L), the statement for the left half.

- (S_L), the left part: no such pair has its descent in the left half, at a level $i$ with $n \ge 2i + 1$.
- (S_R), the right half: no such pair has its descent at a level $i$ with $n \le 2i$. The two together are the root, and this is the cut that the formal proof follows.
- (L), the left increase: from the sixth column on, no step in the left half of a row is negative. It gives (S_L). It is the main road on the left, and one of the three statements on which, as section 3 has told, the final proof rests.

At 20:46 the second record made nodes of the two statements of the staircase reduction that had been handed over (section 5.2). Both speak of every position, and together they give the root.

- (Col): a step that is non-negative stays non-negative in the next row of its column. It follows from (L), by a relation accepted on 20 November;[^508] the paragraph after this list says what that relation draws on.
- (Diag): a step that is non-positive stays non-positive one place down its diagonal, from $(n,k)$ to $(n+1,k+1)$. It is a side road, and section 6.9 tells what became of it and what it added.[^509]

In that night three more records posed nodes on which the story turns, two of them for the right half.

- (Col₁), of 01:48 on 20 November: (Col) for the steps with $n + 1 \le 2k$. It was formally proved and is, as far as the frontier shows, among the nodes through which the informal relations for the right half passed.
- (Diag₁), of the same time: (Diag) for $n \le 2k - 1$. With (Col₁) it gives (S_R). It had no proof of its own and stood by relation only.
- (C), of 01:53: a column which has turned positive in one of its rows up to $2k - 1$ stays positive in the rows after that one, as far as row $2k - 1$. It is one of the three statements of the final proof.
- (Mono), of 02:22, compares neighbouring columns: as long as column $k$ has not turned positive, column $k + 1$ has not either. It speaks of every position, with (Col) it gives the root, and it is one of the three statements of the final proof. It follows from (Diag) and asks less, and the record that posed it gave as its reason that the root needs only this weaker statement (section 6.2).[^510]

On the morning of 20 November, at 06:12, the paper brought four nodes (sections 5.3 and 6.3). Three of them are stated through the paper's curve, on which the deficit $n - k$ of a position equals $k^{2/3}$: a position lies beyond the curve, as a row is read from its right end, when its deficit is larger, that is, when $k^2 \lt (n-k)^3$.

- Target T: the paper's open inequality, that the step $d_{n,k}$ is non-negative for $n \ge 301$ at the positions beyond the curve. It gives the root. It had no proof of its own and stood by relation only.
- (T_R): Target T restricted to the right half. It gives (S_R), and it too had no proof of its own and stood by relation only.
- (CI): a statement about one position in each column $k \ge 260$, that the column has turned positive by the first row beyond the curve. It gives (Mono), and with (Col₁) it gives (T_R). As far as the frontier shows, most of the relations by which the right half stood informally passed through it; the formal proof does not use it.
- (X): the cross product of section 5.3 is positive at every position. It would give the root by itself. It is a side road, and it stayed unresolved.

In the course of 20 November five more of the nodes on which the story turns were posed. Four of them are side roads.

- (CB), of 10:45, for the left half: a bound on the growth of column $k - 1$ along its rows in the left half, for $k \ge 148$; weighted by the critical tilt, the entries of that column do not increase there. It was formally proved. No accepted proof of (L) rested on the node; the formal proof of (L) takes definitions and lemmas, though not the theorem that states (CB), from the record in which (CB) was formally proved.[^511]
- (Base), of the same time, for the left half: a condition on tilted sums over the rows $k + 1$ to $2k + 1$ of a column. With (CB) it would have given (L). It stayed unresolved, and no accepted proof of (L) rested on it.
- (D), of 12:24, for the right half: a convexity, along a diagonal, of the cumulative counts weighted by the critical tilt, for $k \ge 301$ and the lengths $k + 1$ to $2k - 1$. It was formally proved, and only the theorem by which it was proved is used again, in the proof of (L); the node itself is not.
- (E), of the same time, for the right half: an upper bound for the left side of (D). With (CI) and (D) it would have given (T_R). It stayed unresolved, and nothing of it is used again.
- (Mono_265), of 23:42, the fifth: (Mono) for the columns $k \ge 265$. It gives (Mono), and it gives (S_R), each with a finite remainder read from the paper's table. It was the first node whose proof stood, and the first to be formalized.

One relation of the list joins two statements of unlike range: (Col) speaks of every position, and (L) of the left half from the sixth column on. The proof by which (Col) follows from (L) takes from (L) the rows from $2k + 1$ on in the columns $k \ge 148$; for the rows up to $2k$ of those columns it draws on the proof of (Col₁) in its own record, and for the columns up to 147 on a claim that reads the paper's table.[^512] The converse, that (L) follows from (Col), is an accepted relation of the same day. Beside (Col) its proof needs, in every column $k \ge 6$, one row up to $2k + 1$ in which the step is non-negative, from which (Col) carries the sign to all later rows: for $k \ge 265$ it has that row from the entry theorem, a bound on the crossing row that section 6.4 tells, and for the columns below 265 from the paper's table.[^513]

Five further nodes, on which the story does not turn, stand in the first table of section 6.10 with all the others: (Z), (SP), (L1°), (L2°) and (DR).[^514] Of these (L1°) has an episode in sections 6.5 and 6.9 and is stated where it occurs. That table gives every node in the words of the frontier, with the publication that proposed it and the course of its proofs, and the second table of section 6.10 lists together all the accepted relations among the nodes, as they stood at that commit.

### 6.1 The first hours

The later instance was initialized on 19 November at 11:50,[^215] with eight sessions at a time, a new session launched every three minutes, and explorations alternating with attempts. The settings name one model for producers and peers, with a second as an alternative, and another model for reviews; in the event every producer, peer and organizer session ran on the first, every review on the model named for reviews, and the alternative was never launched.[^216] A review was to send out three independent sub-reviewers and then judge for itself.[^217] The root statement of the instance is the same proposition as the earlier instance's, written afresh, with the first seven rows as check values.[^218]

Nine sessions were launched between 12:04 and 12:28. They had the handoff, with its account of where the night's analysis had stopped, and what most of them did first was to compute: to scan large ranges, some in floating-point arithmetic, for the places where candidate properties break. The user, shown their reports and their computation folders, objected, at 14:54 to 14:55: "computed a large range in floating point? why?" The task, the user said, is to solve the actual problem, not to run a computation wondering whether something will break here.[^515]

The second standing input, "the place of computation", followed at 14:56.[^411] It lists what the first sessions had computed: exact tables to $n = 500$, floating-point tables to $n = 4001$, thousands of random test cases. It says that this had brought the proof of the root not one step nearer: asking by computation whether something breaks helps to choose a conjecture and does not prove one. Computation is to be used in two cases only: to check on small examples a particular step of an argument under construction, and to test on small examples a conjecture that arose in building it.

At 20:21, after the first record had been published and the user had been shown the state of the instance, the third input, "treat the core head-on", followed.[^412] The input consists entirely of the user's words, given unaltered. The core of a problem is to be faced and not cut into small pieces. A division that is natural is no fault, but the common core of the parts is still to be sought, and one is not to retreat to something smaller when blocked. Tools that exist in earlier work are to be cited and not reinvented, and duplicates are to be brought together as one object. And what is already complete, if its logic is untidy, is to be made concise: premises removed, conditions deleted, the range widened, what seemed separate connected. The operator added a note, which it says adds no condition, on how citing existing tools sits with the handoff's rule that handed-over results must be proved again. By the note, which marks itself as the operator's reading, the two speak of different things. The user's words are about saying exactly where a tool or a result comes from, and about not presenting as one's own what has only been made a second time; the rule is about what a handed-over result needs in order to count as proved. So a handed-over result that is to be a step of a proof must still be proved again in the instance, and its origin is then given as the handed-over material.[^219]

From the first review on, which was published after the second input,[^220] reviews write that re-measuring or extending a computed range "is not counted as a contribution". After the third input they also require overlaps with earlier records to be cited.

### 6.2 The first wave: the exact structure again, and the statements about columns

Because the handoff forbade citing the handed-over material, the first records had to prove the exact structure again inside the instance. Four records,[^221] published on the first evening between 19:37 and 22:55 and written without knowledge of one another, each re-proved the last-car recurrence of section 4.2. Between them they also re-proved other exact tools that section 4 has met: the decomposition of a parking function into the trees of its forest and its decomposition into prime parking functions,[^516] the independence of the displacements given the outcome, the forest formula, and the fact that for $n \le 2k - 1$ the subtrees above the level form one chain. What this subsection and the next tell of that night and of the morning after it was accepted by the reviews of its records on proofs in words, the statements as claims and the implications between nodes as relations; nothing of it was formalized at the time, and no node was yet proved. Where a statement rests on a table or on another computation, was only observed in computed rows, or is an expectation or a limiting value that no record proves, the text says so at the statement.

Reviews held the tables of these records to the permitted $n \le 30$, so that columns were closed only for $k \le 11$. What was closed in those columns is the inequality $b_{n,k} \ge b_{n,k-1}$ in every row $n \ge 2k + 1$, which the node (L) asks for from the sixth column on. The first record proved it for every $k \le 11$, apart from the four positions (5,2), (7,3), (9,4) and (11,5), where the step is negative.[^222] The proof computes in two places. One is the base table of section 5.4, the exact rows $n \le 30$ that the handoff admitted. The other lies in the record's proof of the growth inequality of section 4.8, where one sum is evaluated exactly for $2 \le k \le 9$, an estimate bounding it only from $k = 10$ on.[^517] A table of finitely many rows closes a column in every row through the critical-tilt propagation of section 5.2, which the record had proved again. For each of the columns 2 to 11 the base table gives finitely many facts in the rows up to a row $n_0 \le 30$, among them the two hypotheses (A1) and (A3), which section 5.2 says are left open in each column, and the theorem gives every row from $n_0$ on.[^223] From column 12 on, the tilted sum that the theorem needs first becomes non-negative in a row above 30, so that the base table no longer reaches the hypothesis:[^224] for the columns 12 to 14 this is a fact of the record's computation and not of a proof, and from column 15 on it needs no computation, since the record looks for that row only from row $2k + 1$ on, which is then above 30.

With these re-proofs came the nodes. Those of the first evening and night include the three statements about columns on which, as section 3 has told, the final proof rests: (L), (C) and (Mono).

The first record, published at 19:37,[^225] cut each row at its centre into the left part (S_L) and the right half (S_R), which together are the root. For the left part it posed the plain statement

 (L) for all $k \ge 6$ and $n \ge 2k + 1$: $b_{n,k} \ge b_{n,k-1}$.

That is, from the sixth column on, no step in the left half of a row is negative.

The second record, at 20:46,[^226] re-proved the staircase reduction of section 5.2 and made its two statements nodes, (Col) and (Diag), with the relation that the two together imply the root. In the night the restrictions of these two to the right half followed, (Col₁) and (Diag₁), at 01:48 on 20 November.[^227] With them, at 01:53,[^228] came the report's statement (C) of section 5.2 as a node,

 (C) for all $k \ge 2$ and $k + 1 \le n \le 2k - 2$: if $d_{n,k} \gt 0$ then $d_{n+1,k} \gt 0$,

the persistence of a positive sign along a column inside the range where there is a single chain.

(Col₁) and (C) differ in their sign and in their last row. (Col₁) carries a non-negative sign, and its last case is the passage from row $2k - 1$ to row $2k$. The conclusion of that case is about the middle step $d_{2k,k}$ of section 3, the step of column $k$ in the middle of the even row $2k$, which lies outside the range of a single chain, and it is as this case of (Col₁) that the middle step enters the reduction of the right half to (Col₁) and (Diag₁).[^229] (C) carries a positive sign and ends one row earlier, with the passage from row $2k - 2$ to row $2k - 1$. It was posed apart from (Col) because the row $2k - 1$ is where the behaviour of a column changes: up to it the steps of the column obey a closed linear equation, and beyond it only an inequality is left.[^230] The difference in the last row returns in section 6.9.

One statement of that night was not in the handed-over material, and the proof of the root would come to rest on it. It concerns the rows at which neighbouring columns turn positive.

Write $q_0(k)$ for the crossing row of column $k$ (section 4.3), here the first row $n \ge k + 1$ at which the step $d_{n,k}$ is positive. Some records work instead with the first row at which the step is non-negative. The two rows coincide unless a step is exactly zero, and the statements that follow say which is meant where it matters.

The staircase reduction asks, through (Diag), that $q_0$ increase strictly with $k$. For (Diag) applies also at the last row before column $k$ turns positive, $n = q_0(k) - 1$, and there it asks that $d_{n+1,k+1} \le 0$, which says that $q_0(k+1) \ge q_0(k) + 1$. Unimodality needs less: it needs only that $q_0$ never decrease. A record of 02:22[^231] posed this weaker requirement as a node, saying that the root needs only this weaker statement. Stated without $q_0$, it is

 (Mono) for all $n \ge 3$ and $1 \le k \le n - 2$: if $d_{j,k} \le 0$ for every row $j$ with $k + 1 \le j \le n$, then $d_{n,k+1} \le 0$.

In words: as long as column $k$ has not turned positive, column $k+1$ has not either. Together with (Col), which says that a column that has turned non-negative stays so, this gives the root: in any row the columns that have not yet turned positive form a final segment, so the row's non-positive steps come after its positive ones. The relation (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root was accepted with the node, and so was the relation (Diag) $\Rightarrow$ (Mono). The first of the two differs from the staircase relation in one premise: it has (Mono) where the staircase has (Diag). (Diag) itself stayed on the frontier, since proving it would still have given (Mono); section 6.9 tells what became of it and how the proofs of the root relate to it.[^518]

The same statement, differing only in the strictness of one inequality, had arisen independently in another exploration of the first hours.[^232] Its report, finished at 18:28 on 19 November, eight hours before this publication, proves that (Col) and the order of the crossing rows give the root. Its work was published later, in a record that notes the overlap.[^519] A search of the handed-over folder for the statement found nothing,[^520] and the handoff itself gives the reduction only in the staircase form, with (Diag).

The reduction was not new to the research as a whole. The earlier instance had proved a reduction of this kind on 10 November and left it as a claim (section 4.8), and the night of 19 November had found the staircase (section 5.2). By this account's reading, the two relations into the root that the later instance had accepted by that night answer to these two.[^521] The proposition of 10 November corresponds to (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root: it supposes that every column, once non-negative, stays non-negative in all later rows, which is what (Col) says, and that the first row at which column $k$ is non-negative never decreases with $k$, which is the order of the crossing rows that (Mono) asks for. The theorem of the report on free exploration corresponds to (Col) $\wedge$ (Diag) $\Rightarrow$ root, whose premises are the report's own two statements. And the rule that the earlier proposition has in its converse, that a negative step stays negative along its diagonal, is (Diag) with strict signs.

To judge by how the two are worded, the proposition of 10 November and the relation with (Mono) count a step that is exactly zero differently. The proposition speaks of the first row at which column $k$ is non-negative, and (Mono) of a column that has not yet turned positive; a step that is exactly zero therefore counts in the proposition as the column having become non-negative and in (Mono) as its not yet having turned positive. The difference can show only where some step is exactly zero. No such step occurs in the exact rows $n \le 1000$ of the earlier instance[^134] or in the rows $n \le 600$ that a record of the later instance gives as computational evidence,[^135] and none is excluded by any theorem. Neither derivation of the root depends on the difference: each of the two implications is proved as it is worded, with steps that are zero allowed.

The proposition of 10 November stood in a record of the earlier instance, and the records of that instance were not among what the sessions of the later instance were given (section 5.4). What happened here for the first time is that the reduction was put on the frontier, as a node with a relation to the root, and worked on.

The record of 02:22[^233] also gave the equation of a column its working form. Write $D_k(x) = \sum_n d_{n,k} \cdot x^n/n!$ for the exponential generating function of column $k$, and $A_{k-1}(x)$ for that of the parking functions whose displacements are all smaller than $k$. The handed-over reports, and the instance's first records after them, had the identity $D_k = A_{k-1} \cdot \mathcal T_k$, in which $\mathcal T_k$ is a series built from single trees, and had worked with it in this form. The record divided by $A_{k-1}$ instead.

Let $c(m,k)$ be the number of prime parking functions (section 4.2) of length $m$ with all displacements below $k$. Then $1/A_{k-1} = 1 - \sum_m c(m,k) \cdot x^m/m!$, and comparing coefficients in $D_k/A_{k-1} = \mathcal T_k$ gives, for $n \ge k + 1$,

$$d_{n,k} = (k+1) \cdot d_{n-1,k} + 2 \cdot b_{n-1,k-1} + Y(n,k) + \sum_{m=1}^{n-k} \binom{n}{m} \cdot c(m,k) \cdot d_{n-m,k}.$$

The series $\mathcal T_k$ has a part that is linear in the two series $\log(A_k/A_{k-1})$ and $\log(A_{k-1}/A_{k-2})$ and a part of second and higher order in them. The coefficients of the linear part are the contributions $\delta_t$ of single trees (section 5.2), and since $\delta_n = (k+1) \cdot d_{n-1,k} + 2 \cdot b_{n-1,k-1}$ for $n \ge k + 1$, they give the first two terms on the right. The term $Y(n,k)$ is the coefficient of the part of higher order; it is non-negative, and it vanishes for $n \lt 2k$,[^522] so that in the rows $k + 1 \le n \le 2k - 1$ the column equation has no such term.[^523] Here $b_{k,k}$ is read as 0, so that the column begins with the step $d_{k,k} = -b_{k,k-1} = -k^{k-2}$. From here on this identity is the *column equation*.

Every coefficient in it is non-negative, and after a suitable exponential tilt its kernel, the coefficients $c(m,k) \cdot x^m/m!$ with which the earlier rows of the column enter, is non-increasing in $m$. The only negative input is the first step of the column: the "shock" of the tree of exactly the threshold size, that is, with exactly $k$ vertices (section 4.4). In the form multiplied by $A_{k-1}$ the kernel had been log-convex, and arguments by order, of which section 5.2 tells, had pointed the wrong way.[^524] A column of steps is thus the response of a positive linear system to one negative shock, with positive source terms, the terms of the equation that do not depend on the other rows of the column, added at each row; (C) and (Col) say that the response, once it has recovered, does not dip again.

The same record proved a lower bound along the diagonal, the *diagonal lower bound* of the later sections:

$$b_{n,k} \ge (1 + 1/k)^{k-2} \cdot n \cdot b_{n-1,k-1} \quad \text{for all } k \ge 2 \text{ and } n \ge k + 1,$$

with equality only at $n = k + 1$. It follows from the fact that the maximum displacement of a random parking function of length $n$ lies, in distribution, between that for length $n - 1$ and that plus one.[^525]

### 6.3 The paper arrives

The paper of section 5.3 reached the instance in the same night. At 00:44 on 20 November its six packages were inserted (section 5.4), and between 05:43 and 11:26 a peer and a review turned each into a record.[^234] No statement of the paper was refused. The peers and reviews noted that parts of it were re-proofs of what the instance had already published, and that its first theorem was weaker than one the instance had. The peers re-derived its proofs, re-ran its computations, and tied its objects to the instance's.

The package on the reduction mattered most; it became a record at 06:12.[^235] Its peer replaced the six nodes the package proposed by four that fitted the frontier as it stood: Target T, the open inequality to which the paper reduces the conjecture (section 5.3), in the paper's form; its restriction (T_R) to the right half; a statement (CI) on where a column crosses, which the next paragraphs state; and (X), the strict positivity of the cross product of section 5.3 at every position with $n \ge 3$ and $1 \le k \le n - 2$. Seven relations were accepted with them, among them (CI) $\Rightarrow$ (Mono).

The cross product had by then come to the later instance twice. It is the quantity $S$ of section 4.5. It came once as the "boundary response" of the report on the Borel chain, one of the five reports that were written between the two instances and handed over to the later one (section 5.2); that report had it from the manuscript, the text in which the earlier instance's proof was written up (section 5.1). It came a second time as the "cross product" of the paper (section 5.3), which was also written between the instances and which the later instance received on its second day. The record that brought in the paper's reduction identifies the two.[^64] The node (X) asks more than the node that the earlier instance had for the same quantity. That node states $S \ge 0$ for $k \ge 3$ and $k + 2 \le n \le 2k - 1$, the positions of the right half as its record delimits it;[^65] (X) asks for strict positivity, and at every position of a row. (X) stayed unresolved, and it was not needed for the root (section 6.9).

The statement (CI) concerns the paper's curve in the plane of steps. Write $r = n - k$ for the deficit. The curve $k = r^{3/2}$ separates two regions of the plane. Where $k \ge r^{3/2}$, that is, at deficits up to $k^{2/3}$, the paper's Theorem 5.9 holds when the deficit is at least 2: a step that is not positive is followed in its row only by negative steps. One part of the theorem's proof is certified by computation, the paper's rational computation of section 5.3, which the record takes over from the paper's appendix.[^526] The other region, $k \lt r^{3/2}$, is the region of Target T.

A column begins, at deficit 0, in the first region and leaves it at deficit about $k^{2/3}$. By an expectation that the record of the paper's reduction reads from the limit law of the maximum displacement and does not prove, the peak of a large row lies at a deficit of the same order and smaller than that of the curve, so that the curve would run just outside the peaks.[^527] By the limiting value that the records give for large $k$, of which they prove no finite form, the step of column $k$ turns positive at a deficit of about $0.6 \cdot k^{2/3}$, still inside the first region. And in the computed rows, as evidence and not as part of a proof, the inequality of Target T, $d_{n,k} \ge 0$ wherever $k^2 \lt (n-k)^3$, holds in every row from 95 to 600, so that no step outside the first region is negative there, and it fails in 81 of the rows up to 94.[^528]

(CI) is the statement that the column has turned positive by the first row outside the first region:

 (CI) for all $k \ge 260$, $d_{n,k} \gt 0$ at $n = k + r_c(k)$,

where $r_c(k)$ is the least positive integer $r$ with $r^3 \gt k^2$.

Column 260 is the first for which that row lies above the paper's table: there $k + r_c(k) = 301$. For the columns 260 to 264 the table shows the crossing and not the row of (CI). These five columns cross at rows 295 to 300, namely at the rows 295, 296, 298, 299 and 300, and the row of (CI) lies above the table, at the rows 301, 302, 303, 305 and 306. A theorem of the record of 02:22 carries the positive sign from the one to the other: a column that has once turned positive stays positive through a stretch of rows after its crossing that reaches at least to deficit $\lfloor 2k/5 \rfloor + 1$, and for every $k \ge 16$ the row $k + r_c(k)$ lies inside that stretch.[^236] So for these five columns (CI) was settled from the start, by the table together with this theorem, and what was open began at 265.

Given (CI), a column that has not yet turned positive at row $n$ is at a position where Theorem 5.9 applies, so the steps of row $n$ beyond it are negative; in particular column $k + 1$ has not turned positive at row $n$ either, which is (Mono). The proof of the relation (CI) $\Rightarrow$ (Mono) also reads facts from the paper's rows $n \le 300$.

The other effect of the paper was the table. From 05:14 records used the exact rows $n \le 300$ as a proof step. The first to do so[^237] thereby proved (L) for the columns up to 147, where the base table had reached 11. By the fourth input this was permitted (section 5.4). Whether a particular use of it was acceptable became the main subject of the user's later interventions.

One incident of that morning interrupted the work. At 08:58 a sub-reviewer, to stop a script of its own, ran a command that ends every Python process by name, and with it ended the runner and twelve sessions. The runner was restarted at 09:15, and an operator notice was added to the standing inputs at 09:23.[^238] Four sessions in production were handed to their peers where they stood, and two peers and six reviews were started again by new sessions.

### 6.4 Proofs accepted and set back, 20 November

During 20 November both parts of the root were tied to the first layer of a column, and in the evening the first solutions of the instance were accepted and, the same evening, set back. The reviews found the proofs told in this subsection correct, and their claims stayed published and citable throughout; three of the proofs were accepted as solutions of nodes on that evening and set back within two hours. Where the text tells something that is no part of those proofs, it says so at the statement.

For the left half, the rows $n \ge 2k + 1$, a record of 10:45[^239] split the step with the critical tilt. With $\mathfrak r_k = (1 + 1/k)^{k-1}$, the reciprocal of the tilt $x_k$ of section 5.2,

$$d_{n,k} = [ b_{n,k} - \mathfrak r_k \cdot n \cdot b_{n-1,k-1} ] + [ \mathfrak r_k \cdot n \cdot b_{n-1,k-1} - b_{n,k-1} ],$$

a diagonal bracket and a column bracket. The constant $\mathfrak r_k$ is larger than that of the diagonal lower bound of section 6.2 by the factor $1 + 1/k$, so the diagonal bracket is a new requirement and not a consequence of that bound.

The diagonal bracket is non-negative for all $n \ge 2k + 1$ as soon as certain tilted sums over the rows $k + 1$ to $2k + 1$ are; for the column bracket no such finite reduction held in general. This made two nodes. (CB) says that the column bracket is non-negative for $k \ge 148$ and $n \ge 2k + 1$, which is the inequality $b_{n,k-1} \le (1 + 1/k)^{k-1} \cdot n \cdot b_{n-1,k-1}$. (Base) is the condition on the tilted sums, also for $k \ge 148$: for every $i$ with $k + 1 \le i \le 2k + 1$, the sum over the rows from $i$ to $2k + 1$ of the diagonal bracket, weighted by $x_k^m/m!$ and taken in the form that the node gives it, is non-negative; the table of section 6.10 has the exact statement.[^240] The bound 148 is not a property of the inequality of (CB), which in the computed rows holds from $k = 10$. The columns up to 147 had been closed for (L) from the paper's table that morning, and the nodes were posed for the columns that remained.

(CB) has the constant of the growth inequality that the earlier instance had proved on 10 November for the cumulative counts $A$ and every $k \ge 2$ (section 4.8). That inequality is one of the conditions of the handed-over critical-tilt propagation, and the first two records of the later instance had proved it again for every $k \ge 2$.[^241] (CB) is the corresponding bound for the exact counts $b_{\cdot,k-1}$, a different statement, and its first proofs worked only for large $k$.

A record of 12:24[^242] added (D), a convexity along the diagonal, and with it (E), an upper bound for the left side of (D) that the table of section 6.10 states. (D) says that a certain weighted count is convex along the diagonal. The count is of the parking functions of length $t$ with all displacements below $c$ in which the last car takes the last spot; for $t \ge c$ there are $c \cdot A_{t-1,c-1}$ of them. The weight is $x_k^t/t!$. Convex along the diagonal means convex when $t$ and $c$ are raised together from $(t-1, k-1)$ through $(t, k)$ to $(t+1, k+1)$. Written out, the node is

$$(k+1) \cdot x_k^2 \cdot A_{t,k} - 2k(t+1) \cdot x_k \cdot A_{t-1,k-1} + (k-1) \cdot t(t+1) \cdot A_{t-2,k-2} \ge 0 \quad \text{for all } k \ge 301 \text{ and } k + 1 \le t \le 2k - 1.$$

Multiplied by the positive factor $x_k^{t-1}/(t+1)!$, the left side of this display is the weighted count at $(t+1, k+1)$, minus twice the weighted count at $(t, k)$, plus the weighted count at $(t-1, k-1)$, which is the second difference along the diagonal.[^243] The bound 301 comes from the relation for which (D) was posed: its proof took the rows $n \le 600$ from the paper's larger table, and a position of the right half in a row $n \ge 601$ has $k \ge 301$.

These four nodes of the day, (CB), (Base), (D) and (E), were not needed for the root as it was proved: none of the proofs of the root that this account tells rests on any of the four nodes.[^529] (Base) and (E) stayed unresolved; sections 6.7 to 6.9 tell what became of each.

For the right half a record of 14:33[^244] bounded the crossing row from above without computation: $q_0(k) \le k + \lfloor 27k/100 \rfloor$ for every $k \ge 265$, the first column whose crossing the rows $n \le 300$ do not show. This is the *entry theorem*.

Its argument looks at column $k - 1$ in the rows in which column $k$ is still negative. Suppose that no step of column $k$ is positive in the rows up to row $N = k + R$, the row of deficit $R$. In each of these rows $d_{n,k} \le 0$ says that $b_{n,k-1} \ge b_{n,k}$, and the diagonal lower bound of section 6.2 then gives $b_{n,k-1} \ge (1 + 1/k)^{k-2} \cdot n \cdot b_{n-1,k-1}$. So the entries $b_{n,k-1}$ of column $k - 1$, each divided by $n!$ and by the $n$-th power of the constant of that bound, do not decrease along the rows up to $N$. These normalized entries obey an equation. It comes from an equation of the record of 02:22 that the entries $b_{n,\ell}$ of a single column $\ell$ satisfy, the counterpart for entries of the column equation for steps: the entry of a row is a positive source term, a cumulative count, plus a combination with positive coefficients of the entries of the rows before it. Used at the last row $N$, where no earlier entry is larger than the last, the equation bounds the last entry from above. Summed over all the rows from $k$ to $N$, it bounds the same entry from below. Both bounds compare the last entry with one cumulative count, which drops out when the two are set against each other, and what is left is an inequality between $k$ and $R$ alone. The two bounds contradict each other once that inequality holds, and its explicit form holds at $R = \lfloor 27k/100 \rfloor$ for all $k \ge 265$. To leading order, by a formal expansion that is no part of the proof, the inequality reads $R^2/(k + R) \gt \sqrt{2R/\pi}$.[^530]

A second criterion in the same record, due to its peer, works on the column equation of section 6.2 for column $k$ itself, in solved form. For the rows $k + 1 \le n \le 2k - 1$ that form reads

$$d_{n,k} = -w(n,k) \cdot k^{k-2} + \sum_{t=k+1}^{n} w(n,t) \cdot [ 2 \cdot b_{t-1,k-1} - (t-k-1) \cdot d_{t-1,k} ],$$

in which $w(n,t) = \binom{n}{t} \cdot (n+1-t)^{n-t}$ are weights.[^245] The first term is the response to the single negative shock, the first step $-k^{k-2}$ of the column. In the sum the entries $b_{t-1,k-1}$ of column $k - 1$ enter with the weights $w(n,t)$, and the earlier steps of column $k$ itself enter with the coefficient $-(t-k-1)$, which is not positive, so that an earlier step that is not positive contributes a term that is not negative.[^246] While the column is non-positive, the feedback of its own earlier rows can therefore only help: its step at row $N$ is at least a weighted sum of entries of column $k - 1$ minus the response to the single negative shock, and the column has turned positive by row $N$ as soon as explicit lower bounds for column $k - 1$ make that difference positive. This second criterion is the one behind the first proof of (CI) and behind two theorems of 21 November that section 6.8 uses.

At 17:59 the first solution of the instance was accepted, for (CI), and with it (Mono) stood by relation.[^247] At 19:10 solutions for (C) and its companion (Col₁), column statements of the right half, were accepted, and (S_R) and (T_R) stood by relation.[^248]

The second of these records proves (C) and (Col₁) in two ranges of columns. For $k \ge 151$ it has a theorem: a column which is positive at some row of deficit at most $27k/100$ stays positive up to row $2k$. The theorem is proved from the two column equations and the diagonal lower bound alone, with the place of the crossing taken from the entry theorem for $k \ge 265$ and from the table below that. The two equations are the column equation of section 6.2, for the steps of column $k$, and the equation for the entries of a single column on which the entry theorem rests, used here too for column $k - 1$.[^249] For $k \le 150$ every case is read from the table. For $k \ge 151$ one sequence then governs the rows from $k$ to $2k$ of a column: the sign of $d_{n,k}$ for $k \le n \le 2k$ is simply whether $n \ge q_0(k)$, and the open statements about the right half are statements about $q_0$, that it does not decrease, or that it stays below the curve.[^531]

The right half thus stood proved, informally, about thirty-one hours after the start. It did not stay so. The accepted proof of (CI) rested on 48 claims of nine records, on a list of explicit estimates, made by hand, with a margin of 1.3 per cent for $k \ge 537$, and on the paper's larger table, the rows up to 600, for the columns below that.

The user's fifth standing input was submitted at 18:54, between the two acceptances.[^250] Its text of that hour is not preserved; what is told here is the file as it was last written, at 20:07. What the input asks is this. A node is not to be treated as informally proved when, before formalization, it is clear that its logic and steps can be made simpler, that a computational proof in it can be removed or narrowed, that a premise can be removed or reduced, or that part of it can be replaced by citing something already proved.

Everything else in the input is the operator's text. It adds that such a proof stays published and its claims stay citable; what it does not yet receive is the status of the node's solution. It says that the user gave the condition without stating a reason. And it marks some of its passages as the operator's own account or reading and not the user's words. One of them is its account of the occasion, which speaks of formalization: a formalization carries over everything that a proof contains, so that whatever the proof could have done without becomes cost. Another gives examples of room that is clear, among them a proof that leans on a wide range of computed results while its argument uses only a narrow part of them.[^251]

The operator then set back the three solutions just accepted, at 19:50 on the user's instruction and at 20:07 by the user's decision on the operator's recommendation. It did so by editing the reviews' verdicts on them directly and marking the edits as the operator's and not a new review.[^252] Six nodes returned to unresolved. At 19:50 these were (CI) and with it (Mono), (T_R) and (S_R), which had stood by relations that have (CI) among their premises or behind them; at 20:07 they were (Col₁) and (C).

The input has a section on this first application, and it gives the grounds. For (CI), the user had the acceptance undone when the operator reported what the accepted proof rested on: the 48 claims, the list of estimates and the larger table. For (Col₁) and (C), the operator recommended the same, on what the review of their record had itself written: that the computation among the steps of the proof is the paper's rows $n \le 300$ and that a formalization would need the facts of those rows or another way to cover the columns $k \le 264$, and that the record had obtained three published results again without citing them. The user did not say where in the three proofs there was room, the input states. As places to start from it offers what the two records and their reviews had noted themselves, for (CI) also the record's own remark that two of its claims are the same criterion and the same equation as those of the record of 14:33, and it adds that the room is not confined to these places.[^253] Section 6.7 tells what took the place of the three proofs: for (C) and (Col₁) a proof that assembles three stages published since the first night, and for (CI) a proof that uses no table.

### 6.5 What "shorter" means, 21 November

The night that followed and the next day are the part of the research in which the user's hand is most visible, and in which the shape of the final proof was fixed. The proofs of this subsection were all found correct by their reviews; what the subsection follows is what became of them as solutions, and of two relations whose acceptance was withdrawn.

At 23:42 on 20 November a record[^254] proved (Mono) again, the statement that a column does not turn positive before the column to its left has, and this time without (CI) and without the paper's rational computation or its larger table. Its idea was to widen the paper's Theorem 5.9 from the curve to a strip.

With $u = r^{3/2}/k$, the region of that theorem is $u \le 1$, and the cross product is positive not only there but for $u \le 11/5$ (when $r \ge 55$, and for every $r \ge 2$ in the columns $k \ge 265$). This is shown by a criterion function $G(r,k)$ in closed form, an explicit elementary function of $r$ and $k$. In the terms of section 4.5 the cross product is $S = B_+ \cdot \Delta - B_- \cdot \Delta'$, so that it is positive exactly when the ratio $B_+ \cdot \Delta/(B_- \cdot \Delta')$ is larger than 1; and $G$ is a lower bound for $k$ times the logarithm of that ratio.[^532] For fixed $r$, $G$ increases with $k$. And every column $k \ge 265$ turns positive inside that strip:

$$q_0(k) \le k + \lfloor (11k/5)^{2/3} \rfloor + 1.$$

This inequality is the *strip bound*. It is a theorem of the same record, proved without computation by the criterion of the entry theorem (section 6.4): the explicit conditions that had been shown there to hold at $R = \lfloor 27k/100 \rfloor$ hold also at $R = \lfloor (11k/5)^{2/3} \rfloor + 1$, for every $k \ge 265$.[^255] So a column that has not yet turned positive is still in the strip, where the cross product forces the next step to be negative. The record made of this a node of its own, (Mono_265), the statement (Mono) for columns $k \ge 265$, proved with no computation at all, and its review accepted both, the solution of (Mono) and that of the new node.

The columns below 265 were at no stage all closed by argument. This record took them from the rows $n \le 300$ of the paper's table. The relation by which (Mono) later followed from (Mono_265) has theorems without table for $97 \le k \le 264$ and reads the table for $k \le 96$. And the Lean proof of (Mono) has the Lean kernel compute two signs in each of the columns 9 to 264 and 39 facts for the columns 1 to 8.

At 01:39, on the user's instruction, the operator set back the solutions of (Mono) and (Mono_265) as well,[^256] and with them (S_R), which had stood again by relation since 23:42. The review had judged that the proof had no room left, from the number of claims it depended on and from the size of its margins; so, at first, had the operator, when the user asked it to read the proof. Told by the user that this was a comparison of numbers and not a judgment made from the text, the operator followed the proof step by step and found room; by the operator's own note, no review had examined the finding.[^533]

What it found concerns the record's Theorem 4.2, which shows $G \gt 0$ in four cases, each for a range of the deficit $r$ and of the column $k$: $r \ge 42$ with $2 \cdot r^{3/2} \le 3k$; $r \ge 55$ with $5 \cdot r^{3/2} \le 11k$; $r \ge 10$ with $k \ge r^{3/2}$; and $2 \le r \le 9$ with $k \ge 86$.[^257] At the positions that the proof of (Mono_265) uses, the first, the third and the fourth of these between them cover the deficits $r \le 54$, the first being needed there for the deficits 42 to 54,[^534] and the second covers the deficits from 55 on. The point of the finding is that $G$ increases with $k$: to show $G \gt 0$ at a position it is enough to show it, for the same deficit, at the smallest $k$ that such a position can have. Each of the three cases takes $k$ at the smallest value that the case itself allows; but every position that the proof of (Mono_265) uses has $k \ge 265$, so that one could put $k = 265$ into $G$. For $k \ge 265$, then, the three cases that cover the deficits $r \le 54$, with their table of constants, could be replaced by two lines, and the second case, for $r \ge 55$, remains.[^258]

By this account's reading, the shortened proof published seven hours later shows what this comes to. A position at which column $k$ has not yet turned positive has deficit $r \le (11k/5)^{2/3}$, that is, $k \ge (5/11) \cdot r^{3/2}$. The smallest $k$ that a position of deficit $r$ can have in the columns from 265 on is therefore the larger of 265 and $(5/11) \cdot r^{3/2}$, and since $(5/11) \cdot r^{3/2} \le 265$ exactly for $r \le 69$, it is $k = 265$ as long as $r \le 69$. Since $G(r,k)$ increases with $k$, it is enough to show $G \gt 0$ at this smallest admissible $k$ for each $r$. The two bounds on the deficit belong to two things: 54 is the last deficit at which the proof uses the three cases that the operator's finding replaces, and 69 is the last deficit at which the shortened proof puts $k = 265$.[^535]

Five minutes after the set-back the sixth standing input recorded what the user had said about that way of judging:[^259] sessions are to read and examine the original texts and make mathematical judgments from their actual content, and not to compute simple surface indicators and judge through them as a proxy.

The effect was immediate, and of two kinds. The first was in the reviews. For the refusals that follow, the ground that the reviews themselves give is in each case the fifth input.[^260] Two of them add that, as the sixth requires, the judgment was made by reading the text.[^261] A review had refused one solution as still shortenable, under the fifth input, already at 23:33 on 20 November; from 04:41 on reviews did so regularly with solutions that they had verified as correct, each time naming the step that could go.[^262] The solutions refused were those of the diagonal convexity (D) twice, of (Col), of the right-half diagonal statement (Diag₁), and of (CB).[^263]

Each of these reviews names more than one place. The first that each names is a step that the proof could do without:

- in the earlier of the two proofs of (D), one proposition;[^264]
- in the proof of (Col), the columns 160 to 300, which were proved again where published claims give them in three lines;[^265]
- in the later proof of (D), one of its lemmas, which the proof could do without if it passed through a published reduction;[^266]
- in the proof of (Diag₁), a hypothesis that the proof uses at two rows only, both of which a published claim covers;[^267]
- in the proof of (CB), the longest estimate, which could be replaced by five lines.[^268]

The second kind of effect was that thresholds fell in the same night and the following morning. One record proved that every column $k \ge 350$ changes sign exactly once, where a theorem published the evening before had reached this only for $k \ge 10^8$.[^269] What had changed was the method, by the later record's own comparison. The theorem of the evening before had closed its columns, in the later record's words, by "estimates of the bulk and a lower bound for the absorption in the deep rows",[^536] and its own record says of the threshold $10^8$ that it is where the estimates end.[^270] The theorem of 04:41, by the same comparison, uses no "estimates of the bulk": it keeps in every row one weighting, built on the constant of the diagonal lower bound, and its criterion contains numbers of the kernel of the column equation alone.[^271] Another proved (CB) for every $k \ge 148$ with no table, where the previous range had been $k \ge 30000$;[^272] section 6.7 tells its argument. At 06:19 an organizer could write that at the level of supported claims the whole root statement was now proved, through (Col) and (Mono) and the paper's tables alone, while on the frontier every one of twenty-four nodes was unresolved.[^273] The twenty-four are the root and the twenty-three other nodes.[^274]

At 08:24 a shortened proof of (Mono_265) was accepted and stood,[^275] and that afternoon the node was formally proved (section 6.6). In place of the four cases of the first proof, three of which the operator had found replaceable, it has one lemma in two cases: for integers $r \ge 2$ and real $k \ge \max(265, (5/11) \cdot r^{3/2})$, $G(r,k) \gt 0$. From the lemma it follows that, for $k \ge 265$ and $n \ge k + 2$, if column $k$ is non-positive through row $n$, then the cross product at $(n,k)$ is positive and $d_{n,k+1} \lt 0$. The proof uses no table, no computation of the paper and no other node.

Its review says that this node "is where that condition arose". By the sentence before it in the review, the condition meant is the sixth input: the review of the first proof and then the operator had judged from the number of dependencies and the size of the margins that nothing could be shortened, and room appeared when the operator followed the proof step by step. This time, the review says, it read what each step assumes and uses and asked at each place how it could be shortened.

The same record had noticed something about the proof by which (S_R) followed from (Mono_265). That proof took a bound on $q_0$ from the entry theorem, with its own list of constants, although the strip bound which the premise already uses implies it: $\lfloor (11k/5)^{2/3} \rfloor + 1 \le \lfloor 27k/100 \rfloor$ for all $k \ge 265$. The operator asked the user whether the standard of the fifth input should apply to the proofs of relations too. The user's answer, quoted in the seventh standing input of 08:36, was "apply the same standard to proofs of relations",[^276] and one minute later the operator withdrew the acceptance of the two relations by which (S_R) followed, from (Mono_265) and from (Mono).[^277] (S_R) was unresolved again. (Mono_265) remained proved, the only proved node of the instance, and for the moment connected to nothing.

The seventh input carries a text of the operator's as well. Its section on what occasioned it speaks of formalization: a formalization carries over the whole of a proof that joins the proof of a relation to the proofs of its premises, so that what the proof of the relation could do without is carried into the proof of the conclusion.[^278] Beside the room that the record had found, the input names a second kind, which it says the operator saw in reading the statement of a claim and which no review had examined: the proof of the relation from (Mono_265) to (S_R) cites a claim of several sentences and uses one of them, and if it cites that claim whole, the claims on which the unused sentences rest enter the dependencies of the proof of (S_R).[^279]

That morning the standard overreached, and the user corrected it. Between 09:56 and 11:20 six records proposed nine solutions, for (L), (Col), (C), (Mono) and others; every review found the proof correct, and every review refused the solution under the fifth input.[^280] Some of the grounds were of the kind the user had meant: the same bound obtained twice, a step not needed. Others followed glosses that the operator had attached to the fifth and seventh inputs: that the reason for shortening is the cost of formalization, so that citing a whole claim while using one sentence of it counts against a proof; and that a proof should read fewer cells of a table.

The eighth standing input, at 12:38, withdrew those glosses in the user's words.[^281] On citing part of a claim it says that if it is needed, even in part, there is no reason not to use it. On the reason it quotes the user: "I did not say to reduce them because declared dependencies are a cost of formalization. If it can be reduced, that is more concise; that is why." On computation it says: the trouble is not that a proof leans on a wide range of computed results; what is wanted is that, even where a computation can show at once that something is true, the more mathematical research that explains why it is true be continued where possible; a solution closed by computation should not yet be accepted, so that the research goes on; and what was meant was crude, large-scale computation done without understanding, not a ban on all computation. This text replaced a first version of the input, which by the input's own account stood among the inputs of the instance from 12:25 to 12:34 and was received by the sessions started in those minutes; the first version had said that no solution with a part closed by computation is accepted, which the input calls the operator's wide reading of the user's words and not what the user meant.[^282] The input changed no verdict; it changed how the next ones were made.

One more correction completed the standard. In the afternoon a peer held back from proposing a relation from (Mono_265) to (S_R), and its review agreed with that reading of the eighth input, because the relation's finite remainder, 43 signs and 3,240 positions in rows $n \le 162$, would be read from the paper's table. The ninth standing input, at 18:08, quotes the user: "I think this much is not large-scale computation".[^283] Half an hour before, at 17:41, a review had drawn the same line from its other side.[^284] The node before that review was the size convexity (L1°): for a fixed $k \ge 12$, the share of the parking functions of length $n$ whose displacements are all smaller than $k$ is convex as a function of $n$ from row $2k + 1$ on, that is, its second difference in $n$ is non-negative at every $n \ge 2k + 1$. Section 6.9 comes back to this node, and the table of section 6.10 has its formula and its range.[^285] Two proofs of it were offered, one citing 29 exact cells of the base table and one free of computation but longer, and the review accepted the first: replacing a small permitted computation by a longer estimate by hand is not a shortening.

By the evening of 21 November the standard under which the root would be proved was therefore this. A proof is the solution of a node when it is correct and no simpler way to the same conclusion is in sight, judged from the argument itself. A small finite remainder read from the paper's table is acceptable. A part closed by a large computation that does not explain why it is true is not yet acceptable, however true.

### 6.6 The first Lean proof

On 21 November at 15:02 the instance's first formal proof was published.[^286] It is the theorem `Parking.mono265`, the statement of (Mono_265), which is the restriction of (Mono) to the columns $k \ge 265$ and whose shortened proof had been accepted that morning (section 6.5). It has 26 modules and about twelve thousand lines and uses no table. The theorem is formally proved: the review of the record reports that it compiled the modules itself and that the theorem depends on no axioms beyond the three standard ones.[^537] The informal proofs that this section sets beside the Lean proof are accepted as claims: a review of the instance supported each of them, and nothing more is claimed for them here.

How the proof was built matters for everything formal that followed, since every later formal record starts from the same file of definitions. The definitions are those of the root: a preference is a function from Fin $n$ to Fin $n$, cars park by the first-free-spot rule, and $b$ and $A$ are cardinalities of sets of such functions. They stand in one file, which every later formal record copies unchanged and every later review compares with the root.[^538]

From the process the development proves one combinatorial fact, the last-car recurrence of section 4.2, in the form $A_{n+1,k} = \sum_j \min(j+1,k+1) \cdot \binom{n}{j} \cdot A_{j,k} \cdot A_{n-j,k}$. Beside it, four elementary facts are proved directly from the definitions: $b_{n,0} = A_{n,0}$; $b_{n,\ell+1} + A_{n,\ell} = A_{n,\ell+1}$; $A_{0,k} = 1$; and $A_{n,k} \le A_{n,k'}$ for $k \le k'$. The second of the four is the relation $b_{n,\ell} = A_{n,\ell} - A_{n,\ell-1}$, which section 2.1 has at once from the definitions; in Lean it is a lemma that has to be proved, because both counts are defined there as cardinalities, each by itself.

Everything else is algebra of sequences and formal power series built on that recurrence. A "single-chain lemma" gives, for $n \le 2k + 1$, the column equation and the diagonal lower bound of section 6.2, and the expansion of a step as a curvature term minus a boundary term (the split $d_{n,k} = \Delta - B_-$ of section 4.5). The lemma is named after the range in which, in the forest, the subtrees above the level lie on one chain (section 6.2); in the record's account of the formal proof that fact appears only as algebra, as the statement that the square of a certain power series has no term of degree up to $2k + 1$. The range $n \le 2k + 1$ is that of the lemma itself, which speaks of one level.
