The Lean theorems drawn from it combine neighbouring levels and have narrower ranges: the column equation and the split for $k + 1 \le n \le 2k - 1$, the rows in which the extra term $Y$ of the equation vanishes, and the diagonal lower bound for $k + 1 \le n \le 2k$.[^287] The single chain has, as the instance defines it, two ranges: $n \le 2k + 1$ for a statement about one level, and $n \le 2k - 1$, as in section 6.2, for formulas that take a second difference in the level.[^288]

The cross product is the quantity of section 5.3 on whose sign the proof of (Mono_265) turns (section 6.5). Its estimate is a statement about weighted compositions. Cut a segment of length $m$ into consecutive parts, give a part of length $j$ the weight $j^{j-1}/j!$ and each cut point $s$ a factor $f(s)$, and let $V_q$ be the total weight of the compositions with $q$ parts, the weight of a composition being the product of the weights of its parts and of the factors of its cut points. Compositions come in through the single chain of the first layer (section 6.2). In the instance's terms, the segment is the lowest subtree of the chain, with $m$ the number of vertices by which that subtree falls short of $n + 1$, and the cut points are the larger subtrees above it, measured in the same way.[^289] On a cut point $s$ the parking problem puts the factor $\gamma/(n + 1 - s)$, where $\gamma$ is the variable of the frozen polynomial of section 4.5, and the boundary terms $B_-$ and $B_+$ of that section are, up to a factor, total weights of this kind over all the compositions of a segment.[^290]

Two facts about such compositions carry the estimate, and each has a name. The *erasing rule* removes one cut point at a time. From a composition with $q + 1$ parts, choose one of the $m$ unit cells of the segment and one of the $q$ cut points, independently and uniformly, and erase the first cut point that is met on the way from the cell to the chosen cut point. When every factor is 1, this rule carries the compositions with $q + 1$ parts, drawn with probabilities proportional to their weights, to the compositions with $q$ parts drawn in the same way.[^539] The *comparison with the middle factor* concerns the factors $1/(N - s)$ with $N \gt m$, which are, up to the constant factor $\gamma$, those of the parking problem. The proofs before it had replaced these factors by their values at the two ends of the range, 1 or the smallest value. The comparison says that they may be replaced by their value at the middle, $s = m/2$: the number of parts of a composition drawn with the true factors is at least as large, in the order of likelihood ratios, as it is when every factor has the middle value. It rests on the erasing rule, since the factor that one more part brings is the factor at the mean position of the cut point which the rule erases; without the factors that mean position is exactly the middle, because the parts can be exchanged, and the factors move it only towards where they are larger. The comparison came with the strip bound of section 6.5.[^293]

The proof needs three comparisons between $V_q$ and $V_{q+1}$, for three choices of the factors. If every factor is 1, then $q \cdot m \cdot V_{q+1} = (q+1)(m-q) \cdot V_q$; this is the erasing rule in its form for counts. If the factors lie between 0 and 1, then $q \cdot m \cdot V_{q+1} \le (q+1)(m-q) \cdot V_q$; this follows from the erasing rule, because the cut points of the composition with $q$ parts are among those of the composition with $q + 1$ parts, and the cut point that is lost takes with it a factor of at most 1.[^292] And for the factors $1/(N - s)$ with $N \gt m$ the inequality runs the other way: $(q+1)(m-q) \cdot V_q \le q \cdot m \cdot (N - m/2) \cdot V_{q+1}$; this is the comparison with the middle factor.[^291] The informal proofs had obtained the two facts by separate arguments, the erasing rule by a walk on compositions and the comparison with the middle factor by an involution that exchanges the lengths of two parts.

In Lean all three come from one identity. Let $P$ be the strictly upper triangular matrix with entries $P_{ij} = (j-i)^{j-i-1}/(j-i)!$ for $i \lt j$, and $\delta P$ the matrix with entries $(j-i) \cdot P_{ij}$. Then $(\delta P) \cdot P = P \cdot (\delta P) = \delta P - P$, which is a case of Abel's binomial identity. The quantity $V_q$ is an entry of a product of copies of $P$ and of the diagonal matrix of the factors, and the identity is applied between the factors of that product.

Forests, the picture of section 4.2 on which every informal proof of these facts rests, do not occur in the Lean source at all. The same holds of the final proof of the root, which section 6.9 tells. The audit of what the final theorem, `Parking.root_unimodal`, depends on lists these from the modules that speak of the process: the definitions, the recurrence with the lemmas of its proof, and the four elementary facts named above.[^294]

Two thirds of the lines came from a formalizer session that had been cancelled. It had been started on the first proof of (Mono_265) on 20 November at 23:42 and stopped at 01:37, as that proof was being set back (section 6.5; the commit that records the set-back is of 01:39). Its unpublished work was the base on which a second formalizer formalized the shortened proof.

### 6.7 The right half stands

In the night of 21 to 22 November the nodes began to stand under the settled standard, and by 10:48 the right half was proved. Proved means here that the node (S_R), the right half of the root, was accepted by relation. Three nodes had received accepted solutions, (C) and (Col₁) at 04:11 and (CI) at 10:48, and with the third of them (S_R) followed in two ways, through relations accepted earlier:[^295]

- (Col₁) $\wedge$ (CI) $\Rightarrow$ (T_R), and (T_R) $\Rightarrow$ (S_R);
- (C) $\wedge$ (CI) $\Rightarrow$ (Diag₁), and (Col₁) $\wedge$ (Diag₁) $\Rightarrow$ (S_R).

Here (T_R) is Target T restricted to the right half, and (Diag₁) is the diagonal statement (Diag) restricted to it. None of this was formal yet: the formal proofs of (C), (S_R), (CI) and (Col₁) came between that evening and the next morning (section 6.9).

The proofs told in this section are proofs in words that their reviews found correct: five were accepted as solutions of nodes, and others that were offered as solutions were refused as such and kept their claims published.[^540] Where a proof reads signs or positions from the paper's table or settles a few small cases by exact arithmetic, and where a statement told beside the proofs rests on a computation that belongs to no proof, the text says so at the statement.

At 23:31 (D) and (CB) received their first accepted solutions.[^296] (D) is the convexity of the tilted cumulative counts along a diagonal, for $k + 1 \le t \le 2k - 1$ (section 6.4), and (CB) is the inequality that makes the column bracket of that section non-negative. The two solutions were the first of the five of this section to be accepted. Each of the two nodes is one premise of an accepted relation, (D) towards (T_R) and (CB) towards (L), the statement about the left half to which section 6.8 comes. Both relations are side roads: neither was completed, because each has a premise that stayed unresolved. In the first it is the node (E), an upper bound for the left side of (D) in the rows after the first row beyond the curve of section 6.3, up to row $2k - 1$. In the second it is the node (Base), the condition on tilted sums of section 6.4. The theorem by which (D) was proved returns in section 6.8.

The proof of (D) is a theorem that holds in every column $k \ge 3$; it is called the tilted convexity theorem below. Fix $k$ and the critical tilt $x_k$ of section 4.8. Say that three positive numbers $y_1$, $y_2$, $y_3$ are *convex in the tilted sense* if

$$e^{\lambda} \cdot y_3 + e^{-\lambda} \cdot y_1 \ge 2 \cdot e^{\lambda/k} \cdot y_2 \quad \text{for every } \lambda \ge 0.$$

For $\lambda = 0$ this is $y_1 + y_3 \ge 2 y_2$, and for $\lambda \gt 0$ it asks that the convexity survive an exponential tilt, with a margin.

The theorem says that the weighted count of section 6.4, taken at three neighbouring points of a diagonal, is convex in the tilted sense. For a row $t$ with $k + 1 \le t \le 2k - 1$ the three numbers are $(k-1) \cdot x_k^{t-1} \cdot A_{t-2,k-2}/(t-1)!$, $k \cdot x_k^t \cdot A_{t-1,k-1}/t!$ and $(k+1) \cdot x_k^{t+1} \cdot A_{t,k}/(t+1)!$. Their second difference is the left side of (D) multiplied by $x_k^{t-1}/(t+1)!$, so that (D) is, for these three numbers, the inequality of the tilted sense at $\lambda = 0$. The theorem gives the inequality for every $\lambda \ge 0$, and the record reads this as the diagonal convexity at every tilt from the critical one upwards, with a margin.[^298] The inequality (D) thus holds for every $k \ge 3$.

The proof has three parts. First, the tilted Cayley sequence $\omega_n = (n+1)^{n-1} \cdot x_k^n/n!$ is convex in this sense at every position: any three consecutive terms $\omega_{n-1}$, $\omega_n$, $\omega_{n+1}$ satisfy the inequality.

Second, the property passes to sums of pairs. For each $\rho$ with $1 \le \rho \le k - 2$, put $q_M = \sum \omega_a \cdot \omega_b$ over $a + b = M$ with $a, b \ge \rho$; then the three numbers $q_M$ at $M = k + \rho - 2$, $k + \rho - 1$ and $k + \rho$ are convex in the tilted sense. Here the range of the sum grows with $M$, and an identity for convolutions makes the terms at the ends of the range cancel exactly. The record notes that this part has no computation in it at all.

Third, by the last-car recurrence the cumulative counts that enter (D) are built from such sums by positive combinations and by factors that preserve the property, a reduction published on the first evening.[^297] One further inequality is needed on the way, because in that reduction the sums of pairs carry a factor slightly larger than 1: what the factor leaves over has to be covered by the margin of the first term of the same expression, a single sequence. This inequality is closed by hand for $k \ge 7$ and by exact arithmetic in ten small cases for $3 \le k \le 6$.[^541]

The three proofs of (D) published before had all been confined to $k \ge 148$ or $k \ge 151$, thresholds that, in the record's words, came from the bookkeeping and not from the object. The node asks only for $k \ge 301$, so each of them had proved it; what was new is a proof with no threshold and, in the node's range, no table. Each of the three had been offered as the solution of the node, and each review had found the proof correct and had refused the solution under the fifth input, by which a proof that can clearly still be shortened is not yet the solution of a node. Each review named what could still go: in the first, among four places, a proposition that the argument did not need and premises that could be citations, in the second a lemma and several premises that a reduction already published would have spared, in the third a single paragraph.[^299] Two further independent proofs offered the same night were refused as solutions because this one existed.

(CB) says that the entries $b_{n,k-1}$ of column $k - 1$, weighted by $x_k^n/n!$, do not increase from row $2k + 1$ on. No accepted solution of another node came to rest on the statement (CB): neither the informal nor the formal proof of (L) uses it. The formal proof of (L) does not use the theorem that states (CB), but it takes definitions and lemmas from the record in which (CB) was formally proved, among them the lemma from which it has one of its ingredients in the columns 6 to 11.[^542] The proof of (CB) does not work on these entries. It works on the prime parking functions of section 4.2 whose maximum displacement is exactly $k - 1$. Their weighted counts satisfy a linear recurrence in which both the kernel of the recurrence and its source term, the term that does not depend on the other rows, decrease, and in such a recurrence a value that is no larger than all the values before it is never exceeded again. A count over an interval of rows shows that such a value occurs before row $k + \lceil 3k/5 \rceil$, and a criterion published with the node carries the resulting descent to column $k - 1$ in every row from $2k + 1$.

This was the argument of the record of 07:59 that morning,[^300] which had brought the range down from $k \ge 30000$ and had been refused because two of its estimates could be replaced by shorter ones. A second version in the afternoon[^301] moved the interval of the count, so that one line bounding the kernel of the column equation of section 6.2 replaced the longest estimate, and was refused for a single paragraph that proved something the induction did not need. The version accepted at 23:31 is the second version, that of the afternoon, without the paragraph and without one further refinement of a constant.

The record remarks that the threshold of (CB) is of another kind than that of (D): the inequality is false in the columns up to 9, where it is refuted by exact computation, tables of exact integers showing that it fails in two to five rows from row $2k + 1$, so that no proof without a threshold can exist.[^543]

At 04:11 (C) and (Col₁) were accepted,[^302] by a proof that contains no new argument and assembles three stages published since the first night. The record had offered two proofs of each node, and this is the one that the review accepted; the other, which rests on a new argument of the record's own, is told after it.[^303] (C) says that a column which has turned positive stays positive up to row $2k - 1$, and (Col₁) is (Col), by which a non-negative step stays non-negative in the next row of its column, restricted to the right half; both were posed on the first night (section 6.2).

The three stages work on one equation. Divide the column equation of section 6.2 by $n!$. In the rows $k + 1 \le n \le 2k - 1$ the equation has no extra term, and its coefficient $c(m,k)$ no longer depends on $k$: for $m \le k$ the restriction on the displacements is empty, so that $c(m,k)$ is the number $(m-1)^{m-1}$ of all prime parking functions of length $m$, and these rows have only such $m$.[^304] With $\zeta_n = d_{n,k}/n!$, $u_n = b_{n,k-1}/n!$ and $\varepsilon_m = (m-1)^{m-1}/m!$, it reads in these rows

$$\zeta_n = (2/n) \cdot u_{n-1} + ((k+1)/n) \cdot \zeta_{n-1} + \sum_m \varepsilon_m \cdot \zeta_{n-m}.$$

The step at row $n$ is a positive source term, an entry of column $k - 1$, plus what the earlier rows of the column feed back; the rows before the crossing are negative, so what they feed back is a debt, and (C) says that after the crossing the debt never again outweighs the source term and the rows that have already turned positive.

The first stage is a theorem of the record that had posed (Mono).[^413] Weight row $j$ by $\psi^{-j}$, where $\psi = (1 + 1/k)^{k-2}$ is the constant of the diagonal lower bound of section 6.2. In this weighting that bound says two things. The net debt of column $k$ up to any row (the sum of its steps so far, with the sign reversed) is at most the weighted entry of column $k - 1$ in that row. And that entry cannot fall, between the crossing row and a later row, by more than the sum of the positive steps in between. The weights $\theta_m = \psi^{-m} \cdot \varepsilon_m$ of the feedback decrease as long as $m$ is below about $3k/5$, and their ratios $\theta_{m+1}/\theta_m$ increase.

From these facts alone, with no estimate of any size, a step at a row $n \le 2k - 1$ after the crossing row $q$ cannot be the first to fail to be positive, provided the weights are still decreasing at lag $n - k$ and $n \cdot \theta_{n-k} \le q \cdot \theta_{q-k}$. The condition concerns two rows only, the crossing row and the row in question, which is why the records call it the *two-point criterion*. Since $(k+r) \cdot \theta_r$ decreases up to $r \approx 0.47 \cdot k$, every column keeps its sign from the crossing up to that deficit (at least up to deficit $\lfloor 2k/5 \rfloor + 1$), without computation and for every $k \ge 2$. The rows of column $k$ from row $k$ up to that deficit are the *range of the criterion*: it ends at the last deficit up to which $(k+r) \cdot \theta_r$ has not begun to increase.[^305] Of the two figures in the sentence before, $0.47 \cdot k$ is the approximate place of that end, and $\lfloor 2k/5 \rfloor + 1$ is a lower bound for it, which is proved for every $k \ge 3$: by hand for $k \ge 16$, and for $3 \le k \le 15$ by a few inequalities between rational numbers that are computed directly, without a table.[^306]

The second stage[^307] carries the sign on to deficit $3k/5$, where the weights stop decreasing. It does so by a variant of the criterion that needs only that the positive stretch since the crossing is at least about $0.8 \cdot k^{2/3}$ rows long when the range of the two-point criterion ends; this holds for $k \ge 64$ when the crossing lies within deficit $\varpi(k) = \lfloor 2k/5 \rfloor + 3 - \lceil 0.8 \cdot k^{2/3} \rceil$. Beyond deficit $3k/5$ signs no longer suffice.

The third stage[^308] uses the equation once more: if the rows since the crossing are non-negative, then $\zeta_n \ge (2/n) \cdot u_{n-1} \cdot (1 - \Xi)$. Here $\Xi$ collects a loss for the positive stretch being only so long and the feedback from lags beyond $3k/5$, the latter compared with the one entry $u_{n-1}$ through the equation that column $k - 1$ itself satisfies. An explicit bound gives $\Xi \lt 1$ in every row up to $2k$ when the crossing lies within deficit $27k/100$. This stage was published in two records of 21 November, the first of which reached the columns $k \ge 95$ and the second the columns $k \ge 82$. That is the last column that the hand arithmetic reaches. Computed from its definition, as an observation that belongs to no proof, $\Xi$ is 0.99965 in the worst row at $k = 81$, too close to 1 for the closed bounds, and exceeds 1 at $k = 80$, where the criterion itself fails.[^544]

All three stages treat the place of the crossing as given. It is known without computation for $k \ge 265$, by the strip bound of section 6.5, and for $125 \le k \le 264$ by a theorem of 21 November that puts the crossing within deficit $k/4$ (Theorem J, which section 6.8 tells). For $82 \le k \le 124$ the proof reads one sign per column from the paper's table, 43 signs in rows 101 to 149. They are the same 43 signs as those of the remainder on which the ninth input had ruled (section 6.5); by that input a finite remainder of that size, read from the paper's table, is not large-scale computation.[^309] So for $k \ge 82$ column $k$ is non-positive before its crossing row $q_0(k)$ and positive from there to row $2k$, and that pattern contains both nodes.

For $k \le 81$ only the first stage is available: inside its range the proof cites it, and outside it reads the cases from the table, 1,632 positions for (C) in rows up to 161. These positions too lie within the cells that the remainder of section 6.5 reads.[^310]

This is the form that the ninth input had made possible, six published claims and a finite remainder. The six are those on which the claim of the accepted proof of (C) says that it rests:[^311]

- three claims of the later of the two records of the third stage: for $k \ge 125$, and for $82 \le k \le 124$ on the hypothesis that the step of the column is positive at the first row beyond the curve, they give the crossing within deficit $27k/100$ and the positive sign from it to row $2k$, and the third of them is the 43 signs, which supply that hypothesis;[^312]
- the theorem of the first stage with its range;[^313]
- the claim of 20 November that reads from the paper's table, for the columns up to 147, that a non-negative step is followed by a non-negative one;[^314]
- the claim that no step is zero in the computed rows.[^315]

The table decides two things in the proof: where the crossing lies in the columns 82 to 124, and why the sign persists beyond the range of the criterion in the columns up to 81.

The record's other proof, its own new structural argument, concerns the feedback of the third stage, from lags beyond $3k/5$. The equation that the entries of column $k - 1$ themselves satisfy has a solved form like that of section 6.4: solved, it gives each entry as a sum of the non-negative source terms of the rows up to it, each spread by a kernel, the *resolvent kernel* of that equation. The bound published before had used of the resolvent kernel only that it decreases. The kernel is also log-convex, and the record uses the log-convexity to bound the feedback of a column by that of an *extreme column*, a fictitious column whose equation has a source term in its lowest row only: the share of the far feedback, taken with weights that do not decrease with the lag, is largest in the column whose only source term lies in its lowest row.[^316]

The other proof contained two results besides, which sections 6.8 and 6.9 use. For $k \ge 11$ and a crossing within deficit $k/3$, the condition of the two-point criterion holds in every row up to deficit $3k/5$, beyond the range of the criterion, so that the column is positive from its crossing row to that deficit.[^317] And for $k \ge 49$, on the same hypothesis, column $k$ is non-positive before its crossing row and positive from it to row $2k$, with no table and no machine computation.[^318]

The argument was published as supported claims and refused as the solution. Its part for the columns from 49 on could be replaced by the citations and the table of the accepted proof, and it proved again a theorem already published. The review's first draft had decided the other way and was withdrawn.

At 10:48 (CI) was accepted, by a shortened form of an argument refused twelve hours before.[^319] (CI) is the statement of section 6.3 that a column $k \ge 260$ has turned positive by the first row beyond the curve. Call a vertex of the forest *heavy* when its subtree has more than $k$ vertices. In the proof the step is bounded below by a first term, a count of ordered pairs of heavy vertices, minus a second term, the share of a single vertex whose subtree has exactly $k$. For a fixed deficit the columns concerned form a short interval that ends on the curve. The first term has to be bounded from below on this interval and the second from above, and each of the two bounds is a closed formula evaluated at one end of the interval.

The refused proof had evaluated the two at opposite ends, the first at the end on the curve and the second at the other end. Both have their worst case at the end on the curve. The second term turned out to increase with $k$ as it stands: it is a sum of products of factors each of which increases with $k$ when the deficit is held fixed, and the other end had seemed its worst only because a closed form that bounded a part of it decreases with $k$.[^320] The bound for the first term hardly moves near the curve, so that it can be taken at a point just beyond the curve at almost no loss. Comparing the two there removed the mismatch, and with it two refinements that the refused proof had carried, both of them sharpenings in the estimate of the second term. That proof had needed only one of the two, and either would have done: its review had named them and had found that the proof still closed with its own constants when either one of them was dropped, though not when both were; that room was its ground for refusing the solution.[^321] No table is used.

Only (CI) received a solution in that record. (Mono), (T_R), (S_R) and (Diag₁) became proved in the same commit through relations accepted earlier, and with them the right half stood.

The root then needed one more node, and any one of five would have been enough: (Col), (L), (S_L), (Diag) and (X). The review states this, and for each of the five it names the accepted relations by which that node leads to the root:[^322]

- (Col) gives the root together with (Mono), and it gives Target T together with (CI);
- (L) gives Target T together with (T_R);
- (S_L) gives the root together with (S_R);
- (Diag) gives Target T together with (CI);
- (X) gives the root by itself.

Target T in turn gives the root, and (Mono), (CI), (T_R) and (S_R) were proved by then. For (L) and for (Diag) the review names only the relation to Target T.

No accepted relation leads from (Diag) alone to the root. The relation that the review names leads from (Diag) and (CI) to Target T, a node from which the frontier has the root follow.[^323] Among the relations into the root itself, which section 6.10 lists, the frontier has (Diag) only together with (Col), in the staircase relation.[^324]

To judge by the frontier of that hour, which has the restrictions of (Col) and (Diag) to the right half, (Col₁) and (Diag₁), as proved, what remained open of these five lay on the left.[^545] The exception is (X), the strict positivity of the cross product, which is a statement about every position. Of the five, (L) was the one that received a solution next (section 6.8).

### 6.8 The left half, and the root proved informally, 22 November

The left half came down to the single statement (L): for $k \ge 6$ and $n \ge 2k + 1$, the step $d_{n,k}$ is non-negative. From (L) follow, by relations accepted on the first two days, the left part (S_L), the column statement (Col), Target T, and with the right half the root.

The solution of (L) was accepted on 22 November at 21:19, as a solution by citation, and with it (S_L), (Col), Target T and the root were accepted by relation; the theorems on which it rests had been accepted as claims, on proofs in words, and none of this was formal; the formal proof followed on 23 November (section 6.9).[^546] Where a proof told in this section reads values from the paper's table or computes exactly on the base table, and where a value belongs to no proof, the text says so at the statement.

By the evening of 21 November the state of (L) was this. At the level of supported claims it was proved for every column $k \ge 148$ without any computation, for the columns 6 to 16 from the base table $n \le 30$, and for the columns from 17 to 147 only by reading the paper's table. The columns 6 to 16 rested on two claims of two days. The first record of the instance had closed the columns up to 11 on the first evening, from the base table and the critical-tilt propagation of section 5.2.[^325] The record of 20 November that split the step into a diagonal and a column bracket (section 6.4) had added the columns 12 to 16, by showing from the same table that each of the two brackets is non-negative in them; for column 16 this meant checking statements about the rows 31 to 33 from the numbers of the table, which the claim itself places at the border of the rule.[^326] One record said where it saw the difficulty, as an observation from computation and not a proof: what was missing in the remaining columns was not a new method but estimates in finitely many columns.[^327]

By this account's reading, under the eighth and ninth inputs a solution of the whole node that closed the columns below 148 by reading thousands of cells of the table could not be accepted.[^547] What those inputs allowed was a finite remainder of the size of the one on which the ninth input had ruled (section 6.5).

Two records of the afternoon of 21 November, before the ninth input, had held back in this way from two nodes that follow from (L): the first proposed no solution of the whole of (Col), and the second none of (S_L). The first wrote that such a solution could not be accepted under the eighth input, the proof for the columns up to 147 being at that time only the table. The second wrote that the columns 12 to 147 were closed by the computation of the table, with no structural argument for why the step is non-negative there, and that the user's intention, as the eighth input conveyed it, was not yet to accept such a proof as a solution.[^328]

Where the line ran above the size that the ninth input had fixed, 43 signs and 3,240 positions in rows up to 162, was left to the reviews, and those of 22 November did not all draw it alike, as one of them sets out.[^329] Three of them judged a reading of the table by its size:

- A review of 03:09 did not count about 30,000 facts read from the paper's table as large-scale computation, and added that the user had never fixed that size.[^330]
- The review of the record of 04:11 held that 4,028 positions in rows up to 248 went beyond the case of the ninth input.[^331]
- The review of the record of 14:57, to which the next paragraphs come, judged the table claim for (L) itself. It accepted its use in the columns 21 to 62, about 2,900 facts in rows up to 138, as of the kind and the size of that case. It said that this judgment would not hold for closing the columns 63 to 124 by that claim, which would read each column up to a row that reaches 258 in column 124, so that the columns 75 to 124, it wrote, needed a new argument.[^332]

The record that closed the gap in the mathematics was published on 22 November at 14:57.[^333] Its central statement, Theorem N′, is about a column after its crossing:

 for $k \ge 44$: if the step of column $k$ is non-negative at some row $n \le k + k/3$, then it is negative before the first such row, non-negative from it up to deficit $3k/5$, and positive in every row after that, with $d_{n,k} \ge 2 \cdot (1 - 5.86/\sqrt{k}) \cdot b_{n-1,k-1}$ for all $n \ge 2k + 1$.

The proof works on the column equation in the critical tilt. With $x = x_k$, $\eta_n = x^n \cdot d_{n,k}/n!$ and $U_n = x^n \cdot b_{n,k-1}/n!$, the equation of section 6.2 becomes

$$\eta_n = \sigma_n + ((k+1) \cdot x/n) \cdot \eta_{n-1} + \sum_{m=1}^{n-k} \kappa_m \cdot \eta_{n-m}, \qquad \sigma_n \ge (2x/n) \cdot U_{n-1},$$

a positive source term, which is the neighbouring entry $b_{n-1,k-1}$ of column $k - 1$, plus a feedback from the earlier rows of the column, in which the positive weights $\kappa_m$ are those with which the earlier rows enter the equation in this tilt. The rows before the crossing are negative, and the question is whether the source term outweighs what the feedback carries forward of them. Two mechanisms answer it in two ranges of rows, and the theorem is the observation that they can be joined.

Just after the crossing, up to deficit $3k/5$, the proof uses the two-point criterion of section 6.7, which keeps the sign of the column row by row. The criterion works in its own weighting, by powers of $(k/(k+1))^{k-2}$. This is the weighting in which that section states the criterion, built on the constant of the diagonal lower bound, and not the critical tilt in which the equation above is written. The record that had proved (C) at 04:11 that day had shown, in the part of it that was refused as a solution, that the condition of the criterion holds for every row up to deficit $3k/5$ whenever the crossing lies within deficit $k/3$.

From there on the proof uses a weighted form of the diagonal convexity, which bounds the debt of all the negative rows at once. The tilted convexity theorem of section 6.7 implies that the tilted steps $\eta_n$ of the column, each multiplied by the weight $\max(n, 2k+1)$, have partial sums bounded below by minus two weighted entries of column $k - 1$ and one end term. Written out, with $\Psi(n) = \max(n, 2k+1)$ for the weight, the bound says that for every $k \ge 3$ and every row $J \ge k$

$$\sum_{n=k}^{J} \Psi(n) \cdot \eta_n \ge -[ \Psi(J) \cdot U_J + \Psi(J-1) \cdot U_{J-1} + (\Psi(J) - \Psi(J-1)) \cdot x^J \cdot A_{J,k-1}/J! ].$$

The sum on the left runs over the rows of the column from its first, row $k$, up to row $J$, each tilted step with its weight. On the right stand the tilted entries $U$ of column $k - 1$ in the rows $J$ and $J - 1$, with their weights, and the end term, which by the form of the weight is present only from row $2k + 2$ on, where it is the tilted cumulative count $x^J \cdot A_{J,k-1}/J!$.[^334] So the negative rows before the crossing can never have left a debt larger than that. The explicit result is $d_{n,k} \ge 2 \cdot (1 - R(n)) \cdot b_{n-1,k-1}$ with $R(n) \le \Phi(k)$ in the rows up to $2k$ and $R(n) \le 5.86/\sqrt{k}$ after them, where $\Phi$ is an explicit bound that decreases in $k$, with $\Phi(44) \le 0.9924$ and $\Phi(43) = 1.0027$. The threshold 44 is the first column for which $\Phi$ is below 1.

Earlier assemblies had switched from the first mechanism to the second at deficit $2k/5$ and had closed only for $k \ge 125$. At that deficit the nearest negative row lies only about $0.13 \cdot k$ rows back from the row at which the second mechanism begins; at deficit $3k/5$ it lies at least $0.33 \cdot k$ rows back. Switching at $3k/5$ closes, with the published lemmas alone, for $k \ge 63$ when the crossing lies within deficit $27k/100$. The record states this as a theorem of its own, for the columns from 63 on. The review found that theorem and its proof to stand, but it did not support the claim that states the theorem, because one sentence of the claim, an attribution, is wrong.[^548] With the hypothesis widened to $k/3$ and three further ingredients of the record (the ordering of the kernel of the column equation for the later crossing, a smaller end term, and a closed bound at the switching rows) it closes for $k \ge 44$.

The ingredients had been published before: the two-point criterion on the first night,[^335] its reach to deficit $3k/5$ in the record of 04:11 that day,[^336] and the weighted diagonal convexity as a consequence of the tilted convexity theorem of 21 November, which has no threshold (section 6.7). The review notes that the proof uses that theorem directly and not the nodes (D) and (CB) of that section.

The theorem does not say where the crossing is; it takes that as its hypothesis. The record supplies it, as $q_0(k) \le k + k/3$, for every $k \ge 45$ (for $k = 44$ the bound holds by a value of the exact table that the record's peer computed, but no published statement gave it).[^549] The theorem with this hypothesis filled in is the record's Theorem N.

For $k \ge 97$ the bound is proved by hand. For $k \ge 265$ it follows from the entry theorem of 20 November (section 6.4). For $125 \le k \le 264$ and for $97 \le k \le 124$ it is given by two theorems of 21 November. Theorem J, of a record of 11:00, bounds the crossing by deficit $k/4$ in the first of these ranges,[^337] and Theorem J′, of a record of 23:24, bounds it by deficit $k/3$ in the second.[^338] These two use the second criterion of section 6.4, the column equation in solved form, with an explicit lower bound for column $k - 1$, and close with margins of 0.16 and 0.68 per cent.

Below 97 no such estimate closes, and the bound is read from the paper's table: one sign in each of the columns 49 to 96, and for the columns 45 to 48 the value $q_0(49) = 64$ together with the fact, read from the same table by an earlier record, that $q_0$ increases strictly over the columns 1 to 264.

The record's own solution of (L) was refused: its review found the proof correct and still shortenable, since it closed the columns from 125 on by an older and longer published derivation where its own theorem already applied.

That evening the review of a record of 18:51 refused three relations that would each have made the root stand.[^339] The three relations led from (CI) to Target T, from (CI) to (Diag) and from (Diag₁) to (Diag). The review found each implication correct and refused all three under the fifth, seventh and eighth inputs, each on at least two of these grounds:

- the proof obtained the bound on the crossing row twice;
- it cited a derivation in which three reviews had named steps that could still go;
- it closed the large rows of the small columns by reading the table claim for (L), about nine thousand facts, and in the first relation more than twelve thousand, in the very columns 75 to 124 for which the review of the record of 14:57 had asked for an argument.

Against the second relation it held besides that its use of the paper's larger table could be replaced by an argument of the same record.[^340]

The same review named the shape in which a session could propose the relations again, and what would have to go for that shape to be accepted. The shape was written for a session that would propose again, and is in the first place that of a proof of the first relation: Theorem N′ for every column from 45 on, with the place of the crossing taken from (CI), from the paper's table or from Theorem N according to the column; the table claim for the columns 6 to 44 only; and the base table below them.[^341]

To judge by the two times that the accepting review records, the solution of (L) that was accepted that night was not written from this advice: the record with the solution had been fixed at 17:36, before the review of 18:51 was published. The accepting review also sets the two side by side. It sums up the recommendation of 18:51, for the part that concerns (L), as Theorem N′ and Theorem N for the columns from 45 on and the table claim for the columns 6 to 44, and calls this the same shape as the solution that it accepts. The recommendation had been made for a proof that read a larger table, so the accepting review did not take the judgment over as it stood; it noted that the direction was the same.[^550]

The accepted solution was published at 21:19.[^342] It is three sentences that cite four published claims, one for each of four ranges of columns: $k \ge 45$ by Theorem N; $21 \le k \le 44$ by a claim of 20 November that reads 1,360 facts from the paper's rows up to 101 and then propagates by the critical tilt; $12 \le k \le 20$ by a theorem whose only table is the base table $n \le 30$; and $6 \le k \le 11$ from the base table directly. With it (L), (S_L), (Col), Target T and the root became informal_proved. Of the two claims that start from the base table, the one for the columns 6 to 11 is the claim of the first record, of 19 November.[^343] The theorem for the columns 12 to 20 had been published at 03:09 that morning. These are the columns whose crossing row lies inside the base table, and what the theorem joins to the table is exact rational arithmetic on its numbers: from them it computes lower bounds for the steps of these columns in rows beyond row 30, and it closes with a criterion for the tail of a column.[^344]

An assembly of citations was thus accepted where earlier assemblies had been set back, and the review explains why at length. The earlier refusals had concerned assemblies whose cited derivations contained identified slack, or obtained the same thing twice, or closed by table what a published argument covered. Here the cited theorem was not a longer form of something shorter but a stronger theorem than its alternative. The comparison is with the other solution offered in the same record, a rewritten, shorter direct proof that worked only from $k = 63$ and read the crossing from the table up to $k = 124$. Citing the theorem moved the columns 45 to 62 and the crossings of the columns 97 to 124 from the table to an argument, "the direction the user wished for computation". And the fifth input's own wording counts replacing a direct proof by a citation of something already proved as a shortening. The direct proof, which the review found correct, was refused for that reason.

The review also states what the accepted proof leaves unexplained: why the columns 21 to 44 satisfy (L), which only the table says, and why in the columns 45 to 96 the crossing falls within deficit $k/3$. The record says of itself that it contains no new mathematics.

Three smaller events belong to the same day. A correct proof with no room to shorten had been refused at noon[^345] because one sentence, not used in the proof, was wrong inside a claim it rested on. The user asked whether the review could not have supported the claim and left its finding as a remark, and the tenth standing input of 13:38 made that the rule for minor flaws.[^346] Of the eighteen records whose reviews began after it, seventeen have all their claims supported, most with remarks; the review of the other[^347] left one claim unsupported and said that its flaw was not a minor one.

At 14:20 the eleventh input asked that the imports of Lean files be chosen by need.[^348] Seven formalizations were running at once, thirty-one compilations were waiting for the program that queues the instance's Lean compilations, its broker, which ran one at a time, and almost all the memory of a compilation was the cost of one blanket import inherited by nearly every module.

At 15:43 the operator added a second notice, a survey of what the explorations had used and where they had stopped.[^349] It was written at the user's request from the thirty-three explorations published up to the point at which the notice closes its count, and from their reviews, and it says of itself that it is not a condition.[^350] Of what had been used it reports that the claims of explorations on which the accepted solutions and relations rest are all equalities or inequalities at finite size, with their ranges and quantifiers stated, and that no claim of the twelve latest of the thirty-three explorations is among them: by their reviews, most of what those twelve proved is a theorem about a limit object, or a theorem for sufficiently large columns or rows with no explicit threshold, or a criterion under an unproved hypothesis.[^351] Of where the explorations had stopped it reports, for each of the four operator questions still open (section 5.4), what the latest review names as remaining, and the operator reads the four remainders as one kind of task: to fix the sign of some quantity in a range where size and level are given by numbers, a range that lies beyond what the theorems reach and is wider than what tables or computation had confirmed.[^352] The methods had differed, the notice says, and the endings had been alike: a new way of writing the object, a theorem about large sizes or the limit that came from it, and that remainder.[^353]

### 6.9 The formal proof, 22 to 23 November

Formalization had run beside the informal work without a break since the morning of 21 November. (Two formalizers started on 20 November had been cancelled; section 7.3 tells of the first, and section 6.6 of the second.) A formalizer was started for a node once the node was informally proved, at once or some hours later, and it built on the Lean sources of the records before it. On the morning after the root had been proved informally (section 6.8) this work reached the root itself, and the formal proof at which it arrived is not the same proof as the informal one, which was accepted and was not formalized.

This section follows the instance up to the commit considered here, at which the root is formally proved (section 2.3), and tells nothing of what the instance proved after it. Every theorem that it names with a Lean name is formally proved: a review of the instance reports having compiled it and found no axiom beyond the three standard ones, and the tables in these proofs are computed by the Lean kernel. The arguments told here are those of the Lean proofs; where a statement was not proved in Lean, because it rests on arithmetic by hand or on a computation outside these proofs or was accepted on a proof in words, the text says so at the statement.

The first formal proof, that of (Mono_265), is told in section 6.6. The next node to receive one was (L1°), the size convexity. It says that for a fixed $k$ the share of the parking functions of length $n$ whose displacements are all smaller than $k$ is convex as a function of $n$; the table of section 6.10 gives the formula, and the range of $k$ and $n$ for which the node states it. The node had been proved informally the day before, by the proof of section 6.5 that cites 29 cells of the base table and was accepted in preference to a longer one free of computation, and its formal proof was published on 22 November at 02:59.[^354] The formal proof of the root does not use the theorem of this node, as the audit told later in this section shows. The column bound (CB) of section 6.4 followed at 17:02,[^355] and at 21:37 came (C), the statement that a column which has turned positive stays positive up to row $2k - 1$.[^356]

The proof of (C) was the first in which the Lean kernel computes entries of the paper's table. Before it only the formal proof of the size convexity (L1°) had the Lean kernel decide anything, 29 inequalities inside the base table. The informal proof of (C) reads facts from the paper's rows, and a Lean proof cannot cite a paper: it has to produce the numbers. The proof derives, from three identities already formalized, two integer recurrences that generate the entries $b_{n,\ell}$. A theorem ties the output of the recurrences to the counts defined from the parking process. The 3,343 facts that the proof needs are then closed propositions which the Lean kernel evaluates by `decide +kernel`. No `native_decide` is used anywhere, so no trust in the compiler is added to the three standard axioms. This is the pattern of every table in the final proof.

The Lean proof of (C) is not the accepted informal proof put into Lean. That proof is an assembly of citations, and formalizing it would have meant formalizing every theorem behind them. The formalizer chose instead the argument of the same record that its review had refused as a solution (section 6.7), because the mathematics underneath that argument is smaller: from the parking process it needs only three equations that were already formalized.

The argument is one theorem, `Parking.persist`: a column whose first positive row has deficit at most $k/3$ stays positive up to row $2k - 1$. Its proof uses the two-point criterion up to deficit $0.47 \cdot k$ and beyond that the inequality of the third stage, both of section 6.7, with the feedback from far lags bounded by its value in an extreme column, a fictitious column whose equation has a source term in its lowest row only. The constants of the Lean version close from $k = 82$ (those of the informal version from 49).

The hypothesis of this theorem is about the place of the crossing. For $k \ge 265$ that place is a theorem, the strip bound of section 6.5, already formalized with (Mono_265). For $82 \le k \le 264$ the Lean kernel computes one sign per column, 183 in all; in the columns 125 to 264 these take the place of Theorem J (section 6.8), which has a hand proof and was not formalized. For $k \le 81$ the Lean kernel checks the statement at every one of the 3,160 positions, and the range of the two-point criterion (section 6.7), inside which the informal proof cites that criterion, is not used. The signs and the positions together are the facts that the Lean kernel evaluates for (C).

In the night that followed, the formal proofs of the remaining nodes of the right half were published. (D), the convexity along a diagonal that section 6.4 states for the lengths $k + 1 \le t \le 2k - 1$, came first, at 21:47, with two proofs. One is the formalization of the informal proof. The other is a proof that the peer found while checking it and that does not need the theorem by which (D) had been proved informally; this second proof is the one the review recommends for use.[^357] The other nodes followed: the right half itself, (S_R), at 04:25;[^358] (Mono) at 04:39, in two ways;[^359] (CI), the statement of section 6.3 on the first row beyond the curve, at 05:49 with no table at all;[^360] and (Col₁), the column statement of the right half, at 06:33.[^361]

The reviews of that night recompiled the new modules themselves, compared the definitions with the root (by reading the file, or by its being byte for byte the one already compared), and audited the axioms. They decline to extend the fifth input's standard to formal solutions without a decision of the user; by that standard a proof that can still be made shorter is not accepted as a solution (section 6.4). They also record what the extension would change. For (D), (Mono) and (Col₁), where two formal proofs were offered, the longer one would fall; for (S_R) and (CI) they found nothing to shorten.

The peer of the record on (Mono) proved something in Lean that decided how the root would be assembled. It concerns the "middle step" $d_{2k,k}$, the step of column $k$ in the middle of the even row $2k$. The inequality of the middle step, one inequality per column, says that this step is not negative: $d_{2k,k} \ge 0$. What the peer proved is an equivalence: the statement of (S_R) holds exactly when the inequality of the middle step holds in every column $k \ge 10$.[^362] The proof of the equivalence rests on the Lean theorems of (C) and (Mono), and it proves neither side.

Three thresholds go with the middle step from here on, and each serves a different purpose: 8 is the column from which the inequality is true, 9 the column from which the proof of (L) yields it, and 10 the column from which the right half depends on it. The inequality itself is true exactly from $k = 8$: the columns 1 to 7 cross after row $2k$, and column 8 crosses at row 16. That it holds for every $k \ge 8$ is formally proved, by the final record: for $k \ge 9$ through the proof of (L), and for $k = 8$ by one comparison of two neighbouring entries of row 16, which the Lean kernel computes.[^551] How this is done is told later in this section, where the proof comes to row $2k$. That the middle step is negative in the columns 1 to 7 is shown by exact computation and is no part of the proof of the root.[^552] In the columns 2 to 7 the Lean kernel computes six further comparisons of neighbouring entries, like the one for $k = 8$, and they stand in the place of the inequality; column 1, whose row 2 has only one step, needs none. The bound 10 belongs to the equivalence and not to the inequality. From column 10 on, the next column has already turned positive at row $2k$, so that a negative middle step would be a descent followed by an ascent; in the columns below 10 the right half does not depend on the middle step.

The left half had come down to (L), the left increase (section 6.8). The formalizer for (L) had been started at 23:00 on the evening of the informal proof, an hour and forty-one minutes after it, and had `Parking.left_increase` compiled by 02:50.[^363] Its proof splits the columns in two where the informal proof split them in four.

For $k \ge 82$ the proof is an induction over the rows $n \ge 2k$, and what it carries from row to row is the whole sign pattern of the column so far: there is a row $q$ of deficit at most $27k/100$ before which the steps are non-positive and from which, up to row $n - 1$, they are positive. It starts from the rows up to $2k - 1$, where the Lean proof of (C) supplies exactly this pattern, with the 183 signs computed by the Lean kernel for the columns up to 264 and by theorem beyond.

The inductive argument starts from the column equation of section 6.8, which is written in the critical tilt: $\eta_n = \text{source term} + \text{feedback}$. Here $\eta_n$ is the tilted step of row $n$, the source term is the positive term of that section, the one that does not depend on the other rows of the column, and the feedback is the sum $\sum_j \kappa_{n-j} \cdot \eta_j$ over the earlier rows of the column, with the positive weights $\kappa$ of that section. The feedback contains the negative rows, and the proof compares its weights with one fixed weight function, $\Psi'(j) = \max(j, 2k - 1)$, which is the weight $\max(n, 2k+1)$ of section 6.8 with $2k - 1$ in the place of $2k + 1$. For a suitable constant $\omega$, depending on $n$, $\kappa_{n-j} \le \omega \cdot \Psi'(j)$ in every negative row and $\kappa_{n-j} \ge \omega \cdot \Psi'(j)$ in every positive one. This is a property of the weights $\kappa$ alone, and it holds for any row $q$ of deficit at most $27k/100$, with $\omega = \kappa_{n-q+1}/(2k - 1)$: the quotient $\kappa_{n-j}/\Psi'(j)$ is at most $\omega$ for $k \le j \lt q$ and at least $\omega$ for $q \le j \le n - 1$. It follows from the decrease of the weights $\kappa_m$ in $m$ and from bounds on how fast they decrease.[^364] The feedback is therefore at least $\omega$ times the sum of all steps so far weighted by $\Psi'$.

That weighted sum is bounded below, by minus the weighted entries $b_{n-1,k-1}$ and $b_{n-2,k-1}$ of column $k - 1$ and an end term, through the weighted diagonal convexity of section 6.8. The bound limits the debt that the negative rows can have left (section 6.8), and it is called the debt bound in what follows. What remains is a comparison of constants, which closes when $12.66 \cdot \sqrt{k} \le 0.7356 \cdot (2k - 1)$. The Lean theorems assume $k \ge 82$. By the record's arithmetic by hand, which the review recomputed and supported as such and which was not checked in Lean, this inequality between the constants is true from $k = 76$ on, and with the other constants of the inductive argument the argument holds from $k = 79$.[^553]

These are the ingredients of Theorem N′ of section 6.8 in another arrangement. Theorem N′ lets the debt bound take over inside the first layer, at deficit $3k/5$, and that is what allowed a crossing as late as $k/3$ and columns down to 44. The Lean proof leaves the rows up to $2k - 1$ to the theorems of (C) and begins the debt bound at row $2k$. The weight is changed as well: its bend, the row up to which it is constant, is moved from $2k + 1$ to $2k - 1$. The reason is that, where the weight is constant, the debt bound needs the convexity along the diagonal without a weight, and the Lean theorems published before the final record give that convexity only for the rows up to $2k - 2$; this section tells of them further on.[^554]

This arrangement is the form of two theorems of 21 November, one of a record of 11:17 and the other of a record of 16:39,[^365] with the arithmetic of the direct proof that had been refused as a solution of (L) at 21:19 (section 6.8). The two theorems, which are proofs in words that were accepted as claims, concern the columns $k \ge 148$: a column whose steps are non-positive before a row $q$ of deficit at most $27k/100$ and non-negative from $q$ up to row $k + \lfloor 2k/5 \rfloor + 1$ has, under one further condition on $k$ or on $q$, a positive step in every later row.[^366] The record of 16:39, the one which observed that what the columns then remaining lacked was estimates in finitely many columns and no new method (section 6.8), proves this also with the weight $\Psi'$.[^367] The record of the formal proof says that 82 is not the limit of this argument but the first column for which the Lean proof of (C) supplies its input, and that the columns 45 to 62, whose crossings lie beyond deficit $27k/100$, could have been reached only by formalizing Theorem N′ itself.

For $6 \le k \le 81$ the proof uses the critical-tilt propagation of section 5.2, by which, given the growth inequality, finitely many facts about the lower rows of a column show that its step is non-negative from a certain row on. The proof takes these facts from a table, in the rows below a row $n_0 \le 2k + 14$ chosen for each column: 5,158 facts, all in rows $n \le 174$. So the columns 45 to 81, which the informal proof had moved from the table to Theorem N′, are in the Lean proof closed by table again. The peer added a second form, `Parking.left_increase_cc`, which cites the Lean theorem of (C) to know that a column stays positive after its crossing, so that the tail sums starting after the crossing need not be computed; it needs 2,158 new facts.

Row $2k$, the row of the middle step, is the one row of column $k$ that neither (C) nor (L) reaches: (C) carries a positive sign as far as row $2k - 1$, and (L) begins at row $2k + 1$. The node (Col₁) does reach it. Its statement, which carries a non-negative sign where (C) carries a positive one, goes one row further than that of (C), its last case being the passage from row $2k - 1$ to row $2k$ (section 6.2).[^368] The formal proof does not use (Col₁). As far as the frontier shows, (Col₁) is among the nodes through which the informal relations for the right half passed, as this section tells further on.

The peer of the final record saw that the proof of (L) gives this row as well. For $k \ge 82$ row $2k$ is the first row that the induction over the rows closes, and (L) uses of that induction only the rows from $2k + 1$. For $9 \le k \le 81$, the columns whose crossing row is at most $2k - 1$, the sign at row $2k$ is among the facts that the peer's table for (L) reads anyway. For $k = 8$, the column that crosses at row 16, which is its row $2k$, the Lean kernel computes one comparison more, that of the two entries of row 16 whose difference is the middle step. Together these give `Parking.middle_step`, $d_{2k,k} \ge 0$ for $k \ge 8$. The right half then follows from (C), (Mono) and the middle step, with six comparisons in rows up to 14 for the columns 2 to 7, where the middle step is negative, and without the formal theorem of (S_R) published four hours earlier.

The final record was published on 23 November at 08:10.[^372] Its theorem is

 `Parking.root_unimodal`: for every $n \ge 1$ there is $p \le n - 1$ such that $b_{n,\ell-1} \le b_{n,\ell}$ for $1 \le \ell \le p$, and $b_{n,\ell+1} \le b_{n,\ell}$ for $p \le \ell \le n - 2$.

In words: every row rises weakly as far as some position $p$ and falls weakly from $p$ on. The theorem is derived from the absence, in every row, of a strict descent followed later by a strict ascent: of positions $i \lt j$ with $d_{n,i} \lt 0 \lt d_{n,j}$. The proof asks where the descent would lie.

If $n \ge 2i + 1$, it would lie in the left part. For $i \ge 6$ there is none, by (L). The columns 1 to 5, which (L) leaves out, are treated separately, column 1 by an induction and the columns 2 to 5 by the propagation used for (L). In them Lean proves that the step is non-negative from row $2i + 1$ on outside four exceptions, the positions (5,2), (7,3), (9,4) and (11,5), at which the step is negative by exact values and by no Lean theorem.[^555] In each of the rows 5, 7, 9 and 11 the Lean kernel verifies that no step to the right of the exception is positive. This is `Parking.left_part`, the statement of (S_L).

If $n \le 2i - 1$, the position $(n,i)$ lies in the first layer of column $i$. By (C), a column that has once turned positive stays positive up to row $2i - 1$; so a step $d_{n,i} \le 0$ means that column $i$ has not turned positive in any row up to $n$. By (Mono), column $i + 1$ has then not turned positive either, and, repeating, neither has column $j$, so that $d_{n,j} \le 0$. These pairs are excluded by (C) and (Mono) alone.

There remains $n = 2i$, the middle of an even row. Here the proof uses the middle step, $d_{2i,i} \ge 0$ for $i \ge 8$, which leaves no descent; for $2 \le i \le 7$ the six comparisons show that the next step is not positive, and the argument of the preceding paragraph does the rest. The pairs with $n \le 2i$ make up the statement of (S_R), which the record thus proves a second time, as `Parking.right_half_cc`.

Of the formal theorems of nodes, the proof of the root uses some and not others:

- used, by the list of the review's audit of dependencies: those of (S_L), (L), (C), (Mono) and (Mono_265);
- not used, as the same audit states: the published formal theorems of (S_R) and of the relation "(S_L) and (S_R) imply the root";
- not used either: the formal theorems of (D), (CB), (CI), (Col₁) and (L1°);
- of the formal records of (CI) and (Col₁), none of the modules proper to them enters the proof.

Of the record of (D) three Lean theorems do enter the proof, though not the node's theorem. All three are about one quantity, the second difference along a diagonal of the tilted cumulative counts $x_k^n \cdot A_{n,\ell}/n!$: the counts at the positions $(N+1,k)$ and $(N-1,k-2)$ minus twice the count at $(N,k-1)$. The node (D) is stated for another normalization, the weighted count of section 6.4. The first theorem says that the second difference is non-negative for $k \le N \le 2k - 2$; it is proved in that record from the same tilted convexity theorem (section 6.7), for $k \ge 7$.[^373] The second says the same with each count multiplied by the number of its row, for $k \le N \le 2k - 1$. The third bounds the second difference below by a negative multiple of the difference of the two outer counts, also at the sizes $N = 2k - 1$ and $2k$. The second and the third are valid for $k \ge 3$.[^374]

The debt bound on which the induction for (L) in the large columns rests is built from the three. It needs the second difference, taken with the weight $\Psi'$ on each of its three counts, to be non-negative in every row from row $k$ on. Up to row $2k - 2$ the three weights are equal, and this is the first theorem. From row $2k$ on each weight is the number of its row, and this is the statement of the second theorem carried to every later row by an induction, which starts from the second theorem and takes from the third what its inductive argument needs of the rows up to $2k$. At the one row between, $2k - 1$, the two weightings differ by a term that is not negative.[^556]

The review compiled the 53 new modules afresh, found the axioms to be `propext`, `Classical.choice` and `Quot.sound`, compared the statement with the root's definition of unimodality, and accepted four formal solutions: two for (L), one for (S_L), one for the root.

In shape, then, the formal proof follows the cut of the first record of the instance, a left part and a right half. In substance its right half is made of the two column statements of the instance's first night, (C) and (Mono), the latter resting on the shortened theorem of 21 November. Its left part is, in the small columns, the critical-tilt propagation run on tables, a reduction that the handoff had passed on from the report on the left half and that the instance's first record had proved anew,[^375] and in the large columns an induction over the rows $n \ge 2k$ that starts from the single change of sign of the column up to row $2k - 1$.[^376]

On how the formal proof stands to the reduction of 10 November (section 4.8), which this account reads as corresponding to the relation (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root (section 6.2), the sources give two things. The frontier has (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root as an accepted relation, and at the commit of the formal proof it still lists the root as informally proved through that relation, among others.[^136] The formal proof is the Lean form of the proof of another accepted relation, (S_L) $\wedge$ (S_R) $\Rightarrow$ root, which derives the root from a statement on the left part of every row and one on its right half; (L) enters it through the left part. It uses (Mono), and it treats the rows of a column by three statements: (C), which section 5.2 gives as the column statement for the right half with strict signs, up to row $2k - 1$; the inequality of the middle step for the one row $2k$; and (L) from row $2k + 1$ on.[^137] For the columns $k \ge 9$ the final record proves in Lean one statement on the signs of a column in all its rows, `Parking.column_signs`: there is a row $q$ with $k \lt q \le 2k - 1$ such that the steps are non-positive before $q$, positive from $q$ up to row $2k - 1$, and non-negative from row $2k$ on. This statement is a little weaker than (Col) in those columns: (Col) also excludes a step that is exactly zero before the crossing row and negative in the next row, and the Lean proof does not prove this.[^138] (Col) itself was accepted by relation and by a solution of its own, and it had no formal proof.

The informal proof of 22 November and the formal proof of 23 November are therefore not the same proof. On the left both rest on (L), but they prove it by the same method only in the columns 21 to 44. The informal proof has Theorem N′ from $k = 45$ on and needs from the table only the place of the crossing, and that only up to $k = 96$. The Lean proof uses the propagation from a table throughout $6 \le k \le 81$, and from $k = 82$ an induction over the rows which has the place of the crossing from the Lean kernel up to $k = 264$ and from a theorem beyond.

On the right the informal proof was, as far as the frontier shows, a set of relations: most of them through (CI), (Col₁) and the paper's Theorem 5.9, with facts read from the paper's rows $n \le 300$, and one from (Mono_265) with a few thousand facts of the same table (section 6.5). One of them passed through (Diag₁), the diagonal statement restricted to the right half, which had no proof of its own: from 22 November at 10:48 it stood by relation from (C) and (CI), and with (Col₁) it gave (S_R).[^557] The Lean proof uses neither (CI) nor (Col₁) nor Theorem 5.9, nor (Diag₁), which had no formal proof; it goes through (C), (Mono), which contains (Mono_265), and the middle step.

What the two have in common is the residue that only a table decides: (L) in the columns 21 to 44, and the place of the crossing in the small and middle columns.

Two pairs of numbers describe the computation, and they concern different things. The first pair belongs to (L). The formalizer's proof has the Lean kernel compute 5,158 facts for the columns 6 to 81, and the peer's variant 2,158, because the variant takes from the Lean theorem of (C) that a column stays positive between its crossing and row $2k - 1$.

The second pair belongs to the root, and both of its members are built on the peer's variant. A first version of the record obtained the root from (S_L) and the formal theorem of (S_R); its proof passed through 8,983 facts computed by the Lean kernel, among them the 3,423 of (S_R). The published version obtains the right half from (C), (Mono) and the middle step, which removes those 3,423 and adds the 551 of (Mono) and seven comparisons: 6,118 in all, namely 3,343 for (C), 551 for (Mono), 2,158 for (L), 59 for (S_L) and 7 for the right half, all in rows $n \le 300$.

The table gives the count of the published version position by position: for each range of positions, what gives the sign of the step there, and how many facts the Lean kernel computes for it.

| positions | the sign of the step is given by | facts computed by the Lean kernel |
|---|---|---|
| $k \ge 265$, $n \ge 2k$ | induction over the rows, started from the rows up to $2k - 1$ (debt bound) | none |
| $82 \le k \le 264$, $n \ge 2k$ | the same | 183 crossing signs, one per column, taken over from the proof of (C) |
| $9 \le k \le 81$, $n \ge 2k$ | critical-tilt propagation from a table | 2,099 |
| $6 \le k \le 8$, $n \ge 2k + 1$ | critical-tilt propagation from a table | 59 |
| $k = 1$; $2 \le k \le 5$ with $n \ge 2k + 2$; the rows 5, 7, 9, 11 | induction; propagation; direct comparison | 1 + 44 + 14 = 59 |
| the rows $k + 1 \le n \le 2k - 1$: a positive step stays positive | (C) | 3,160 positions for $k \le 81$, and the same 183 signs for $82 \le k \le 264$; none for $k \ge 265$ |
| the same rows: a column that has not yet turned positive keeps the next from turning positive | (Mono) | two signs per column for $9 \le k \le 264$ (512) and 39 facts for $k \le 8$; none for $k \ge 265$ |
| $n = 2k$ with $k = 8$; $n = 2k$ with $2 \le k \le 7$ | direct comparison | 1 + 6 = 7 |

The review accepted this amount of computation at the level of the root on three standing inputs together: the fourth, by which the user had accepted a root closed with the rows $n \le 300$, and the eighth and ninth, which make the kind and the size of a computation the standard and not its presence. It also wrote down what the acceptance costs. With the root formally proved, attempts and explorations on it stop, while three things remain true only because a table says so, in the Lean proof and in the accepted informal proof alike, with no published argument that says why they hold:[^377] (L) in the columns 21 to 44, the place of the crossing in the columns from about 21 to 96, and the persistence of sign in small columns outside the range the estimates cover.

The frontier's standing question about the root as a whole, which the system itself had placed there (section 5.4), was closed in the way the system closes it whenever a root is formally proved, with its fixed wording, "Resolved by the root's formal settlement, not by an explanation". The words are the program's[^378] and are not a session's judgment of this proof. Record and review both say that there is no new mathematics in the final record.

When the formal proof was published, the root had further derivations on the frontier besides the formal one: the informal relations through the left part and right half, through (Col) and (Mono), through Target T, and, since 07:00 that morning, through the staircase (Col) and (Diag).

The diagonal statement (Diag), by which a step that is not positive is followed on its diagonal by another that is not positive, had been proved informally on 23 November at 07:00,[^379] again by citation. Of the sixteen nodes that were proved at the commit considered here it was the last to become so: its solution was accepted nearly ten hours after the root had been proved informally and seventy minutes before the formal proof was published, and it had no formal proof. Its only newly written argument is three lines long. If column $k$ changes sign once, at its crossing row $q_0(k)$, then $d_{n,k} \le 0$ means $n \lt q_0(k)$, and $d_{n+1,k+1} \le 0$ follows as soon as column $k + 1$ crosses strictly later. So for a column that changes sign once, (Diag) follows from the strict increase of the crossing rows. (The fact itself, the record notes, was already in four earlier records.)

The proof uses this for the columns 45 to 264, where Theorem N′ gives the single crossing and the strict increase of $q_0$ is read from the paper's table. For $k \ge 265$ the proof needs no table and does not go through the order of the crossings: (CI) puts the crossing within deficit $k/3$, so Theorem N′ gives the single crossing and a non-positive step lies in the region of Theorem 5.9; there a theorem of the evening before shows that it is followed on the diagonal by a negative one. For the columns up to 44 the cases themselves are read from the table. The record says that it overcomes no new difficulty: why two neighbouring columns below 265 never cross in the same row remains a fact of the table.

Neither the informal proof of the root of 22 November nor the formal proof of 23 November uses (Diag). When the root was proved informally on 22 November at 21:19, (Diag) was unresolved, and the relations through which the root then followed from (L) have (Diag) nowhere among their premises: (L) $\Rightarrow$ (S_L) with (S_L) $\wedge$ (S_R) $\Rightarrow$ root, (L) $\Rightarrow$ (Col) with (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root, and (L) $\wedge$ (T_R) $\Rightarrow$ Target T $\Rightarrow$ root.[^558] The formal proof passes through the five node theorems that the audit lists; the final record names (Diag) in none of its files, and the review of that record, which mentions the acceptance of (Diag) of that morning, says that its verdicts do not rest on it.[^559]

What (Mono) has to do with (Diag) was told where (Mono) was posed (section 6.2), and it is recalled here. The staircase reduction that was handed over asks, through (Diag), that the crossing rows increase strictly with $k$; (Mono), posed on the first night, asks only that they never decrease, and the record that posed it says that the root needs no more than this weaker statement. (Mono) follows from (Diag) by an accepted relation, and the relation (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root differs from the staircase relation (Col) $\wedge$ (Diag) $\Rightarrow$ root in that one premise. Of the three relations through which the root followed in the informal proof, one is (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root. The formal proof is the Lean form of the proof of another of the three, (S_L) $\wedge$ (S_R) $\Rightarrow$ root, and the theorem of the node (Mono) is one of the five node theorems through which it passes: it enters the right half together with (C) and the inequality of the middle step. By this account's reading, (Mono) thus stands in the relations into the root where the staircase has (Diag): it is the weaker statement that gives the root together with (Col), and it is the one of the two that one route of the informal proof and the formal proof use.[^560]

What the informal proof of (Diag) added is on the frontier. The staircase relation had been accepted on the first evening, when the two nodes were posed, and since (Col) had come to stand with the root, on the evening of 22 November, it had lacked only (Diag). From 23 November at 07:00 both of its premises were proved informally, so that the frontier counted four informal derivations of the root where it had counted three. Four further accepted relations have (Diag) among their premises, and each leads to a node that was proved already, formally or informally: to (Diag₁), to (Mono), with (C) to (S_R), and with (CI) to Target T.[^561]

Seven nodes were still unresolved when the formal proof was published, none of them needed: (X), (Base), (E), and four that the opening of section 6 names as nodes on which the story does not turn, (SP), (L2°), (DR) and (Z). Their statements are in the table of section 6.10.

Four of the five operator questions with which the instance had begun (section 5.4) were still open, among them the one that asks what object, at finite $n$, plays the part that makes the limit distribution unimodal. Answers proposed to two of them had been refused by their reviews.[^380] The one resolved was the question about (C), which had asked from what regularity of the terms of the column equation (C) follows and whether that regularity comes from the complete monotonicity of the Borel distribution. It was resolved on 21 November at 08:33 by a record[^381] whose answer was that the regularity lies in the sizes of the entries of the neighbouring column $k - 1$ and does not come from complete monotonicity, which yields an order and an upper bound and no persistence. (The answer says that it speaks of the existing proofs, which rest on tables for the columns $k \le 150$ and for the crossing rows of the columns 151 to 264 and give the far rows of a column no proof without constants. It also says that it has not shown that no other route leads from complete monotonicity to (C).)

### 6.10 The nodes of the later instance

The later instance stated twenty-three propositions as nodes beside the root, and none of them was restated afterwards. At the commit considered here sixteen were proved: eleven formally, and five only informally, of which three ((Diag₁), Target T and (T_R)) had had no proof of their own and stood through relations from other nodes. Seven were unresolved, each still joined to the root or to a proved node by an accepted relation.

The table gives for each node its statement, the publication that proposed it, and the course of its proofs up to that commit. The statements are those of the frontier at that commit,[^382] abridged where a row says so. They are written with $d_{n,k} = b_{n,k} - b_{n,k-1}$, $x_k = (k/(k+1))^{k-1}$, $\mathfrak r_k = 1/x_k$, $p_n = (n+1)^{n-1}$, and $r_c(k)$ the least positive integer $r$ with $r^3 \gt k^2$. "By relation" means that the state came from other nodes through an accepted relation and not from a proof of the node itself; "set back" refers to the operator's changes of 20 and 21 November (sections 6.4 and 6.5).

At that commit the frontier holds thirty relations among these nodes and the root, and a second table lists them all.[^418] Each row is one relation: the nodes in its second column, taken together, imply the node in its first. Relations into the same node stand together, in the order in which the first table has the nodes, and the relations into the root stand last. The third column gives the state of the premises at that commit, as the frontier marks it. Where it says proved, every premise of the row was proved, formally or informally, and the frontier counts the node of the first column as informally proved through that relation; this is so in twenty-three rows. Where it names a node, that premise was unresolved, and the other premises of the row, where it has others, were proved; these are the other seven rows, one for each of the seven unresolved nodes.
