The frontier holds accepted relations only: one that a review refused, or whose acceptance was withdrawn, is not on it. Two relations of the table nevertheless have the premise and the conclusion of a relation that was refused or withdrawn: each of the two was proposed again by a later record, with another proof, and accepted.[^562] The first leads from (Mono_265) to (S_R), as did one of the two relations whose acceptance the operator withdrew on 21 November (section 6.5): the proof of the withdrawn relation had obtained the bound on the crossing row a second time, and a record of 22 November proposed the relation with a proof that does not, which was accepted at 11:43.[^563] The second leads from (CI) to (Diag), as did one of the three relations that a review refused on the evening of 22 November (section 6.8): the record that proved (Diag) informally proposed it again with a shortened proof, in which, by its review, none of the four grounds of the refusal remains, and it was accepted with the solution of (Diag) on 23 November at 07:00.[^564] Of the other relations told of in those two places, from (Mono) to (S_R), from (CI) alone to Target T and from (Diag₁) to (Diag), the frontier has none.

For two pairs of statements of unlike range that the table joins, this account says what the relation between them draws on beside its premise. For (L) and (Col), each of which follows from the other, it is said after the list at the opening of section 6. The other pair is (CI), a statement about one position in each column $k \ge 260$, and (Diag), a statement about every position. The proof of the relation from (CI) to (Diag) is the proof by which (Diag) itself was accepted (section 6.9): it takes from (CI) only the bound on the crossing row in the columns $k \ge 265$, has everything else in those columns from published claims that their reviews supported, and in the columns up to 264 rests, beside such claims and the record's own argument of three lines (section 6.9), on a finite remainder read from the paper's table.[^565]

The last five rows are the relations into the root: four whose premises are all proved, and one, from (X), whose premise is unresolved. So the root stands there informally in four ways and formally once. The second of the four, the staircase relation, is the one whose premises the informal proof of (Diag) completed (section 6.9).

## 7. Looking across the two instances

### 7.1 The user's interventions

The standing inputs have been described where they fell. Set side by side they show one concern pursued for two weeks through two instances, and they explain why the later instance did not simply repeat the earlier one.

In the earlier instance the user intervened on the conduct of the research and left the standard of review alone; each of the inputs of 9 November says so (section 4.7). The texts of 8 November describe research in which more was being confirmed and no more understood, and they decline to instruct. The decision of 9 November on computation asks for more thinking and less range-filling. The input of that night names what is wanted, the reason why the whole row is unimodal, and corrects the operator's suggestion that thin margins might mean there is no reason.

The sessions responded as far as conduct can respond: they stopped certifying columns, they wrote sections on the question into their records, and their reviews recorded each time that the question remained open. But, as this account reads it, a proof by regions and certificates was by then half built, the acceptance of a proof did not depend on its being short or explanatory, and the root was closed two days later in the form it had.

In the later instance the same concern was built into the terms, and then, when that proved not to be enough, into the standard of acceptance itself. The rules of the handoff restricted computation and required everything to be proved anew (section 5.4). The second and third inputs, on the first day, again concern conduct (section 6.1): prove and do not measure; face the core; cite what exists and do not reinvent it. The third input adds, in the user's words: where something is already complete but its logic is untidy, work to make it concise, to remove premises, to delete conditions, to widen its range, to connect what had seemed separate.

The fifth input, a day later, made that last request a condition of acceptance (section 6.4). The fifth input itself states this link, in a section that the operator marks as its own reading and not the user's words: the third had asked it of the way sessions work, the fifth makes it part of what a review requires. The user's quoted sentence in the fifth does not mention the third. The sixth said how the judgment is to be made, from the proof and not from counts, and the seventh extended the condition to the proofs of relations (section 6.5).

The eighth, ninth and tenth then each took back something that had made the standard stricter than the user meant. The eighth took back the idea that shortening is for economy of formalization, and the idea that computation as such counts against a proof. The ninth took back the idea that a finite remainder of a few thousand facts read from the paper's table is computation of the unwelcome kind (both inputs are told in section 6.5). The tenth took back the idea that a wrong side sentence sinks a correct claim (section 6.8).

The operator's part in this can be stated plainly, because the sources are explicit about it. The operator wrote the inputs; from the third on it quoted the user and marked its own readings as its own. Its glosses on the fifth and seventh inputs were wrong in two respects and were corrected by the eighth; an earlier version of the eighth, in force for nine minutes, was itself too wide and was replaced.

Four times the operator changed the published verdicts of reviews directly (sections 6.4 and 6.5), on five solutions and two relations, and each change is marked in the file as the operator's and not a re-review.[^383] Three times it did so on the user's instruction or decision. The fourth time, in withdrawing the two relations, it applied to them, on its own judgment, the standard that the user had just extended to the proofs of relations, as the seventh input says.

After 21 November at 08:37 the operator changed no verdict. From then on the reviews applied the standard of the inputs themselves, and of the thirty-three solutions that stand refused at the commit considered here, twenty-eight were refused by reviews. Every solution that the operator set back was replaced later by a shorter one that a review accepted, except that of (Mono), the statement that a column cannot turn positive before the column to its left has. (Mono) stood again by relation and then by its Lean proof.

The table lists the standing inputs of the two instances in order of time, with the instance of each (E the earlier, L the later) and what it asked or decided. The inputs of the later instance are counted in it, as everywhere in this account, by their order of submission (section 2.2). The first is the handoff. The second and the third, of the first day, concern the conduct of the research (section 6.1). The fourth brought in a paper. The fifth to the twelfth carry the user's later rulings: the fifth to the tenth on the standard of acceptance (this section), the eleventh and twelfth on the use of the machine (section 7.5). Two notices of the operator are not counted: the notice that followed the interruption of 20 November (section 6.3) and the survey of 22 November (section 6.8).

The file names of most of these inputs are given with the milestones in section 8, and the others where the input is described.

### 7.2 The role of computation

Computation did three different jobs in this research, and the history of the user's interventions is largely the history of separating them.

The first job is experiment: exact rows are computed from the last-car recurrence of section 4.2, and in them candidate properties are tested and counterexamples found. Both instances lived on it. The earlier instance's first records found the failure of log-concavity at (80,40) this way. The later instance and the night of 19 November refuted a long list of stronger properties by exact witnesses, and several structural theorems were first seen in tables. No input objects to this job. The second input of the later instance objects to a degenerate form of it, scanning large ranges to see where something breaks before having an argument for which the scan is needed.

The second job is the base case: a finite range that no general argument can reach because the statement behaves differently there. The step at the centre of a row is negative for $2 \le n \le 15$ (section 4.5). The increase of a column from row $2k + 1$ on fails at four small positions, (5,2), (7,3), (9,4) and (11,5). Every proof in this history has such a base.

The third job is to stand in for an argument: to close a range that an argument might cover but does not yet. The earlier instance's proof used computation in this way throughout (section 4.10). All rows up to $n = 20000$, the right half up to $n = 50000$, whole columns up to $k = 3130$, and inequalities in the general range verified cell by cell in interval or double-precision arithmetic: these are where its general arguments begin. This account reads the records as showing that their ranges were set by where estimates with explicit constants happened to become strong enough. The user's decision of 9 November stopped the widening of such ranges but could not change the shape of a proof already built on them.

The later instance was founded on the refusal of the third job, and then had to find where the line runs. The handoff allowed tables for $n \le 30$ only. The fourth input admitted the paper's rows $n \le 300$, as something already computed (section 5.4). For a day proofs leaned on those rows, and on the paper's larger table to $n = 600$, as freely as they were allowed.

Under the fifth and sixth inputs proofs were then set back for what they could shed (sections 6.4 and 6.5). In that night the larger table and the paper's rational computation went out of the derivation that leads to (Mono), and thresholds fell by orders of magnitude: a single-crossing theorem, which says that a column changes sign exactly once, came down from $k \ge 10^8$ to $k \ge 350$, and the column bound (CB) from $k \ge 30000$ to $k \ge 148$. The larger table stayed in that night's proof of (Col), the statement that a column which has turned non-negative stays so, and in the diagonal statements until the evening of 22 November.[^384] The seventh input extended the standard to the proofs of relations the next morning.

The eighth and ninth inputs fixed the line in the user's words. What counts is not the presence of computation, nor the number of cells read, but whether a part of a statement is being shown true by a large computation that does not explain why it is true. A remainder of a few thousand facts in rows up to 162 was ruled to be acceptable.

What computation remains in the two final proofs of the later instance can be stated exactly. The informal proof of the left increase (L), hence of the root on the left, reads from the paper's table 1,360 facts for the columns 21 to 44 and 48 signs for the crossings of the columns 49 to 96. The columns 45 to 48 follow from $q_0(49) = 64$ and the strict increase of $q_0$, which are also table facts ($q_0$ is the crossing row of section 6.2). For the columns 6 to 20 the proof uses the base table, directly for the columns 6 to 11 and through a theorem for the columns 12 to 20, as section 6.8 tells, and for $k \ge 97$ it uses no table.

The formal proof has the Lean kernel compute 6,118 facts in rows $n \le 300$ (section 6.9). The share of computation is larger in it than in the informal proof, the columns 45 to 81 being closed by table where the informal proof has a theorem. Both reviews name the small columns of (L) and the position of the crossing as unexplained; the review of the formal proof adds the persistence of sign in the small columns of (C).

### 7.3 The role of Lean

In the earlier instance formalization began on the second day and was stopped on the fifth. It produced one accepted formal theorem, about an auxiliary statement that is a consequence of the root (the node D of the earlier instance, section 4.3), and a formal model of the parking process with the count $(n+1)^{n-1}$ and the last-car recurrence proved for the actual counts. When the pause came (section 4.7), five formalizations of nodes on the path to the root were in progress and were ended unpublished. Their state shows what formalizing that proof would have required: for the central step alone, an analytic theorem for $n \ge 19999$ and a certificate in thousands of rows; in two days the formalizer had compiled the analytic theorem and, of the certificate, the rows up to 1808.[^385] The reviews of the right-half proof say that a Lean proof of it would have to rebuild each of the computer proofs it depends on.

In the later instance formalization was a condition of success from the start, and the fifth input placed the user's standard of acceptance before it: a proof is to be made as simple as it can be before it is formalized. (The reason, by the eighth input, is conciseness itself and not the cost of formalizing.)

Sixteen formalizer sessions were started up to the commit considered here. Two were cancelled when the proofs they were formalizing were set back, ten led to the formal records listed in the table of this section, and four were still at work. The first of the two, the formalizer of the first accepted proof of (CI), the proof that section 6.4 tells of,[^386] was cancelled on 20 November at 18:54, within a minute of the fifth input and before the commit that set its proof back; section 6.6 tells of the second. By the operator's own note on each, both were cancelled by the operator, the formalization of (CI) on 20 November and that of (Mono_265) on 21 November.[^387]

The formal development is small in its foundations and uniform in its method: the definitions of the process, one recurrence proved from them, formal power series, and tables computed by the Lean kernel from recurrences proved correct. The table lists the nodes in the order in which they received their formal proofs, with the Lean theorem of each, the time of its publication and the record.

| node | theorem | published | record |
|---|---|---|---|
| (Mono_265) | `Parking.mono265` | 21 Nov 15:02 | L seq 62 `r3e5dcd7e` |
| (L1°) size convexity | `Parking.size_convexity` | 22 Nov 02:59 | L seq 84 `rabec2373` |
| (CB) | `Parking.column_growth_bound` | 22 Nov 17:02 | L seq 102 `r78a97aa6` |
| (C) | `Parking.chain_column` | 22 Nov 21:37 | L seq 105 `r538f630c` |
| (D) | `Parking.FLC.first_layer_convexity_tree`, `Parking.FLC.first_layer_convexity` | 22 Nov 21:47 | L seq 106 `r8a9fd8d8` |
| (S_R) | `Parking.right_half` | 23 Nov 04:25 | L seq 112 `rebe4c3c` |
| (Mono) | `Parking.mono`, `Parking.mono_via_chain_column` | 23 Nov 04:39 | L seq 113 `r5a5749f3` |
| (CI) | `Parking.curve_increase` | 23 Nov 05:49 | L seq 115 `rd4cb2be4` |
| (Col₁) | `Parking.col1`, `Parking.col1_table` | 23 Nov 06:33 | L seq 117 `r27a01811` |
| (L) | `Parking.left_increase`, `Parking.left_increase_cc` | 23 Nov 08:10 | L seq 120 `r3d3d37ac` |
| (S_L) | `Parking.left_part` | 23 Nov 08:10 | the same |
| root | `Parking.root_unimodal` | 23 Nov 08:10 | the same |

Of these, the root's proof depends on the theorems of (Mono_265), (C), (Mono), (L) and (S_L). Every formal record states, and every review confirms by compiling it afresh, that the proof uses no `sorry` (the word with which Lean lets a proof be left out), no `native_decide`, and no axioms beyond `propext`, `Classical.choice` and `Quot.sound`. The toolchain was Lean 4.32.0 with Mathlib throughout.

Formalization also fed back into the mathematics. A decay property of the kernel of the column equation was found while formalizing (CB), and the formal proof has it where the accepted proof in words has another inequality, the one line of section 6.7 that bounds this kernel. For $k \ge 148$ the weights $\kappa_m$ of the kernel, as section 6.8 writes them in the critical tilt, satisfy $(m+1) \cdot \kappa_{m+1} \le (m-1) \cdot \kappa_m$: for $k \le m \le k + \lceil 3k/5 \rceil - 2$, the range in which the formalizer found it, the inequality is *formally proved*, inside the Lean proof of (CB), and for every $m \ge k$ the peer then proved it, in a proof in words that the review *accepted* as a claim and that was not put into Lean.[^388] Of the two formal proofs of (D), the one its review recommends is a different and simpler proof than the informal one, found by the peer. And the observation that assembled the root, that the proof of (L) yields the middle step of the right half as one more row, was made by a peer checking a formalization (section 6.9).

### 7.4 What was carried from the earlier instance, and what the later one did that the earlier had not

Three kinds of thing can be distinguished.

The first is what reached the later instance from the earlier one, through the manuscript, the night's reports and the paper, and was proved again there. It is the exact structure of the problem: the outcome permutation with its capacities, the forest representation, the single chain in the right half (section 4.2), and the decomposition of a step into a curvature term and a boundary term, which is the earlier identity of ties and blocked maxima in another form (the split $d_{n,k} = \Delta - B_-$ of section 4.5). With it came, and were proved again there, the cross product of two consecutive steps (section 5.3), the critical tilt $x_k$ with the growth inequality for the cumulative counts that goes with it (section 4.8), and the knowledge that the row is not log-concave and that a list of stronger properties fail. The last-car recurrence is from the literature and is present at every stage.

The second is what existed in the earlier instance and did not reach the later one, and was found again or not used. The reduction of unimodal rows to columns that change sign once at a row that does not decrease is of this kind (section 4.8): the first sessions of the later instance found a reduction of this kind again, the night of 19 November having found the neighbouring reduction through the diagonal statement; section 6.2 sets the two reductions beside the relations (Col) $\wedge$ (Mono) $\Rightarrow$ root and (Col) $\wedge$ (Diag) $\Rightarrow$ root of the later instance. So are the two certified counterexamples found again that night (found again, that is, by the inference stated in section 5.2). And so is the earlier proof itself, which the later instance could read in the manuscript but was not allowed to use: its cut of the left half into layers, its induction on the right, its certificates.

The third is what the later instance did that the earlier had not. The reduction of the root to statements about columns is not among these things: the earlier instance had proved it, had found that the soft argument it had tried could not give the column statement, and had not made that statement a node. What the later instance did was to take that reduction as its road. It made (Col), (Mono) and (C) nodes and proved them, and (L) as a single statement for all $k \ge 6$. It wrote the column equation in a form with a positive kernel and one negative shock, and bounded the row at which a column turns positive, without computation for $k \ge 97$. It extended the paper's theorem on the persistence of a decrease from a curve to a strip, in a closed form short enough to formalize. It proved the first-layer convexity (D) without threshold. It joined two mechanisms into the theorem that a column which has crossed early never returns (Theorem N′, section 6.8). And it formalized the root, in a Lean proof built from (C), (Mono), (L) and the strip theorem, in which some of these arguments are replaced by tables that the Lean kernel computes (section 6.9). The earlier instance had explicit estimates beginning at $n = 20001$, $k = 6668$ or $k = 3131$ and computation below; the later instance has arguments beginning at $k = 44$, 97 or 265 and a few thousand table entries below.

What neither did is to answer the question the user put on 9 November, why the whole row must be unimodal (section 4.7). The later proof is shorter. In this account's judgment its three column statements say more plainly than the earlier proof's many regions what unimodality consists of. But its reviews, like the earlier ones, record that parts of it are true only because a table says so, and four of the five questions with which the instance began, which ask for the explanation, were still open when the root was closed.

### 7.5 How the two instances were run

The two instances differ in scale and in pace, and some turns of the story are turns of the machinery and not of the mathematics. The table sets the two side by side: the sessions of each by role, the models on which they ran, the number of sessions allowed at once, the candidates refused publication, the time from a producer's start to publication, and the publications of each day.[^566]

| | earlier instance | later instance, to the commit considered |
|---|---|---|
| sessions | 1,263: 310 attempts, 100 explorations, 12 formalizers, 382 peers, 375 reviews, 84 organizers | 352: 52 attempts, 35 explorations and the 6 inserted packages, 16 formalizers, 106 peers, 117 reviews, 20 organizers |
| models | producers on `codex/gpt-6.1-sol` until 7 Nov 18:06, then `claude/claude-fable-5-1`; peers and reviews on `codex/gpt-6.1-sol` until 6 Nov 19:49 (four peers of 5 Nov on a fallback model), then `claude/claude-opus-5-5`; organizers on `codex/gpt-6.1-sol` | every session on Claude models (`claude/fable` for producers, peers and organizers, `claude/opus` for reviews); the second model named in the settings was never launched |
| sessions at once | 8 at the start; 12 from 9 Nov; 16 from 10 Nov; 8 and 10 on 12 Nov (and 4 for about an hour that night, in which no session was launched, by the operator's own record of the settings); 6 on 13 Nov | 8 at the start; between 4 and 16 afterwards; 8 from 23 Nov 00:51 |
| candidates refused publication | 17, none with mathematical content (16 reports of "nothing free to work on", all before 6 Nov 10:24, and 1 empty); from that time a session that ended without research ended without peer or review, and 44 did so | none |
| time from a producer's start to publication (median) | 1.1 hours on 5–6 Nov, about 5 hours from 7 Nov | about 11 hours |
| publications per day | 17, 94, 45, 18, 35, 81, 79, 57, 12 | 5, 31, 42, 30, 12 |

The program that runs the sessions was rewritten under the earlier instance during its first five days, up to the evening of 9 November, and then left alone. Four of the changes are singled out here, because the sources connect them with the course of the research.

On the evening of 5 November, after four hours in which the frontier held only two nodes and the same theorems were being proved twice in parallel, attempts were allowed to share a node by registering sections of it, the device described in section 2.2. The user chose this over forcing a finer split of the nodes, and refused a rule that sections be drawn narrowly, on the ground that narrowing loses patterns visible only in the whole.[^389]

On 6 November at 14:17 a session that ends its work was made to resume in the same conversation, up to three times, and to decide whether it may stop, with the instruction to stay with the unresolved difficulty.[^390] Together with the stronger models for peers and reviews that evening and for producers the next day, this is when the time a record took rose from one hour to five (the medians of the table). On the same evening the rule was set that a doubt raised by a later record does not suspend an earlier result, and that only the finding of a dedicated re-review withdraws it.[^391] Three such re-reviews took place and withdrew five claims, none on a path to the root.[^392]

On 9 November the questions were introduced (section 4.7), after an analysis had found that the frontier could hold only statements with exact implications, so that questions of understanding were not there to be chosen.[^393]

Three interruptions of the earlier instance were of the machine and not of the research: a restart by the user on 6 November at 09:56, an unexpected stop of the machine on 10 November, and the restart on 13 November at 00:59. By the times that the sources give, the stop of the machine fell at 10:08:34. The runner had last launched a session at 10:08:07; nine of the twelve sessions then running wrote their last lines at 10:08:34, among them notices that their connection had dropped; and the system log gives the same second as the time at which the system started again.[^394] How long the machine was down was not established. The system log, in an entry written at 10:08:43, after the restart, gives 09:58:59 as the time of the previous shutdown of the system and calls that shutdown unexpected, and the summary that closes the extract of the log calls 09:58:59 the last time the system was alive. That time lies nine and a half minutes before the stop: after it the runner launched sessions at 09:59:35 and at 10:08:07, and the sessions wrote on until 10:08:34.[^414]

When the runner was started again on 10 November at 10:30 it found all twelve sessions that had been running dead. By this account's reading, the stop of the machine twenty-two minutes earlier is what ended them; no source states it.[^567]

That only five publications appeared between noon and midnight on 8 November, by contrast, was not an interruption: all eight sessions were occupied by pipelines of ten to twenty hours, whose records appeared the following night.

The later instance ran on the same generation of the program, with one change made for it before it started, the three sub-reviewers of a review,[^395] and several made while it ran.[^396] Its interruptions were the insertion of the paper (section 5.4), the command that ended every process (section 6.3), and the restarts of the runner that followed the operator's four changes of verdicts. There were three restarts, on 20 November at 20:07 and on 21 November at 01:39 and 08:37, the first serving two changes.

On the morning of 22 November, between the publications of 04:11 and 10:48 that section 6.7 tells, the later instance stood still. No session was launched between 03:28 and 09:13, the log of the Lean broker, the program that queues the instance's Lean compilations (section 6.8), is silent from 05:14 to 09:15, and nothing was published between 04:56 and 10:48. The sources that were read do not say why.

Two things about its running bear on the last two days. The first is that seventeen reviews and five peers failed and had to be repeated, eleven of the reviews because their sub-reviewers, running in the background, were stopped ten minutes after the reviewing session's turn ended.[^397]

The second is that the machine, with four cores and sixteen gigabytes of memory, was shared from the afternoon of 22 November with a second instance on another problem. That evening a Lean compilation waited a median of twenty-three minutes from queue to end, against a minute and a half the day before. The formalizations of (Mono), (CI) and (S_R), which their formalizers had finished between 17:07 and 18:29 on 22 November, were published between 04:25 and 05:49 the next morning. The eleventh and twelfth inputs and the lowering of the session limit belong to this congestion.

## 9. What was checked

This section tells how the account was made and how it was checked; how far the account can be trusted comes, in brief, to this. Section 2.3, sections 4 to 7 and the milestone table were compared with the primary files, sentence by sentence, by five checkers who had not written the account, and the three revisions that followed were checked by sessions that had not made them: in the first and the third the old text was compared with the new, claim by claim, and in the second and the third every sentence added from the sources, or changed in what it says of them, was verified against its source. No one in the making of the account or in the revisions that added to it verified a proof, compiled a Lean file or repeated a computation of the records, so that where the account calls a statement proved it reports what the reviews of the two instances did, and whether the mathematics is right was not examined. Some passages were checked less than the rest: of the passages replaced after the first check, the 53 that concern the earlier instance and the comparison of the two instances and still stand in the text were not compared with the sources a second time, apart from the mathematical passages of sections 4.2 to 4.10 and the comparison of the two reductions, which were later rewritten and compared; and sections 1 to 2.2 and 3 were checked only as far as they repeat later sections or where the second or the third revision added or changed a sentence there from the sources. The unit "What was not checked" gives these limits in full, and the unit "What was not found" lists what the sources that were read do not say.

**How the account was made.** Twenty-four source readers divided the material. Twelve read the 438 publications of the earlier instance in order of time, eight the 120 of the later, two the documents written between the instances, and two the running of each instance: its logs, settings, session files and inputs, and the memory notes of the sessions that acted as the operator. For each publication the readers had its event, the record's description and claims, and the review's verdicts. They read in full the expositions and reviews of the records they judged decisive, and not of the others. One assembler wrote the account from the reports of the source readers. It read for itself several of the standing inputs of the later instance, the two root statements and a few records, and nothing else of the primary material.

**What "proved" means here.** No one in this process verified a proof, compiled a Lean file or repeated a computation of the records. The things done in the making of the account that come nearest are these: one of the readers who went back to the records for the mathematical passages checked the algebra by which the two formulas of section 5.2 agree; one of the checkers searched the Lean files of the record of the root's formal proof for the objects named in section 6.6, without reading the proof bodies of most of them; and two of the checkers, as they compared the account with its sources, recomputed a few numbers that the account or a record states, among them the capacities of the outcome that section 4.2 gives as an example, their product and the sum of its seven counts. The mathematical passages tell the mathematics directly, and what they tell is what the records state and how their arguments go, as far as the readers followed them. Each stretch says once what standing its statements have, and a statement whose standing differs from that of its stretch says so in its own sentence. Section 2.2 defines the words for the standings, among them *formally proved*, *accepted* and *certified by computation*, and the two levels, informal and formal, at which a review accepts the solution of a node. The words say what a review of the instance did with a claim, a solution or a relation, or that the statement has no such standing. Where this account calls a statement proved at one of the two levels, accepted or certified by computation, it therefore reports verdicts of the reviews of the instances on what their records state; of a formal proof it reports that the review says it compiled the Lean proof. It reports nothing that was established in the making of this account: whether the mathematics is right was not verified.

**The check against the sources.** Five checkers who had not written the account compared it with the primary files, sentence by sentence, each for a group of sections and for the rows of the milestone table that fall in its period. Between them they covered section 2.3, sections 4 to 7 and every row of the table, and each row was opened at its coordinate. They found 10 statements wrong, 89 imprecise (a time, a number, a range, a name, or a paraphrase that said more or less than its source), 12 inferences stated as fact and 3 statements without support. All of these were corrected or marked.

The errors of most consequence were in the comparison of the two instances, which no single source reader had been placed to see. The account had said that the reduction to columns, of the kind that the later instance came to anew, was absent from the earlier instance, where the earlier instance had proved it on 10 November. It had identified a node of the later instance with an inequality of the earlier one that concerns a different count. And it had understated how much of the earlier proof the later sessions were able to read.

**The reading tests and the return to the records.** A reader given only the text then reported what it had understood and where it could not follow. Its main findings were that the assembly of the final proof was nowhere shown, that nodes were named without being stated, and that the mathematical passages named ideas where they should have stated them. Four further readers went back to the records for these places. From the reports of these four readers the mathematical passages of sections 4.2 to 4.10, 5.2 to 5.4 and 6.2 to 6.9 were rewritten, and section 6.10 was added. On the way they found some thirty more places where the earlier wording had been wrong, among them the definition of the layers, the statement of the pair inequality, the mechanism of the entry theorem, the statement of Theorem N′, and the difference between the reductions of 10 and of 19 November.

Two checkers then compared the rewritten passages of sections 4 and 6 with the reports of the four readers who went back to the records, and with the records where those reports left a doubt. They found 39 places to correct, of which the ones that mattered were a range lost from the certified ratio of section 4.6, a formula made to carry three limits in section 4.8, and a factor counted twice in the description of the node (D); these were corrected. A second reader with nothing but the text read the revised account, and the contradictions and broken references it found between old and new passages were removed. Its remark that the proofs of the earlier instance are told more fully than some of the later one was met only in part.

**The checks that were repeated.** When the account was first finished, three comparisons were missing: two checkers had been stopped before they could report by a safeguard of the system, which the assembler's notes give as the safeguards of the model for the one and a safety classifier for the other, and the passages supplied by the last of the four readers who went back to the records had been put in as they came. All three were then given to checkers on another model.

The first had been the task of one of the two stopped checkers: the passages rewritten from the report of the third of the four readers who went back to the records (sections 4.1, 4.7, 4.11 and 5 and the close of section 6.9, from the root's further derivations on the frontier to the end of that section) were compared with that report and, where it left a doubt, with the sources. There were 15 findings, 2 of them statements that were wrong, and all were corrected. The second concerns the passages supplied by the last of the four readers: the passages on the proofs of (C), (CB), (CI), (Diag) and of (L) in the large columns and on the middle step were compared with the records. There were 10 findings, 2 of them wrong, and all were corrected; this checker did not finish looking through sections 7 and 8 for sentences that state the same facts in other words. The third had been the task of the other stopped checker: of the passages replaced after the first check, the 48 that still stand in the text and concern the days between the instances and the later instance (sections 5, 6.1 to 6.6, 7.1, 7.4 and 7.5 and rows of the milestone table) were each compared with the sources. None was wrong, 9 were imprecise and were corrected, and 2 could not be checked because they rest only on the private transcript (the times and the wording of the user's messages of 04:10 and of 11:09 and 11:37 on 19 November).

**The revision of the wording.** After these checks the account was revised three times. In the first, the wording of the whole account was revised for how it reads. This revision was made in eight parts, by AI sessions that worked from one sheet of common decisions and under the instruction that no claim, number, time, name or qualification was to change. Readers who had only the text read the revised account. The old text was compared with the new claim by claim and in both directions, by checkers who had taken no part in the revision. The first comparison, made after the rewriting, covered the whole text, part by part. Two more followed, one after the repair of what the first comparison and the readers had found and one after the last repair, and each covered the passages that its repair had changed. A last comparison covered one reference to two sections, which was restored after the third comparison had found it narrowed. In none of these comparisons was a claim of the old text found missing. What the comparisons did find was a qualification weakened or lost, a mark of inference separated from its claim, a coordinate standing behind another statement than before, or an order of events or a reason that the old text did not give; at each such place the content of the old text was restored. A few clauses that the new text had added, and that other sections of the account or `SOURCES.md` support, were kept. A script also compared the code spans, numbers, quotations and coordinates of the old and the new text after every version, and all 412 code spans and all 280 coordinates of the old text were in the new one. What the readers still could not follow led to the second revision.

**The explanations added from the sources.** The second revision followed a decision of the user: the account should tell how the knowledge developed without the reader having to open the records. One session went through what the readers had not been able to follow and planned 103 places. In 56 of them something named was to be explained, in 8 a step was to be told and not only its outcome, in 24 a reader could not reconcile two statements, and in 14 a word was not used steadily; the remaining place was an opening on where the problem comes from. Eight sessions carried the plan out, each for one part, from the records of the two instances and the other sources, and they gave every statement they added the coordinate of its source, except a few statements about how this account was made, which rest on its own working files. The opening on the problem was written from two files that had been made for another purpose, and from the files of the research that these two name. The two files are not a survey of the literature: they report what the files of the research say about the works it cites, how far each was read and what it contains, and only the bibliographic data of the works were confirmed elsewhere, in registries. The published works themselves were not opened for this account. Every sentence added or changed was then verified against its source by a checker who had not written it, and these checkers opened the sources themselves. They reported 72 places: sentences that said more than their source, less, or something else, among them a few conclusions of the account's own, which were removed; coordinates that were incomplete or named a passage that does not hold the statement; and doubts. All 72 were repaired by sessions that opened the source again. The repaired sentences were verified once more, by checkers who had made neither the sentence nor the repair, and five further corrections followed. One of the checkers was stopped by a safeguard before it reported; its part, sections 4.6 to 4.11, was verified instead by two checkers on another model. As in the making of the account, no one in this revision verified a proof, compiled a Lean file or repeated a computation. The explanations as this revision added them said what the records state and how their arguments go; the third revision, which is told below, changed that form. That the added explanations agreed with the records was verified in this revision; whether the mathematics is right was not.

Where a reader had not been able to reconcile two statements, the sources of both were opened. Where both statements say what their sources say, the account now says so at the place, in the text or in the note of the statement, and does not decide between them; where a source itself joins the two, its statement is given as that source's. Where the account itself had slipped against a source that is plain, it was corrected to the source. The corrections are these. In section 3 what is said of how the final proof treats the columns below 6 and the row of the middle step was corrected; the earlier text had spoken of a short table. Sections 4.5 and 4.10 had given integer interval arithmetic for the certificates of all the columns up to 500; the certificates are exact for the columns up to 120 and in integer interval arithmetic for the columns 121 to 500. In section 5.2 two things were corrected: which place is the first that is hit, and a condition that asks for at least a certain value, where the earlier text had said that the value is exceeded. In section 5.3 the hypothesis and the conclusion of the paper's Theorem 5.9 were corrected, and in section 6.3 the same theorem's hypothesis that the deficit is at least 2. Section 6.2 had said that no session of the later instance could see the earlier records; it now says that those records were not among what the sessions of the later instance were given. The same section had called the first step of a column an entry. Section 6.7 had said that the refused proof of (CI) depended on two refinements; that proof closed with either of the two dropped. Section 6.8 had said that the review of 18:51 named the shape of a solution; what that review named was the form in which the relations could be proposed again. Section 7.3 now says that the formalizer of the central step had compiled the analytic branch as well as the rows up to 1808. It also says that both of the formalizers that were cancelled in the later instance were cancelled by the operator, by the operator's own note on each, where the earlier text had said that who cancelled them was not checked; the note of that sentence names the file of the runner's state that carries the two notes.

Two statements of the account were looked for in the sources in this revision and not found. Both were left in the account as they stood, and the third revision took both up again. One stood in section 5.4: that the lineage of the two instances is usually given as the later being based on the earlier. The third revision looked for this description again: no source of the research that was read describes the later instance as based on the earlier one. Section 5.4 now says this in place of the earlier statement, and it adds the nearest thing that a source says. The description arose as a question in the making of this account, which the note of the new sentence of section 5.4 tells, together with a belief about the lineage that the assembler's notes ascribe to the commission of this account; the commission itself was not opened. The other is in section 6.8: that under the eighth and ninth inputs a solution of the whole of (L) that closed the columns below 148 by reading thousands of cells of the table could not be accepted. Two records of 21 November say the like of two other nodes under the eighth input, and no source that was read states it of (L). Since the third revision the sentence opens with the mark of a reading of this account, and its note says what the sources state.

Two further statements were seen in this revision to differ in part from a source, and they too were left as they stood. The first was not corrected. It says that the memory notes were written by the sessions that acted as the operator, and it stands in this section and at the head of the notes, to which the third revision moved it from section 2.2; one of the notes cited, M-0733, was written by a session that says it committed another's changes. The second is in a note to section 4.8. The note belongs to what that section says of three inequalities, the first-layer inequality, its version with probability weights and its version with the critical tilt, and it says that E seq 263 calls the three a ladder; the ladder of that record has four rows. Since the third revision the note says so itself, and says that this account does not use the fourth row. Since that revision section 4.8 for its part sets out four versions of the first-layer inequality. The fourth of them, the flat version, is not the fourth row of that record's ladder: the two have different weights.[^568]

**The notes and the formulas.** The second revision also changed the form of the document. The coordinates, which had stood in parentheses in the sentences, were turned into notes at the end of the document, 416 of them at that stage. A script did this for the parentheses that held nothing but a coordinate, except two that it passed over, which were converted afterwards. Reversing the script, by putting every note back at its mark, gives the earlier text. That shows that the script lost nothing; it does not show that everything it put into a note belongs there. Of the parentheses that held a coordinate together with a remark, a session moved nine whole into notes, where the remark concerns the source or how this account used it, and divided seven, so that the remark stays in the sentence and the coordinate stands in a note. Another session then checked the whole conversion. It reported nine places, five of them as doubts, and all nine were changed; among them were three statements that the script had carried into notes with their coordinates, and the three were returned to the text. The formulas, which had been written in plain text, were set in LaTeX, about 1,800 of them. Three quantities of section 2.1 are now written with indices: the counts and the step, as the README beside the folder of this document writes them, and the cumulative counts in the same way. Binomial coefficients are set in the usual form, and every other formula kept, in that typesetting, the letters it had. A script compared the letters, digits and operators of every paragraph in the two forms, and checkers who had not done the typesetting compared the formulas one by one, in all eight parts. They found no difference. Three italic phrases now end or begin one symbol earlier or later than before, so that no mark of emphasis touches a formula. How the page draws the formulas was not checked for the whole document.

**The last reading.** A reader who had only the text then read the account in this form, and two more read it beside histories of other research, each for one half of it. After their reading a table of the accepted relations among the nodes was added to section 6.10 from the frontier, a remark on one source was moved from section 6.9 into a note, and a few sentences were added or reworded in sections 1, 4.2, 4.9, 6.1, 6.2, 6.4, 6.5, 6.7 and 7.3. These changes were checked by checkers who had not made them: the table against the frontier, the added sentences against the sections they rest on, and the reworded ones against what they had said. The paragraphs of this section on the first two revisions were checked in the same way against the record of the work, and corrected where they said more, less or something else than it shows, or gave a number wrongly. With these changes the document had 414 notes. The account was then read once more as a whole by a reader who had only the text. After that reading nothing was changed but what a last check of these changes found: a coordinate missing from one note, one reference in section 1, and wordings of this section.

**The mathematics told directly.** The third revision followed a decision of the user, on the report of the last reader of the second revision. That reader, who had only the text, could follow the course of the research and could not follow much of its mathematics. It named as the cause that the mathematical passages told their mathematics through which record said what, that objects had several names and letters several meanings, and that remarks on how the account was made interrupted the story. Since this revision the mathematical passages, chiefly those of sections 4.2, 4.6, 4.8, 4.9 and 6.2 to 6.9, tell the mathematics directly and say what standing their statements have, as the paragraph on what "proved" means here describes. Which record states a step, a record's own name or letter for something, and every coordinate that had still stood in the running text of sections 1 to 7 are in the notes. A sheet fixed one name for each object and one meaning for each word and letter, and the note at a changed letter gives the record's letter. Remarks on how the account was made left the running text of sections 3 to 7 for the notes of the statements they concern, and where such a remark limits what a statement claims, the limit stayed in the text. Three things were explained anew from the records, because that reader had not been able to say them: how the three ideas of the proof of the right half fit together (section 4.6), how a parking function determines its forest and how trees and blocks stand to each other (sections 4.2 and 4.8), and what (Diag) contributed at the end (the opening of section 6 and sections 6.2, 6.7 and 6.9). Seven passages changed their section. The numbered sections kept their numbers and their order, and the tables their places.

One session planned the revision: it drew up the sheet of names and established the three explanations from passages of the records, which it named. Other sessions rewrote the account from the plan, the sheet and the sources, and each listed every sentence that it had turned from a report of a source into a direct statement and every statement that it had added from a source, with the passage behind it. Sessions that had not written the text then checked it in two ways. One check opened the source of each of the 151 entries of these lists and asked whether the source states the sentence and whether the text gives it the standing that the reviews of its instance gave it. The other compared the old text with the new, claim by claim and in both directions. The two checks reported 115 places, 67 from the sources and 48 from the comparison. None was a statement that a review had refused, or that a later record had corrected, told as a fact. The most frequent fault was of one kind: a sentence of standing at the head of a stretch covered more than the stretch bears, so that an observation in computed rows, a formal expansion, an expectation of a record or a value read from a table stood as if it were proved. The others were chiefly these: a statement of "never" or "not formalized" about a node of the later instance without the limit that this account follows that instance only up to its commit of 23 November, 08:10, after which the instance formally proved further nodes; an inference of this account that had lost its mark; and a date that came from the plan and not from the records. The plan had put the first writing down of the reading of the forest off the outcome on 10 and 11 November, where the records show it on 6 and 7 November, as section 4.2 now tells it.

All these places were repaired by sessions that opened the source again, and each repair was checked by a session that had made neither the text nor the repair. These checks reported 21 further places, none of them a repaired sentence that disagreed with its source, and several in sentences that no earlier check had named. Two were slips that the account had had since it was first written: that nothing rested on (CB), where the formal proofs of (L) and of the root do not use the theorem that states (CB) and take definitions and lemmas from the record in which (CB) was formally proved (the opening of section 6 and section 6.7); and where the shape properties of the true row fail (section 4.11), which the review of the record concerned had corrected. Twenty of the 21 were repaired and one was declined. The check of that repair found eight more places, which were repaired and checked in their turn. The account has 564 notes where it had 414. A script checked after every stage that each of the 414 is still a note with one mark and keeps every code span, coordinate and number of its old text; 109 of them were extended, none was shortened, and the new ones hold chiefly what left the running text.

After these repairs two readers who had only the text read the account as a whole. Asked straight after the reading, each explained in its own words the three things that the last reader of the second revision had not been able to say, and neither found anything wrong in its explanation when it went back to the text. Both reported that the sentences on standing, as the revision had first placed them, stood between the reader and the mathematics, and that the nodes of the later instance, introduced in prose, were hard to hold. One further repair followed. It changed how the standing is said and not whether: a section says it in one sentence at its head, and a statement of another standing carries it in a word or a clause. It turned the introduction of the nodes into a list and shortened what was told more than once. This repair was compared with the text before it, claim by claim, by sessions that had not made it, and its additions were checked at their sources. These checks reported nineteen places. Eighteen were corrected, in the words that the checkers gave or, at one place, by restoring the sentences that had stood there, and one needed no change; the corrections were compared with the checkers' reports. A last reading by a reader who had only the text followed, and nothing was changed after it.

**What was not checked.** The other 53 passages replaced after the first check that still stand in the text concern the earlier instance and the comparison of the two instances (in sections 2.3, 3, 4 and 7 and in the milestone table). A checker was sent to compare them with the sources a second time and was stopped again by a safeguard before it reported; they were not sent a third time. They rest on the checker of the first round who proposed them or, where the assembler wrote them, on the assembler alone, except for the mathematical passages of sections 4.2 to 4.10 and the comparison of the two reductions, which were afterwards rewritten and compared as described above. That comparison stood then in sections 4.8 and 5.2; since the third revision it stands in sections 5.2 and 6.2.

Further, sections 1 to 2.2 and 3 were checked only as far as they repeat later sections, except for the sentences that the second revision added or changed there before it changed the form of the document and those that the third revision added or rewrote there from the sources, which were verified as described above. The same holds of the passages that stood in section 2.2 at the time of the second revision and stand since the third at the head of the notes and beside the table of section 7.1. A few sentences describe the document itself, saying where the coordinates stand and where the archive of the earlier instance can be obtained. They stood in sections 1 and 2.2, and since the third revision those of section 2.2 stand at the head of the notes. They carry no coordinate and were not verified against a source; the session that checked the conversion into notes read them against the document as it then stood. After that, the session that ran the second revision widened one of them, which checks had found too narrow, and removed another, which said more than was established. In the third revision the head of the notes was changed in two places: its sentence on where a source is named outside the notes was corrected after a search of the whole text, which a check repeated, and a sentence was added on what the notes hold beside coordinates. The figures of sections 7.2 and 7.3 that concern the earlier instance were not opened again by the checker of those sections. The older runs of October were not read. No session transcript was read, except the user's own messages in the transcript of 19 November and the last entries of the transcripts of the twelve sessions that were running when the machine stopped on 10 November, which were opened for their times and their kind (section 7.5).

In sections 4.3 to 4.9 the statements about E seq 85, 115, 117, 137, 148, 154, 162, 168, 173, 183, 190, 195, 263, 291 and 337, and about the three records of further derivations at the end of section 4.9, were taken, when the account was made, from the descriptions of these records and from the summaries of their reviews; their expositions were not read then. In the second revision four of these records were opened in part, for the explanations: of E seq 168 the assessment in the file of its review, and of E seq 263, of E seq 337 and of the third of the three records of further derivations parts of the exposition. None of the four was read whole. In the third revision every one of these records except E seq 183 was opened in part, for the standing of its statements or for a passage that is now told directly. Of each of them the assessment in the file of its review was read, and of E seq 162, 168, 173, 190, 195 and 337 and of the first of the three records of further derivations nothing else. The statements of claims or of nodes in the record's own file were read of E seq 85, 115, 117, 137, 148, 154, 263 and 291 and of the second of the three records, and passages of the exposition of E seq 115, 117, 137, 263 and 291 and of the third of the three records. None of these expositions is recorded as read whole.

As before, no one in the third revision verified a proof, compiled a Lean file or repeated a computation of the records. Its checks established that the text says what the records state and gives each statement the standing that the reviews of its instance gave it; whether the mathematics is right was not examined. To settle whether two constructions of the forest, in records of 6 and 7 November, give the same forest, one checker compared them for every permutation of length up to 7 and found that they agree; no record says so in those words, and the text of section 4.2 does not rest on it. Where a note of section 4.2 names the earliest record found for a statement, the search was a search by words through the files of the records among the first 120 publications of the earlier instance, with a further search through those among the first 29. How the page draws the formulas was not checked in this revision.

**Where sources disagreed.** The account says so at the place and, where it followed one of them, says which; it does so in the text or in the note of the statement. Among the cases are these. One is the number of routes by which the earlier instance proved the root, where the account sets the sources side by side, says in a note what one of them counts, and does not decide (section 4.9). Another is the verdicts that the operator of the later instance edited, for which the history of the review file was followed and not its present state. A third is the times of publications, which differ by up to about a minute between the file of the event and the commit; they are given here by the commit, with a few exceptions of one minute.

**What was not found.** The sources that were read do not say: why the research was begun anew on 5 November (section 4.1); who wrote the standing inputs of 5 and of 7 November (sections 4.1 and 4.5); who withdrew the outside evaluation on 7 November, or why (section 4.4); who changed the model of the producing sessions that evening (section 4.5); who restarted the earlier instance's runner in the night of 13 November, and why its sessions stopped writing at 06:35 (section 4.11); who wrote the five documents from which the paper grew (section 5.3); whether the session of 19 November opened the earlier instance (section 5.2); what the fifth input of the later instance said on 20 November at 18:54, before it was rewritten (section 6.4); why the later instance stood still on the morning of 22 November (section 7.5); and whether anyone outside these sessions has examined the manuscript of the earlier proof (section 5.1).

**Where the coordinates lead.** The two archives hold the folder `knowledge/` of each instance at the last commit considered here. That of the later instance is published with this document; that of the earlier instance, at 349 MB, is not in the repository and can at present be obtained only on the machine on which it was made, as the head of the notes says. In them a sequence number, a record with its claims and its review, and the final state of the frontier can be opened. The other sources that the account cites are published in the folder `sources/` beside this document, as far as they are files: the standing inputs, the runner's state and logs, some of the cited session files, the structure document with its reports, the paper and the documents around it, the operator's copies, and the extract of the system log. For the Git histories of the two instances the folder holds a list of their commits with times. The manuscript and the memory notes are tracked files of the repository; the files of the manuscript are tracked although their directory is otherwise ignored.[^398] Not published are the private session transcript of 19 November, the transcripts of the instances' sessions and some other files of their sessions, the Git repositories of the instances themselves (and so the earlier states of the frontier), some working files of the days between the instances, and the older runs of October. `SOURCES.md` gives the place of each kind of source and the list of what is not published. In the published copies the name of the Windows account of the machine has been replaced by `<account>`; nothing else in them was changed.

## Notes

The notes give, for the statements they are attached to, the coordinates of the sources. The coordinate of a statement in the running text stands in a note here, and the numbered mark after the statement leads to the note. Beside the coordinate a note says, where there is need of it, which record states a step of an argument, what name or letter a record itself has for something, and how this account came to the statement. The coordinates use the words of section 2.2 for the research system. In the tables the coordinates stand in the cells, and a few sentences of section 9 name a source itself, by its sequence number or by the number of a memory note. The coordinates are of the following kinds. In them "E" stands for the earlier instance and "L" for the later one; their directories are named in section 2.3.

- "E seq 133" is the 133rd publication of the earlier instance. The folder `knowledge/events/` of each instance holds one file for each publication, with its sequence number and time, and each publication is one commit (with one exception: in the earlier instance the set of questions of 9 November, E seq 188, was committed together with the next record).
- A token such as `rce3dc75` is the first eight or nine characters of a record's identifier, which is also the beginning of the name of its folder under `knowledge/records/`; these characters identify the folder uniquely within its instance.
- A seven- or nine-character hexadecimal token after the word "commit" is a commit of the instance's own Git repository.
- "Input" followed by a date and time is a standing input, a file under `control/inputs/` whose name begins with its submission time in nanoseconds.
- "M-0731" is a note in the working memory of the repository in which the research system itself is developed, the file `기억/layer1/M-0731.md`. These notes were written by the sessions that acted as the operator at the time, one with each commit of that repository, and they are used here as a secondary source for what the operator and the user did.
- Other files are given by path. Paths in this account are relative to the root of the repository, with two exceptions: a path that begins with `sources/` is relative to the folder of this document, and a path that begins with `knowledge/`, `control/`, `runner/` or `sessions/` is relative to the directory of the instance concerned. The repository meant here, and in what follows on where the sources are published, is the one in which this document lies, in the folder `solved/parking-unimodality/history/`; the memory notes are tracked files of it.

The sources that this account cites are published with it, except those that `SOURCES.md` names as not published and the archive of the earlier instance, which at 349 MB is left out of the repository. That archive can at present be obtained nowhere but on the machine on which it was made. The file `SOURCES.md`, in the folder of this document, says for each kind of coordinate where the source is to be found: in one of the two archives of the instances' published knowledge (section 2.3), in the folder `sources/` beside this document, or in a tracked file of the repository. It also lists the sources that are cited and not published, with the reason for each.

[^158]: E seq 386, `r6fc60e0d`. The first two of these three records were read for this account in the summaries of their claims and reviews only, and of the third the opening sections of the exposition were read as well; how the last two connect onward to the second-layer comparison was not traced.
[^159]: E seq 386, `r6fc60e0d`, §0.1 and §1.1. The record writes $d$ for the deficit, which this account writes $r$. It writes $s_c(d)$ for the probability that a uniformly random parking function of length $c + d - 1$ has $M \le c - 1$, $D_d(k)$ for the second difference of $c \cdot s_c(d)$ at $c = k$, and $\mathrm{src}(d)$ for the source term. What the last of the three records bounds from below is, in full, this. The quantity behind it is the level times the probability that $M$ stays below the level, taken along the diagonal of deficit $r$, on which the level and the length grow together. The record takes the second difference of this quantity at level $k$, and the source term is what is left of that difference when a positive combination of the same differences at smaller deficits has been subtracted. The recurrence of which it is the source term is the renewal identity that §1.1 of the record cites, $D_d = \sum_{a \lt d} X_k(d,a) \cdot D_a + \mathrm{src}(d)$, in which the coefficients $X_k(d,a)$ are not negative: it gives the second difference at one deficit from those at the smaller deficits and the source term.

[^168]: E seq 431, `r5b203f9f`. The two statements on the model are the claims `c-ism-unimodal` (the record's Theorem 2′) and `c-ism-lc` (its Theorem 3.5), both marked as proof with interval arithmetic and both supported by the review. The inequality of the text is, in the words of §0 of the exposition, that the relative decrease of an atom of the model over one level is smaller than the hazard rate, and by §0 the properties of the model come together from practically this one inequality. The analytic part of the proof holds for $N = n + 1 \ge 300$; the rows $2 \le n \le 400$ for unimodality and $3 \le n \le 400$ for log-concavity were decided with exact rationals and interval arithmetic by two independent codes of the peers, and the review decided them again with its own code. Where the properties of the true row fail is the claim `c-shape-map`, which the review supported with two corrections, given in its verdict on the claim and in its assessment of the index. The record has the failures in the rows up to 500 between $n/2$ and $0.70 \cdot n$; by the review the failures of the log-concavity of the row begin at a level of 0.4911 of the row (row 112, level 55), and the range is from $0.49 \cdot n$ to $0.70 \cdot n$. And the record's first failures of the two monotonicity properties, at $n = 494$ and at $n = 499$, are to be read as the start of the large failures, apart from exceptions in small rows, of the one in the rows 4 to 7 and of the other in the rows 4 to 10, some of them after the first descent of the row; by the review's exact computation of the rows 11 to 500, every failure from row 11 on lies before the first descent.

[^394]: E `runner/state.json`. The time 09:58:59, which the text gives further on in the same paragraph, is the log's, and the launch at 10:08:07 is from the runner's state file. In the state file the launches are the times `started_at` of the sessions `sf3f36cf7…` (09:59:35) and `s42b21d67…` (10:08:07). The last lines are those of the transcripts of the twelve sessions that were running, the files `sessions/<id>/transcript-….jsonl` and `sessions/<id>/cli.stdout.jsonl` of the earlier instance, of which only the last entries were opened, for their time stamps and their kind. In nine of the twelve sessions the last entry carries a time from 10:08:34.040 to 10:08:34.589 (the files give it in UTC, as 01:08:34), and in five of the nine it is a notice that the connection was dropped or that no route was to be had; the other three sessions had last written at 10:00:05, 10:07:48 and 10:07:55. The time of the start of the system is that of the log's entry on the start of the operating system, which the extract gives to the second. The times before the stop are those of the machine's clock as the sessions wrote them, and the time of the start is that of its clock after the restart. By this account's reading the two cannot be compared to the second: a restart takes longer than the second that they leave, and on 6 and 13 November the same log has 121 seconds and 80 seconds between the shutting down of the operating system and its start. The log gives 09:58:59 as the time of the previous shutdown; the launches and the last lines show the machine at work for nine and a half minutes after it.

[^432]: E seq 30, `r99471147`, of 6 November, 03:56, exposition §2: reading a left child of the binary tree as a first child and a right child as the next sibling gives a bijection between the outcomes and the rooted forests in which every vertex has a larger label than its descendants; in this forest the subtree of spot $i$ is the vertex of the binary tree with its left subtree, and it has $h_i$ vertices. The claim `joint-leaf-transfer` rests on this, and its review confirms the size $h_i$. The record says in its §2 that it does not take the coordinates by car and the coordinates by label to be the same; E seq 27, `r6a8c8b6e`, of 6 November, 02:58, had proved the like for the displacements alone, with the same reservation (claim `multiset-transfer`). E seq 110, `rde86d3df`, of 6 November, 23:43, in its file `bulk-first-vacancy-proof.md`, §1, Lemma A, a result of the peer that the review went through and on which the claims `bulk-cumulative-theorem` and `block-bad-recursion` rest: take the root of the binary tree and, in turn, its right children, each with its left subtree; each of these sets is a stretch of consecutive spots, and the capacity of its last spot is its length; a parking function corresponds one-to-one to a division of the cars into such stretches together with, for each stretch, the displacement of its latest car, any of the values 0 to the length less one, and a parking function on its other cars, the stretches standing in the decreasing order of their latest cars; so a stretch of $t$ spots has $t \cdot p_{t-1} = t^{t-1}$ structures, and the lengths are distributed as the sizes of the trees of a uniform rooted forest. The exposition of the record, §6.2, says that this rewrites in the language of the outcome the recurrence along the chain of E seq 13. E seq 117, `ra6587477`, of 7 November, 04:25, exposition §2.3, a result of the peer (claim `decreasing-forest-canonical`): the parent of spot $i$ is the first spot on its right that holds a later car; the subtree of spot $i$ is the stretch that the record writes $(t_i, i]$, so that its size is $h_i$; the roots are the spots of the maxima of the outcome read from right to left, and the components are the stretches between two consecutive such maxima; the construction is a bijection between the outcomes and the forests on $n$ vertices in which the labels decrease from a parent to its children, which the record calls decreasing forests; and a quantity that depends only on the shape of the forest has the same weighted sum over the outcomes as over the forests of the model. The review checked for every outcome with $n \le 7$ that the size of the subtree is $h_i$. By this account's reading, the forest of E seq 30 is the forest of E seq 117, because in both the subtree of spot $i$ consists of the same spots; E seq 117 does not cite E seq 30. E seq 117 itself ends the passage, the first paragraph of its §2.3, with the sentence that the decomposition of an outcome at its largest label, into the part before that label and the part after it, is the same as the recursion that hangs the forest of the first part under the root and sets the forest of the second part beside it. That is the recursion by which E seq 30 builds its forest from the binary tree, a left child being read as a first child and a right child as the next sibling. These are the earliest statements that a search found. The search went through every `.md` file, `record.json` and `review/assessment.json` in the folders of E seq 1 to 120 for the records' words for a decreasing forest, a Cartesian tree, a first child and a next sibling, the first larger value on the right, the maxima from right to left and the spine, and through every paragraph of E seq 1 to 29 that names a forest or a tree together with the outcome or a capacity. A statement in other words is not excluded by it.
